The Tensionless Lives of Null Strings
Arjun Bagchi, Aritra Banerjee, Ritankar Chatterjee, and Priyadarshini Pandit

Abstract
The tensionless limit probes the very high energy regime of string theory in contrast to the well studied point-particle limit which reduces to Einstein gravity. Tensionless strings sweep out null worldsheets in the target space and hence are also called null strings. This article aims to provide a comprehensive review of tensionless null string theory beginning with the initial work of Schild, and continuing to the foundational work of Isberg et al (ILST) and then focussing on developments in the past decade. Recent work centres on the emergence of the Carrollian Conformal Algebra as residual worldsheet symmetries of the ILST action and the identification of tensionless limit as a worldsheet Carrollian limit on the string worldsheet. Carrollian structures are used to address the classical and quantum aspects of the null string. In the classical theory, the aforementioned limit agrees with the analysis from the ILST action. Symmetries, constraints, mode expansions computed from both perspectives match nicely providing a robust cross-check of the analyses. We discuss closed and open null strings as well as their supersymmetric cousins. The quantum null string comes with several surprises, the foremost of which is the emergence of three consistent quantum theories from the ILST action. We detail the canonical quantisation and the spectrum of the triumvirate of theories. We discuss the novelties of the quantum null theories and the effect compactifaction has on them. We also discuss Carroll strings, applications of these ideas to strings approaching black holes and give a quick overview of other related developments. Invited Review for Physics Reports.



1
Introduction

Unifying classical gravity and quantum mechanics to form a unified framework of a quantum theory of gravity remains
the
outstanding problem in theoretical physics. Of the vying contenders of such a theory, string theory is one of the strongest contenders.

Of the four fundamental forces of nature, all except for gravity are amenable to methods of quantum field theory (QFT). Gravity is a fundamentally non-renormalizable theory and hence resists any attempts to shoehorn it into standard quantisation techniques of relativistic QFTs. One of the principle causes for the uncontrollable ultraviolet divergences in quantum gravity framed in the language of relativistic QFT is the point-particle like nature of interaction in QFTs.

String theory offers a very unconventional solution to the problem by positing that fundamental objects are not point-like but extended one-dimensional strings. String theory was historically introduced as a theory to understand strong interactions before the advent of quantum chromodynamics, but it was later understood that this theory with the postulates of special relativity and quantum mechanics as inputs, naturally and rather remarkably generates a quantum theory of gravity. In other words, the spectrum of string theory contains a massless spin-two excitation that can be identified as the graviton, the quanta of the gravitational field.

One of the most attractive features of string theory is the paucity of free parameters in the theory. The spectacularly successful Standard Model of Particle Physics, which explains nature around us to a very high accuracy, in its most minimal form, has 19 free parameters. von Neumann famously said, “With four parameters I can fit an elephant, with five I can make it wiggle its trunk.” Theorists always prefer to have as few free parameters in their theories as possible. String theory is particularly nice in this regard, - it contains two free parameters, the length of the fundamental string
\ell_{s}
and the string coupling
g_{s}
.
Free string theory is thus dictated by just
\ell_{s}
or equivalently
\alpha^{\prime}=\ell_{s}^{2}=\frac{1}{2\pi T}
, where
T
is the tension of the string.

Bosonic free strings are described by the Polyakov action
148
. For strings propagating in a flat target spacetime, this is given by:

\displaystyle S_{P}=-\frac{T}{2}\int d^{2}\xi\sqrt{-\gamma}\gamma^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}X^{\mu}\partial_{\beta}X^{\nu}\eta_{\mu\nu}.

(1)

Here
\xi^{\alpha}=\sigma,\tau
label the coordinates of the two dimensional worldsheet swept out by the string.
X^{\mu}
are spacetime coordinates which also act as pullback scalar fields on the worldsheet.
\gamma^{\alpha\beta}
is the worldsheet metric and
\eta_{\mu\nu}
is the flat metric on the background spacetime. This theory has worldsheet diffeomorphism invariance which act as gauge symmetries that necessitates choosing a gauge on the worldsheet. One can choose the worldsheet metric to be flat and after accommodating for Weyl symmetries on the worldsheet, there is still a residual gauge symmetry which turns out to be two copies of the Virasoro algebra:

\displaystyle[{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{\pm},{\mathcal{L}}_{m}^{\pm}]=(n-m){\mathcal{L}}^{\pm}_{n+m}+\frac{c^{\pm}}{12}\delta_{n+m,0}(n^{3}-n),\quad[{\mathcal{L}}_{n}^{\pm},{\mathcal{L}}_{m}^{\mp}]=0.

(2)

This thus signals the appearance of a two dimensional (2d) relativistic conformal field theory (CFT) on the worldsheet of the relativistic tensile string theory. The power of 2d CFTs is central to the understanding of quantum mechanics of the string. In the framework of Old Covariant quantisation (OCQ), one treats the string as a bunch of free worldsheet scalars and imposes the constraints in terms of the Virasoro algebra on the Hilbert space. The spectrum of the theory, so calculated, gives rise to a tachyonic vacuum state and a massless first excited level of states which contain a spin-two excitation that is identified as the graviton, and an infinite number of states at higher levels.

Given that free string theory has only one free parameter, there are a priori three different regions that we could tune this parameter to get different theories, viz.

\displaystyle T=\text{finite}\,(\equiv T=1),\quad T\to\infty,\quad T\to 0.

(3)

The first choice is just the usual relativistic tensile string that we discussed above and is given by the Polyakov action (
1
). The second one,
where the string tension goes to infinity, is the well studied point-particle limit of string theory. Here the fundamental string reduces to a point particle giving rise to (super)gravity from string theory. The final one is the tensionless limit
152
;
120
and would be the focus of this review.

The regimes described above in (
3
) is similar in spirit to what one could envision doing with the speed of light (
c
) in a relativistic QFT, i.e.

Relativistic QFT:

\displaystyle\quad c=\text{finite}(\,\equiv c=1)

(4a)

Galilean QFT:

\displaystyle\quad c\to\infty,

(4b)

Carrollian QFT:

\displaystyle\quad c\to 0.

(4c)

The first is of course the statement that the absolute value of the speed of light does not matter and for convenience this can be set to one in natural units in a relativistic theory. The
c\to\infty
limit is the familiar non-relativistic limit where Lorentz transformations reduce to their Galilean counterparts, and time becomes absolute. The final one is the unconventional Carroll limit, where instead of time, space becomes absolute. Here lightcones close up and usual motion comes to a standstill. Carrollian symmetries, born of curiosity in the 1960’s
131
;
153
, is now finding wide ranging applications starting from condensed matter physics to quantum gravity
11
.

For the string, the similarity between (
3
) and (
4
) is far from a coincidence. The free string, as described above, has only one free parameter and tension can thus be viewed in terms of a worldsheet speed of light.

In this review, we will be interested in the singular limit of string theory where the tension of the string goes to zero. As we will see, mirroring what happens in the QFT case, Carrollian symmetries would arise, now on the worldsheet. The analogy with point particles here is very apt. Massless point particles travel along null geodesics. The tensionless limit is the string analogue of the massless point particle. Here the string worldsheet becomes null
152
. Carrollian symmetries naturally arise on null surfaces
86
;
87
and hence also in this case. We now give a brief outline of this primary feature of the tensionless string. The action of these strings propagating, on a flat background geometry, following ILST
120
is given by

\displaystyle S_{\text{ILST}}=\int d^{2}\xi\,V^{\alpha}V^{\beta}\partial_{\alpha}X^{\mu}\partial_{\beta}X^{\nu}\eta_{\mu\nu},

(5)

where
V^{\alpha}V^{\beta}
replaces the degenerate
\sqrt{-\gamma}\gamma^{\alpha\beta}
in the limit
T\to 0
. Worldsheet diffeomorphisms still exist in the limit, but an analogous covariant gauge fixing leads to a different residual symmetry algebra:

\displaystyle[L_{n},L_{m}]=(n-m)L_{n+m}+\frac{c_{L}}{12}\delta_{n+m,0}(n^{3}-n),

(6a)

\displaystyle[L_{n},M_{m}]=(n-m)M_{n+m}+\frac{c_{M}}{12}\delta_{n+m,0}(n^{3}-n),

(6b)

\displaystyle[M_{n},M_{m}]=0.

(6c)

This was recognised as the Conformal Carroll algebra in two dimensions (2d) or equivalently the 3d Bondi-van der Burgh-Metzner-Sachs algebra
1
1

1

or interestingly the 2d Galilean Conformal Algebra, which is isomorphic to the Carrollian Conformal Algebra in these dimensions
30
.
in
86
.

The same algebra has made its appearance as the asymptotic symmetry algebra in 3d asymptotically flat spacetimes
39
and has been proposed as the symmetry dictating the 2d holographically dual field theory
30
;
24
. The programme is called the Carrollian approach to flatspace holography and has gained much recent traction with generalisations to the 4d – 3d case following
17
;
80
. We point the reader to the recent review
11
for more details on this.

For most applications, including the above mentioned best studied case of flatspace holography, Carrollian (conformal) symmetries arise as spacetime symmetries. In contrast, on the worldsheet of the tensionless string, these symmetries are residual gauge symmetries. We will see that the rather intriguing quantum aspects of the null tensionless string arise from various curious properties of the Carroll (conformal) symmetries and the algebra (
6
) and its supersymmetric generalizations help us organise the quantum null (super)string.

This review aims to provide the reader an in-depth analysis of the tensionless null string
2
2

2

We should mention that there has been a lot of recent remarkable progress in the context of tensionless strings in AdS spacetime, specifically superstring in AdS
{}_{3}\times
S
{}^{3}\times
T
4

88
;
89
. This has been used to provide a derivation of AdS
3
/CFT
2

89
. These tensionless strings are
not
null strings. This is because in AdS, unlike flat spacetime, there is another scale, the AdS radius, with respect to which the tensionless limit can be taken without making the string null. We will not be considering these tensionless strings in our review.
. Our review is divided into four parts. Part
I
of the review provides some historical background, starting with the initial construction of the null string by Schild
152
. We also collect some basics of string theory we would need for constructing null strings and review aspects of Carrollian symmetries, especially Carrollian conformal field theory in
D=2
, which will be essential in what follows.

Part
II
and
III
form the main material of our review. Part
II
focusses on the formulation of the classical null tensionless string. Much of the recent resurgence in the field follows the seminal work by Isberg et al
120
. The action of the null string that we would use in the main body of the paper is the ILST action (
5
). The initiation of the recent body of work was the identification of (
6
) as the BMS
3
algebra in
32
. This also led to understanding of the tensionless limit as an ultra-relativistic or a Carroll contraction on the worldsheet. The understanding was made more concrete in
20
where the first hints of the very rich quantum structures were also uncovered. We discuss this and the related curiosities in the quantum null string in Part
III
.

In all our discussions in the first three parts focussed on Carrollian symmetries arising on the string worldsheet. Finally in Part
IV
, Carrollian symmetries appear on the spacetime and we cover related developments of strings moving in Carrollian and generalities of Carrollian spacetimes.

To facilitate reading of the review, at the start of each part, we briefly summarise the different sections contained in the part and link them so that the reader can easily navigate through the pages.

Part I
Beginnings: History and Essentials

This part of our review start with a recollection of early work on the theory of null strings beginning with the pioneering study of Schild. We will then devote a couple of sections to some basic but essential material on tensile string theory and Carrollian symmetries.

Outline of Part I

Part I of this review contains the following:

•

Null Strings: A historical interlude
: Here we take the historic route and describe Schild’s seminal work
152
in some detail. We also mention other important advances in the field.

•

String Theory: Collecting ingredients to go null
: We will require some specific constructions of string theory, especially in the Hamiltonian framework, which may be somewhat unfamiliar. We discuss these aspects in a section.

•

A Summary of Carrollian Symmetry
: Conformal Carrollian symmetries arise on the worldsheet of the tensionless string and helps organise the theory of null strings. In this section we collect the central results of two dimensional Carrollian CFTs which we will use heavily in the later parts of the review.

This part of the review is meant to provide necessary background for the uninitiated reader. For the experts, only the historical interlude may be the interesting read in this section.

2
Null Strings: A Historical Interlude

Our treatment of null strings in the main body of the review would be based on a modern symmetry based approach and would follow from the important work of Isberg et al
120
from the early 1990s and the identification of the worldsheet symmetries with the 2d Conformal Carroll (or BMS
3
) algebra
32
in 2013 and subsequent developments in the last decade. But before this, it is important to make a historical detour and this section is an acknowledgement to the important developments preceding
120
.

We begin at the very beginning. The first formulation of strings with null worldsheets can be traced back to Schild’s seminal work
152
in the 1970’s. We now build a bit of background and delve into Schild’s work in some detail.

2.1
Massless point particles

Let us consider a free relativistic point particle of mass
m
propagating in a
D
dimensional Minkowski spacetime (light speed
c
is taken to be unity). The action is the proper time between the initial and final locations of the particle in the spacetime along the worldline of the particle:

\displaystyle S=-m\int_{A}^{B}\sqrt{-ds^{2}}=-m\int_{\tau_{A}}^{\tau_{B}}d\tau\sqrt{-\dot{X}^{2}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ ds^{2}=\eta_{\mu\nu}dX^{\mu}dX^{\nu},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \dot{X}=\frac{dX}{d\tau}.

(7)

Here
\tau
is affine parameter defined on the worldline,
X^{\mu}(\tau)
is the position of the particle in spacetime. This action is invariant under the reparametrization
\tau=\tau^{\prime}=f(\tau)
.
It is clear however that the action for massless point particles cannot be arrived at by naively taking
m\to 0
on (
7
).

There is an alternate way to understand the problem without taking massless limit which was highlighted in
152
. The equation of motion (EOM) of the action (
7
) gives

\displaystyle m\frac{d}{d\tau}\Bigg(\frac{\dot{X}}{\sqrt{-\dot{X}^{2}}}\Bigg)=0.

(8)

Under appropriate reparametrization, EOM simplifies to

\displaystyle\sqrt{-\dot{X}^{2}}=c\quad\implies m\frac{d}{d\tau^{\prime}}\Bigg(\frac{\dot{X}}{\sqrt{-\dot{X}^{2}}}\Bigg)=\frac{m}{c}\ddot{X}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \text{where}\penalty\ \dot{X}=\frac{dX}{d\tau^{\prime}}.

(9)

Here
c
is a constant. The left hand side (LHS) of (
8
) or (
9
) makes sense only
\dot{X}^{2}\neq 0
. Clearly, this EOM isn’t applicable for massless particles which move along the null direction (where
\dot{X}^{2}=0
).

We now detail the way around this problem. In
159
, Stückelberg proposed a new action for the point particle which can describe particles traversing in the null direction too. The action is

\displaystyle S=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{A}^{B}d\tau\penalty\ \dot{X}^{2}.

(10)

The EOM for this action is

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{d\tau}\frac{\partial(\dot{X}^{2})}{\partial\dot{X}}=\ddot{X}=0.

(11)

So the EOM for this action is equivalent to (
9
) (provided
c\neq 0
in (
9
)) indicating that the action (
10
) is equivalent to (
7
) for particles traversing in timelike and spacelike geodesics. However, (
11
) implies
\dot{X}^{2}
is arbitrary constant in
\tau
, which can take positive, zero, or negative value. Hence (
10
) action allows all possible geodesics, namely timelike, spacelike and null geodesics. Based on this observation Schild argued that the action (
10
) is superior to the action (
7
) to describe relativistic point particles.

However, there are some important drawbacks of this action. The applicability of this new action (
10
) comes at a cost. As later pointed out in
125
, the action (
10
), unlike (
7
), is not invariant under
\tau
reparametrization. Since (
10
) describes particles traversing all possible geodesics, there is no way of knowing beforehand whether the particle it describes is massless or massive. These issues can be taken care of by using the
\tau
reparametrization invariant “einbein” action discussed later in section
3.1
.

2.2
String tracing a null worldsheet

We now consider a string propagating through a
D
dimensional flat spacetime. The worldsheet of the string is parametrized by
\tau
and
\sigma
. The Nambu-Goto action
141
;
103
of the usual string theory is the invariant area of the worldsheet:

\displaystyle S_{NG}=T\int d^{2}\sigma\sqrt{-\det{\gamma_{\alpha\beta}}},

(12)

where
\sigma^{\alpha}=\{\tau,\sigma\}
are coordinates on worldsheet (i.e.
\alpha=0,1
) and
\gamma_{\alpha\beta}
is the induced metric on the worldsheet given by

\displaystyle\gamma_{\alpha\beta}=\partial_{\alpha}X^{\mu}\partial_{\beta}X^{\nu}\eta_{\mu\nu}.

(13)

Here
X^{\mu}=X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma),\penalty\ \penalty\ \mu=0,1,\cdots,D-1
are the coordinates of the
D
dimensional target spacetime and
\eta_{\mu\nu}
is the target spacetime (Minkowski) metric. Taking
T\to 0
limit on this action naively would make it vanish.

In
152
, an important drawback of the Nambu-Goto action was noted. The EOM for Nambu-Goto action (
12
) is given by

\displaystyle\dot{\Pi}_{\mu}+K^{\prime}_{\mu}=0,

(14)

where
\Pi_{\mu}
(the canonical momentum) and
K_{\mu}
are given by

\Pi_{\mu}=\frac{\partial{\mathcal{L}}}{\partial{\dot{X}^{\mu}}}=T\frac{(\dot{X}\cdot X^{\prime})X^{\prime}_{\mu}-X^{\prime 2}\dot{X}_{\mu}}{\sqrt{(\dot{X}\cdot X^{\prime})^{2}-\dot{X}^{2}X^{\prime 2}}},

(15a)

K_{\mu}=\frac{\partial{\mathcal{L}}}{\partial{X^{\prime\mu}}}=T\frac{(\dot{X}\cdot X^{\prime})\dot{X}_{\mu}-\dot{X}^{2}X^{\prime}_{\mu}}{\sqrt{(\dot{X}\cdot X^{\prime})^{2}-\dot{X}^{2}X^{\prime 2}}}.

(15b)

In the above equations,
X^{\prime}=\partial_{\sigma}X
and
\dot{X}=\partial_{\tau}X
. (
14
) and (
15
), imply that (
14
) is valid only if

\displaystyle\sqrt{(\dot{X}\cdot X^{\prime})^{2}-\dot{X}^{2}X^{\prime 2}}\neq 0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \implies\penalty\ \penalty\ -\det{\gamma_{\alpha\beta}}\neq 0.

(16)

This means that the worldsheet area traced by string must be non-zero. However, since Minkowski spacetime allows null submanifolds, there should be special case where string worldsheet is null. Nevertheless, (
14
) and (
16
) clearly shows that Nambu-Goto action cannot describe such strings. As we shall see in the later part, null worldsheet is directly connected to tensionless strings in the same way null geodesics are connected to massless particles.

2.3
Schild Strings

In
152
, Schild generalised Stückelberg’s analysis to strings. The action proposed was string analogue of (
10
):

\displaystyle S=\frac{1}{4}\int\penalty\ d^{2}\sigma\penalty\ \Gamma^{\mu\nu}\Gamma_{\mu\nu},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \Gamma^{\mu\nu}=\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}X^{\mu}\partial_{\beta}X^{\nu}=\dot{X}^{\mu}X^{\prime\nu}-X^{\prime\mu}\dot{X}^{\nu}.

(17)

For future use, we also note the following

\displaystyle\Gamma^{2}=\dot{X}^{2}X^{\prime 2}-(\dot{X}\cdot X^{\prime})^{2}=\det{\gamma_{\alpha\beta}}.

(18)

EOM for this action are

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\alpha}\Big(\frac{\partial\penalty\ \Gamma^{2}}{\partial(\partial_{\alpha}X^{\mu})}\Big)=\partial_{\alpha}(\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\Gamma_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\beta}X^{\nu})=0

(19)

Using
\partial_{\beta}X^{\mu}\frac{\partial\penalty\ \Gamma^{2}}{\partial_{\alpha}X^{\mu}}=\delta^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ \beta}\Gamma^{2}
, the EOM reduces to

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}\Gamma^{2}=0.

(20)

Hence EOM for (
17
) implies
\Gamma^{2}
is arbitrary constant (which can be zero as well) in the worldsheet coordinates. Since EOM allows
\Gamma^{2}=0
as a possibility, this action allows null strings as a solution.

But again, (
17
) does not specify the nature of the string worldsheet and suffers from the lack of reparametrization invariance, similar to the Stückelberg action
3
3

3

The easiest way to see this is is to use (
18
) to rewrite the reparametrization invariant Nambu Goto action (
12
) as

\displaystyle S_{NG}=T\int d^{2}\sigma\sqrt{-\Gamma^{2}}.

(21)

In the light of the fact that this action is invariant area element, squaring the integrant in (
17
) would mean integrating
\Gamma^{2}=-\det{\gamma_{\alpha\beta}}
throughout the worldsheet which won’t allow the action to be invariant any more.
. A better way to construct a reparametrization invariant action will be to use Hamiltonian formalism
120
(see also
133
) outlined in the upcoming sections. We shall see that, on the action formulated in this way for tensile strings we can take a sensible
T\to 0
limit.

However, Schild in his work provided some important insights about the geometric nature of the worldsheet of null strings which will continue to hold even for the new reparameterization invariant action for null strings.

2.4
Geometry of the Null Worldsheet

Following Schild, we now discuss the geometric nature of the null string worldsheet. We first look into the tangent surface to the worldsheet.

The null worldsheet has
\Gamma^{2}=0
and obeys EOM (
19
). For a string with
\Gamma^{\mu\nu}
given in (
17
) and
\Gamma^{2}=0
,
\Gamma^{\mu\nu}
can be expressed as

\Gamma^{\mu\nu}=A^{[\mu}B^{\nu]},\qquad\qquad A^{2}>0,\penalty\ B^{2}=0\penalty\ \penalty\ \text{and}\penalty\ \penalty\ A\cdot B=0,

(22)

where
A^{\mu}
and
B^{\mu}
are tangent vectors to the null worldsheet. The tangent 2d surface to the worldsheet is spanned by
A^{\mu}
and
B^{\mu}
and hence a generic vector in the tangent space is given by

V=c_{1}A+c_{2}B

(23)

The above equation (
23
) along with
B^{2}=0
in (
22
) implies
V^{2}=c_{1}^{2}A^{2}>0
, which in turn implies that for
c_{1}^{2}\neq 0
(i.e.
V
doesn’t coincide with
B
),
V
is spacelike. Also, the fact that
A\cdot B=0
from (
22
) and (
23
) together imply that
V
is orthogonal to
B
. The takeaway from here is that whenever we take two independent vectors in a tangent space of a null worldsheet, at least one of them must be spacelike. Before we proceed we also note the following identities which will be useful later

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mu\nu}V_{\nu}=c_{1}A^{2}B^{\mu},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \Gamma^{\mu\nu}B_{\nu}=0.

(24)

That means as long as
c_{1}\neq 0
, for any vector
V
in the tangent space,
\Gamma^{\mu\nu}V_{\nu}
will give us a non-zero null vector. However, for any null tangent vector (where
V
coincides with
B
) to the null worldsheet we have
\Gamma^{\mu\nu}B_{\nu}=0
.

Now let us consider a null worldsheet given by
X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)
. The derivatives
\partial_{\tau}X^{\mu}
(or
\dot{X}^{\mu}
) and
\partial_{\sigma}X^{\mu}
(or
X^{\prime\mu}
) are essentially two independent tangents to the worldsheet, and hence, according to the previous argument at least one of them has to be spacelike. Since in our notation
\sigma
is the spacelike worldsheet coordinate, we choose
X^{\prime\mu}
to be the spacelike vector, i.e.
X^{\prime\mu}X^{\prime}_{\mu}>0
. Let us also consider a one parameter family of null curves on this null worldsheet

\Phi(\tau,\sigma)=\textit{constant}\qquad\qquad\text{with}\qquad\Phi^{\prime}\neq 0.

(25)

From the perspective of the target spacetime these null curves will be expressed as
X^{\mu}=X^{\mu}(s)
where
s
is the affine parameter defined on a given null curve. Now we want to construct a null vector field tangential to the null curves
X^{\mu}=X^{\mu}(s)
. Since these null curves reside on the worldsheet, the vector field tangent to them is also a vector field tangent to the worldsheet. Since we know that
X^{\prime\mu}
is spacelike, using (
24
), we can define a null vector field tangential to the worldsheet in the following way

\displaystyle N_{\mu}=\frac{1}{\Phi^{\prime}}\Gamma_{\mu\nu}X^{\prime\nu}.

(26)

Figure 1:
Heuristic depiction of a closed string null worldsheet with one parameter family of null curves
\Phi(\tau,\sigma)=constant
with different values of constants. Any of these curves also can be expressed in target spacetime as
X^{\mu}=X^{\mu}(s)
(here the curve
\Phi=c_{n}
is expressed in this way). Null vector tangential to the curve
N^{\mu}
at a given point
s
on the curve
\Phi=c_{n}
(depicted as sky-blue dot in the figure) is shown by the bold red arrow.

We want this vector field to be tangential to the curve
X^{\mu}(s)
which means we must ensure that the vector field
N
coincides with
dX/ds
. After that we can choose the affine parameter on the null curve
X^{\mu}(s)
such that
dX/ds=N
. Hence we have the following two equations which need to be simultaneously satisfied by
N

N^{\mu}=\frac{dX^{\mu}}{ds}=\dot{X}^{\mu}\frac{d\tau}{ds}+X^{\prime\mu}\frac{d\sigma}{ds},

(27a)

\frac{dX^{\mu}}{ds}=N^{\mu}=\frac{1}{\Phi^{\prime}}\Gamma^{\mu\nu}X^{\prime}_{\nu}=\frac{1}{\Phi^{\prime}}\Big(\dot{X}^{\mu}X^{\prime 2}-X^{\prime\mu}(\dot{X}\cdot X^{\prime})\Big).

(27b)

Comparing coefficients of
\dot{X}
from both (
27a
) and (
27b
) one can see that

\displaystyle\frac{d\tau}{ds}=\frac{1}{\Phi^{\prime}}X^{\prime 2}\neq 0.

(28)

Now, since
N^{\nu}
is null tangent vector to the worldsheet, from (
24
) one can see

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mu\nu}N_{\nu}=\Gamma^{\mu\nu}\frac{dX_{\nu}}{ds}=0,\implies\Gamma^{\mu\nu}\dot{X}_{\nu}\frac{d\tau}{ds}+\Gamma^{\mu\nu}X^{\prime}_{\nu}\frac{d\sigma}{ds}=0,

(29)

Now, since the null curve has been defined on the worldsheet as
\Phi(\tau,\sigma)=\textit{constant}
, this leads to another identity

\displaystyle 0=\frac{d\Phi}{ds}=\dot{\Phi}\frac{d\tau}{ds}+\Phi^{\prime}\frac{d\sigma}{ds}.

(30)

Using (
28
), (
29
) and (
30
) one arrives at
\Gamma^{\mu\nu}\dot{X}_{\nu}=\dot{\phi}N^{\mu}
, and together with the definition in (
26
) this gives

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\alpha}X_{\nu}=N^{\mu}\partial_{\alpha}\Phi.

(31)

Now, let us look back into the EOM of the Schild action (
19
). The l.h.s. of that equation can be rewritten using (
31
) as

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}(\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\Gamma_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\beta}X^{\nu})=\partial_{\alpha}(\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\Phi N_{\mu})=\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\Phi\partial_{\alpha}N_{\mu}=\dot{\Phi}N^{\prime}_{\mu}-\Phi^{\prime}\dot{N}_{\mu}.

(32)

In the above we have used the fact that
\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}(\partial^{2}_{\alpha\beta}\Phi)N_{\mu}=0
due to antisymmetry of
\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}
. Now, applying (
30
) in the above we see that

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}(\epsilon^{\alpha\beta}\Gamma_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\beta}X^{\nu})=-\frac{\Phi^{\prime}}{\frac{d\tau}{ds}}N^{\prime}_{\mu}\frac{d\sigma}{ds}-\Phi^{\prime}\dot{N}_{\mu}=-\frac{\Phi^{\prime}}{\frac{d\tau}{ds}}\Bigg(N^{\prime}_{\mu}\frac{d\sigma}{ds}+\dot{N}_{\mu}\frac{d\tau}{ds}\Bigg)=-\frac{\Phi^{\prime}}{\frac{d\tau}{ds}}\frac{dN_{\mu}}{ds}.

(33)

Since
\Phi^{\prime}
and
d\tau/ds
are non-zero finite numbers, the EOM (
19
) reduces to
\frac{dN^{\mu}}{ds}=0
, which, in turn, gives the following

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}X^{\mu}}{ds^{2}}=0.

(34)

The above equation (
34
) is the EOM of null geodesic in the flat spacetime where
s
is the affine parameter. One can also choose the affine parameter to be the worldsheet coordinate
\tau
and (
34
) becomes
\ddot{X}=0
. Hence one can say that the worldsheet of string governed by EOM (
19
) consists of null geodesics. We shall later see that this property appears as EOM for null strings irrespective of the action for the strings.

Schild also discovered another property inherent to the null worldsheet which would appear as constraints for null strings irrespective of the actions proposed in future studies. We already know from previous analysis that the tangent space to the null worldsheet at any point can always be spanned by a spacelike vector and a null vector which are normal to each other. Since on each point on the worldsheet there are spacelike tangent vectors it is always possible to consider a spacelike curve (i.e. tangent to the curve is always spacelike) on a null worldsheet. Let us consider a spacelike curve on the null worldsheet parametrized by
\sigma
(i.e.
X^{\mu}=X^{\mu}(\sigma)
). Since the tangent space to the null worldsheet also essentially contain a null direction orthogonal to the spatial direction, whenever we define such spacelike curve on a null worldsheet, we can also define a null vector field
N^{\mu}(\sigma)
orthogonal to the curve at any point
\sigma
, i.e.

\displaystyle N^{2}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ N_{\mu}\frac{dX^{\mu}}{d\sigma}=N_{\mu}X^{\prime\mu}=0.

(35)

Tangent to the curve at
\sigma
(i.e.
\frac{dX^{\mu}}{d\sigma}
) itself is the spacelike vector. Hence at each point
\sigma
there must be a null vector
N^{\mu}(\sigma)
which belongs to the tangent space and also orthogonal to
\frac{dX^{\mu}}{d\sigma}
. Since
N^{\mu}(\sigma)
belongs to the tangent space to the null worldsheet, one can define null curves along the worldsheet with
N^{\mu}
as tangent. From the previous analysis we know that these curves are null geodesics defined on the worldsheet. If the affine parameter on the worldsheet is chosen to be
\tau
, then we have

\displaystyle N^{\mu}=\frac{\partial X^{\mu}}{\partial\tau}=\dot{X}^{\mu},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ X^{\mu}=X^{\mu}(\sigma,\tau).

(36)

They lead to following identities

\displaystyle\dot{X}^{2}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \dot{X}\cdot X^{\prime}=0.

(37)

As we will see in the later parts, these equations will appear as constraints in the study of any action valid for null strings.

2.5
Subsequent Developments

The action proposed by Schild is not reparametrization invariant and this is an important drawback. Looking at the history of string theory, we see that in 1970s and 1980s, various actions were being proposed as alternatives to the Nambu-Goto action. Schild’s action (
17
) itself was not meant to be an action just for null strings, but as a better alternative to the Nambu-Goto action for relativistic strings, which, unlike Nambu-Goto action, was applicable for null strings too
4
4

4

In fact, for a while, Schild action (
17
) was seriously considered to be the replacement of Nambu-Goto action and was used to construct quantum theory of relativistic strings
91
. Nambu himself offered a Hamilton-Jacobi formulation of Schild action in
142
. For further studies of relativistic string dynamics based on Schild action (
17
) one can see
137
;
126
;
90
. A supersymmetric version of Schild action was proposed by Ishibashi et al in their seminal work on IKKT matrix theory
122
. There they showed that the supersymmetric Schild action proposed by them is classical equivalent of the Green-Schwarz superstring action in its Nambu-Goto form. Then they used the Schild-type action to construct a matrix model for the Green-Schwarz superstring.
. The now well known Polyakov action, which was introduced just a few months before Schild’s work
54
;
79
, was yet to be accepted universally. It was only accepted in 1981 when Polyakov would demonstrate the usefulness of this action in quantisation of string theory
148
. We will see later that Polyakov action is the key to obtain an action of null string with reparametrization invariance. In this section however, we will review the important developments in the study of null strings in 1980s and 1990s which would help us understand how our understanding of null strings evolved.

Attempts to construct null string actions

In
125
, Karlhede and Lindstrom delved deeper into strings with null worldsheet. As pointed out before, they identified the problem of Stückelberg action and Schild action not being reparametrization invariant. They proposed a new reparametrization invariant action for tensile bosonic string mimicking the einbein action for massive point particles (discussed later in (
46
))

\displaystyle S_{KL}=\int d^{2}\sigma\penalty\ \phi\penalty\ (\Gamma^{2}-T^{2}\phi^{-2}).

(38)

Here,
S_{KL}
denotes Karlhede and Lindstrom action. In the above
\Gamma^{2}
is same as (
18
) and
T
is the string tension. They identified that null strings in flat target spacetime are identical to tensionless (
T\to 0
) strings much in the same way particles travelling in null directions are identical to massless (
m\to 0
) particles. The null string action, hence, becomes

\displaystyle S_{KLN}=\int d^{2}\sigma\penalty\ \phi\penalty\ \Gamma^{2},

(39)

where
S_{KLN}
represents Karlhede and Lindstrom action for null string. The EOM for this action gives
\Gamma^{2}=0
, ensuring that the worldsheet is null. Following the arguments presented in (
2.4
), the equations (
34
) and (
37
) will automatically follow. However, we shall see that in later works Lindstrom et al would study null strings using an even more improved formalism.

In
134
, Lizzi et al proposed a different formulation for null strings. In an earlier work,
33
, Balachandran et al proposed their own brand of action for both bosonic strings as well as superstrings. The bosonic action proposed by them is

\displaystyle S_{BLS}=\int d^{2}\sigma\penalty\ \Sigma_{\mu\nu}\Gamma^{\mu\nu},

(40)

where
\Gamma^{\mu\nu}
is same as (
17
). They showed that for appropriate choice of
\Sigma
one can obtain the EOM of both Nambu-Goto strings (
14
) as well as of Schild’s null strings ((
34
) and (
37
)). A Hamiltonian formalism for the action similar to (
40
) for null string case was performed by Zheltukhin in
162
.

Attempts to quantise bosonic null strings

The earliest attempt to quantise null strings can be found in
134
, which built on the formalism outlined in
33
and attempted to develop a quantum theory for bosonic null strings in the light-cone gauge. The authors used Weyl ordering instead of normal ordering as ordering of operators. This choice resulted into a surprising finding; the quantum theory was consistent in any target spacetime dimension i.e. there was no critical dimension! They also found that even after quantisation, null string’s spectrum remains continuous, a result which is difficult to interpret physically. Same conclusion was drawn in some subsequent studies such as
2
;
38
. However, in later studies
94
;
95
Gamboa et al performed quantisation of bosonic null strings using normal ordering of operators and found that the critical dimension of the quantum theory still remains
D=26
.
The apparent contradiction between the results in
134
and
94
was later reconciled in
95
, where it was shown that these choices of normal ordering correspond to two different definition of vacuum, and consequently, two different quantum theories. We will see later that the underlying quantum structure is even richer and leads to three different quantum theories starting out from a single classical theory
7
.

The vacuum corresponding to the formalism in
134
was later identified as induced vacuum
15
, which arises at the tensionless limit of usual tensile string vacuum
15
;
7
. Another important finding in
134
which would later play a crucial role in the study of null strings is that, for closed null strings the Fourier modes of the constraints (
37
) give rise to an algebra, which was later identified as BMS
3
algebra
5
5

5

However, the algebra found by Lizzi et al. did not have any central extension. The reason behind this was again the Weyl ordering they chose to work with. As already pointed out, this ordering correspond to Induced vacuum and it has been explicitly shown in
7
that for this vacuum the worldsheet algebra doesn’t have central extension. We shall review this in later part.
, the symmetry algebra of null infinity of 3d asymptotically flat spacetime
39
.

The vacuum proposed in
94
is now known as the flipped vacuum
7
. Quantum theory constructed on this vacuum has a truncated spectrum having only finite number of massless states. Later it was observed in
68
that this spectrum exactly coincides with the spectrum of ambitwistor string theory, which was originally proposed by Mason and Skinner in
139
to explain the CHY formulae for massless particles.

Supersymmetric null strings

The study of supersymmetric null strings began with a series of works
38
;
94
;
95
. The work
38
followed Green-Schwarz (GS) formalism to study null superstrings.
The works
94
;
95
followed Ramond–Neveu–Schwarz (RNS) formalism to study the same. The studies in
94
;
95
played important role in the subsequent understanding of null bosonic strings and superstrings. We list some of their key results:

•

In
95
, actions for bosonic null string were identified which would be the basis of subsequent studies of null strings. It was later found out that this action emerges after gauge fixing the reparametrization invariant action of bosonic null strings later constructed in
132
. The gauge fixed action for null superstring was also found here which was later identified as the homogeneous tensionless limit of Ramond-Neveu-Schwarz (RNS) superstring action
21
.

•

In
94
, the constraint algebra for the null superstring action was constructed. This algebra would be later identified as homogeneous version of Supersymmetric Conformal Carrollian Algebra. This algebra has applications beyond the study of null strings, e.g. it appears as the asymptotic symmetry algebra of 3d flatspace supergravity
40
.

•

As already highlighted, the studies
94
;
95
developed a quantum theory for null strings having critical dimension
D=26
. Here the null superstring theory was also quantised in light-cone gauge and the critical dimension was calculated be
D=10
. The spectrum obtained quantising the null superstrings contained finite number of massless states. This would later be connected to the Ambitwistor string
68
.

However, the actions Gamboa et al used in their studies of bosonic strings and superstrings were gauge fixed actions, and not ones which were reparametrization invariant. Subsequent works
132
;
133
filled this gap for both bosonic null strings and null superstrings.
133
followed the construction of reparametrization invariant Polyakov action for tensile string given in
54
and constructed the same for null string action by taking tensionless (
T\to 0
) limit on the Polyakov action. The reparametrization invariant action in
133
at suitable gauge choice leads us to the bosonic action used in
95
. Similarly for the action for null superstrings too, the reparametrization invariant action in
133
under appropriate gauge choice reduces to the null superstring action used in
95
.

ILST Formalism

In 1993, Isberg et al wrote a seminal paper on null strings which provided a complete and concrete classical formulation of bosonic null strings
120
. Following their earlier work
132
, they first constructed reparametrization invariant action for null string. As advertised earlier, this formulation would be the basis of the more recent advances of null tensionless string theory and will be central to this review. We will consider this at length in the main body of the review and in particular in Part
II
.

Other works

We now briefly mention other works which provided useful insights in the studies of null strings.
In
121
, Isberg et al studied spacetime conformal invariance for quantum null strings. From the tensionless limit on the Polyakov action as shown in
133
one can clearly see how tensionless limit is directly connected to the non-Riemannian structures on the string worldsheet. In
78
it was further shown that the sting tension is proportionate to the worldsheet speed of light
c
, which means null strings correspond to the
c\to 0
limit (which is now called to be Carrollian limit) on the worldsheet. The
77
also studied the quantum null stings in de Sitter background. Further,
135
showed how at tensionless limit the worldsheet residual gauge symmetry of tensile strings (Virasoro algebra) reduces to the same for tensionless strings (BMS
3
algebra). Although Schild’s formalism for null strings was later abandoned, a generalisation of Schild’s study in curved background can be found in
123
.

This concludes our recapitulation of the history of the development of the theory of null strings.

3
String Theory: Collecting ingredients to go null

Our review is about tensionless null strings and superstrings. Before we go into the specific details of the null string, it is important to pause and collect various things from the well-known tensile theory. This section can be easily skipped for experts, but for the ease readability of non-experts, this is an amalgamation of tensile string theoretic methods which we will adopt to the tensionless world later. In order to make this section of a manageable length and for better readability, we will focus solely on the closed bosonic theory and leave the details of open strings and superstrings to appendices. Appendix
A
briefly covers bosonic open strings and Appendix
B
summarises the RNS superstring.

3.1
Hamiltonian Formalism

We saw in previous section that the standard relativistic point particle action (
7
) fails to describe dynamics of massless (or lightlike) point particles. We encountered similar problem in the Nambu-Goto action (
12
) while studying strings with null worldsheet. While the Stückelberg action (
10
) and Schild action (
17
) solve this problem for massless particles and null strings respectively, they fail to be reparametrization invariant. However, in 1976 Brink et al provided an altogether different formalism for relativistic point particles
53
. By employing Hamiltonian formulation, they managed to construct a reparametrization invariant action where massless limit could be taken. In the same year,
54
6
6

6

However, they didn’t provide the details of the steps in their work which can be found in later works such as
133
;
120
.)
employed the same Hamiltonian formalism to formulate a new reparametrization invariant action for strings. The string action they constructed is now famous as Polyakov action
7
7

7

This action was independently proposed by Deser et al in
79
. However, they did not follow the Hamiltonian formulation to obtain the action.
. In this section we present the Hamiltonian formulation for both relativistic point particles and bosonic strings which will provide us with the necessary tools to construct the actions for massless point particles and null strings.

Relativistic point particles:

Looking at the relativistic point particle action (
7
), one immediately identifies two things: first, the action is designed in such a way that the canonical momentum of this action automatically satisfies the relativistic mass-shell constraint

\displaystyle P_{\mu}=\frac{\delta I}{\delta\dot{X}^{\mu}}=\frac{\eta_{\mu\nu}m\dot{X}^{\nu}}{\sqrt{-\dot{X}^{2}}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \implies\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ P^{2}+m^{2}=0.

(41)

Since this constraint is an immediate consequence of the definition of canonical momentum, this constraint is called primary constraint. The second thing to note is: the canonical Hamiltonian of this system vanishes

\displaystyle H_{c}=P_{\mu}\dot{X}^{\mu}-L=0,\qquad L=-m\sqrt{-\dot{X}^{2}}.

(42)

In order to perform a Hamiltonian formalism for this theory, Dirac proposed a new Hamiltonian which is a Lagrange’s multiplier times mass shell constraint (
41
)

\displaystyle H_{T}=\frac{1}{2}e\big(P^{2}+m^{2}\big),\qquad e=e(\tau).

(43)

More generally in order to deal with such systems with vanishing canonical Hamiltonian, we need to define a total Hamiltonian
(H_{T})
which is the primary constraints of the system times Lagrange multipliers. The new Lagrangian corresponding to the Hamiltonian in (
43
) in terms of
X
and
P
would be

\displaystyle L_{T}=P_{\mu}\dot{X}^{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}e\big(P^{2}+m^{2}\big).

(44)

Since in a space with coordinates
X
, a Lagrangian is expressed as
f(X,\dot{X})
, (
44
) cannot be a Lagrangian in the Minkowski spacetime where the particle is propagating. However, if one considers the phase space, where both position
X
and momentum
P
are space coordinates, (
44
) is allowed to be a Lagrangian
8
8

8

This kind of a Lagrangian should not be confused with a Routhian. Here
P_{\mu}
’s too, are treated as position coordinates in phase space, not momentum coordinates. This form of action has been used in the study of non-Lorentzian limit of relativistic point particles
46
;
42
.
. Hence we call (
44
) the Phase Space Lagrangian of the point particle and the corresponding action

\displaystyle I_{T}=\int d\tau\penalty\ \left\{P_{\mu}\dot{X}^{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}e\big(P^{2}+m^{2}\big)\right\},

(45)

the phase space action. Now, let us translate this into an action that is defined in the Minkowski spacetime. In order to do that, we first find the EOM for
P
in the phase space action. That is
P^{\mu}=e^{-1}\dot{X}^{\mu}
. Replacing
P
’s in the phase space action gives us the following action

\displaystyle I(X,e)=\frac{1}{2}\int d\tau\penalty\ (e^{-1}\dot{X}^{2}-em^{2}).

(46)

It is a simple task to verify that eliminating
e
from this action using EOM of
e
gives the original action (
7
).

Generalisation to strings:

We now generalise our construction to string theory. We begin with the Nambu Goto action:

\displaystyle S_{NG}=T\int d^{2}\sigma\sqrt{-\det{\gamma_{\alpha\beta}}}.

(47)

The EOM for the above action takes the following form

\dot{\Pi}^{\mu}+K^{\prime\mu}=0,

(48)

where
\Pi_{\mu}
(the canonical momentum) and
K_{\mu}
are given in (
15
). For strings propagating in Minkowski space, we see that
\Pi_{\mu}
satisfies two primary constraints

\Pi_{\mu}X^{\prime\mu}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \Pi^{2}+T^{2}X^{\prime 2}=0.

(49)

Just like point particles, we see that the canonical Hamiltonian for this action vanishes

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{c}=\Pi_{\mu}\dot{X}^{\mu}-\mathcal{L}=0,

(50)

implying worldsheet diffeomorphism invariance of the Nambu-Goto action. Here again, we use the primary constraints in (
49
) to construct a Hamiltonian

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{T}=\rho\penalty\ \Pi_{\mu}X^{\prime\mu}+\frac{\lambda}{2}\left(\Pi^{2}+T^{2}X^{\prime 2}\right),

(51)

where
\rho(\tau,\sigma)
and
\lambda(\tau,\sigma)
are Lagrange multipliers. Using this Hamiltonian we can obtain the phase space Lagrangian for bosonic strings.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{P}=\Pi_{\mu}\dot{X}^{\mu}-\frac{\lambda}{2}\left(\Pi^{2}+T^{2}X^{\prime 2}\right)-\rho\penalty\ \Pi_{\mu}X^{\prime\mu}.

(52)

The corresponding phase space action for bosonic strings is given by

\displaystyle S_{PS}=\int\penalty\ d^{2}\sigma\penalty\ \left\{\Pi_{\mu}\dot{X}^{\mu}-\frac{\lambda}{2}\left(\Pi^{2}+T^{2}X^{\prime 2}\right)-\rho\penalty\ \Pi_{\mu}X^{\prime\mu}\right\}.

(53)

This form of the action is important for non-Lorentzian limits of string theory
64
;
43
;
101
;
66
;
14
. Integrating out the canonical momentum from this phase space Lagrangian (
52
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{P}=\frac{1}{2\lambda}\left[\dot{X}^{2}-2\rho(\dot{X}\cdot X^{\prime})+(\rho^{2}-\lambda^{2}T^{2})X^{\prime 2}\right].

(54)

Identifying the coefficients of
\dot{X}^{2}
,
\dot{X}\cdot X^{\prime}
and
X^{\prime 2}
as metric components

\displaystyle g^{\alpha\beta}=\begin{bmatrix}1&-\rho\\
-\rho&\penalty\ \penalty\ \rho^{2}-\lambda^{2}T^{2}\end{bmatrix},\hskip 14.22636pt\sqrt{-g}=\sqrt{-\frac{1}{\text{det}g^{\alpha\beta}}}=\frac{1}{\lambda T}\penalty\ ,

(55)

we obtain the Polyakov form of the string action

\displaystyle S_{P}=\frac{T}{2}\int d^{2}\sigma\sqrt{-g}g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}X^{\mu}\partial_{\beta}X^{\nu}\eta_{\mu\nu}.

(56)

We recall that for point particle case, the action (
46
) could be interpreted as scalar field theory in the worldline (parametrized by
\tau
) of the particle where the Lagrange multiplier
e
plays the role of square root of worldline dynamical metric. Here too, one can see that the action (
56
) is a scalar field theory on two dimensional worldsheet (parametrized by
\tau,\sigma
) where
g^{\alpha\beta}
(which is constructed from Lagrange multipliers
\rho
and
\lambda
) plays the role of worldsheet dynamical inverse metric.

3.2
Classical analysis: Closed string

The Polyakov action (
56
) is known to display Poincare symmetry in the target spacetime, worldsheet diffeomorphism invariance given by

\displaystyle\sigma^{\alpha}\to\sigma^{\prime\alpha}=\sigma^{\prime}(\sigma),

(57)

and Weyl invariance given by

\displaystyle g_{\alpha\beta}\to g^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}=e^{2\phi}g_{\alpha\beta},\qquad\phi=\phi(\tau,\sigma).

(58)

Since the worldsheet diffeomorphism invariance and the Weyl invariance are gauge symmetries, one can fix the gauge to simplify the action. Since there are two coordinates to fix and the woldsheet metric has three independent components, by choosing
\sigma,\tau
appropriately we can always fix the metric to be conformally flat, i.e.

\displaystyle g_{\alpha\beta}=e^{2\phi}\eta_{\alpha\beta}.

(59)

Since the action (
56
) is Weyl invariant, the action constructed on
g_{\alpha\beta}=e^{2\phi}\eta_{\alpha\beta}
always equals the action constructed on
g_{\alpha\beta}=\eta_{\alpha\beta}
. Hence, we can make the following gauge choice for the worldsheet metric

\displaystyle g_{\alpha\beta}=\eta_{\alpha\beta}.

(60)

This gauge choice is called conformal gauge.

Residual gauge symmetry:

Even after this gauge fixing, (
56
) is found to be invariant under the following gauge transformation

\displaystyle\sigma^{+}\to\sigma^{+}+f(\sigma^{+}),\qquad\sigma^{-}\to\sigma^{-}+g(\sigma^{-}),\qquad\sigma^{\pm}=\tau\pm\sigma.

(61)

Under the gauge transformation, a function
G(\sigma^{+},\sigma^{-})
transforms as

\displaystyle\delta G=f(\sigma^{+})\partial_{+}G+g(\sigma^{-})\partial_{-}G\penalty\ =\penalty\ [\mathcal{L}(f)+\bar{\mathcal{L}}(g)]G.

(62)

In the above
\partial_{\pm}=\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{\tau}\pm\partial_{\sigma})
. The generators of these transformation (
62
) can be Fourier expanded as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(f)

\displaystyle=f(\sigma^{+})\partial_{+}=\sum_{n}a_{n}e^{in\sigma^{+}}\partial_{+}=-i\sum_{n}a_{n}\mathcal{L}_{n}

(63a)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{L}}(g)

\displaystyle=g(\sigma^{-})\partial_{-}=\sum_{n}b_{n}e^{in\sigma^{-}}\partial_{-}=-i\sum_{n}b_{n}\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}.

(63b)

In the above, we have used the Fourier expansions
f(\sigma^{+})=\sum_{n}a_{n}e^{in\sigma^{+}}
and
g(\sigma^{-})=\sum_{n}b_{n}e^{in\sigma^{-}}
. From (
63
) one can see that the symmetry transformations (
61
) are generated by the infinite number of local generators
\{\mathcal{L}_{n},\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\}
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{n}=ie^{in\sigma^{+}}\partial_{+},\qquad\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}=ie^{in\sigma^{-}}\partial_{-}.

(64)

These generators satisfy the Witt algebra given by

[\mathcal{L}_{n},\mathcal{L}_{m}]=(n-m)\mathcal{L}_{n+m},\qquad[\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n},\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{m}]=(n-m)\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n+m},\qquad[\mathcal{L}_{n},\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{m}]=0.

(65)

Upon quantisation of this theory, we encounter the centrally extended version of this algebra called the Virasoro algebra given by

\begin{split}[\mathcal{L}_{n},\mathcal{L}_{m}]=&(n-m)\mathcal{L}_{n+m}+\frac{c}{12}n(n^{2}-1)\delta_{n+m},\\
[\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n},\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{m}]=&(n-m)\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n+m}+\frac{\bar{c}}{12}n(n^{2}-1)\delta_{n+m},\\
[\mathcal{L}_{n},\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{m}]=&0,\end{split}

(66)

where
c
and
\bar{c}
are the central charges of the Virasoro algebra. This is famously also the symmetry algebra of a relativistic 2d CFT.

Equations of motion and constraints:

Polyakov action (
56
) gives us the following EOM of the fields
X^{\mu}

\displaystyle\square X^{\mu}=0.

(67)

In the conformal gauge (
60
) this equation simplifies to the free wave equation

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}\partial^{\alpha}X^{\mu}=0.

(68)

The Polyakov action (
56
) also contains the worldsheet metric
g_{\alpha\beta}
and there will be EOM of these fields. Since these fields are not dynamic, these EOM act as constraints on
X^{\mu}
. Since the variation of the action with respect to the metric gives us the stress energy tensor
T_{\alpha\beta}
, the constraints are given by

\displaystyle T_{\alpha\beta}=-\frac{2}{T}\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\frac{\delta S_{P}}{\delta g^{\alpha\beta}}=0.

(69)

Solving (
69
) for Polyakov action and applying the conformal gauge
g_{\alpha\beta}=\eta_{\alpha\beta}
one gets the following

\displaystyle T_{00}=

\displaystyle T_{11}=\frac{1}{2}(\dot{X}^{2}+X^{\prime 2})=0

(70a)

\displaystyle T_{01}=\dot{X}\cdot X^{\prime}=0

(70b)

The constraints in (
70
) can be rewritten in terms of worldsheet lightcone coordinates as

\displaystyle T_{++}=(\partial_{+}X)^{2}=0,\qquad T_{--}=(\partial_{-}X)^{2}=0.

(71)

Mode expansion of closed strings:

Now, we look into the most generic solution to the EOM (
68
). The solution of (
68
) can be Fourier expanded as below

\begin{split}X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=x^{\mu}+\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\alpha_{0}^{\mu}(\tau+\sigma)&+\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\tilde{\alpha}_{0}^{\mu}(\tau-\sigma)\\
+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}&\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\left[\alpha^{\mu}_{n}e^{-in(\tau+\sigma)}+\tilde{\alpha}^{\mu}_{n}e^{-in(\tau-\sigma)}\right].\end{split}

(72)

We are interested in closed strings. So
\sigma
and
\sigma+2\pi
need to be identified, which implies boundary condition
X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma+2\pi)=X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)
and
\alpha_{0}=\tilde{\alpha}_{0}
. We end up with

\displaystyle X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=x^{\mu}+2\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\alpha_{0}^{\mu}\tau+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\left[\alpha^{\mu}_{n}e^{-in(\tau+\sigma)}+\tilde{\alpha}^{\mu}_{n}e^{-in(\tau-\sigma)}\right].

(73)

In the above
\alpha^{\mu}_{0}=\tilde{\alpha}^{\mu}_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}k^{\mu}
,
k^{\mu}
being the momentum of the closed string. In order to see this more plainly, we first look into the momentum conjugate of
X^{\mu}
is given by

\begin{split}&\Pi_{\mu}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\dot{X}^{\mu}}=\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\dot{X}^{\nu}\eta_{\nu\mu},\end{split}

(74)

The total momentum of the string can be found by integrating the canonical momentum over the entire string i.e.,

\begin{split}k^{\mu}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\sigma\penalty\ \Pi^{\mu}=\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}&\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\sigma\penalty\ \dot{X}^{\mu}=\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\penalty\ 2\pi\sqrt{2\alpha^{\prime}}\alpha^{\mu}_{0},\\
\implies&\alpha^{\mu}_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}k^{\mu}.\end{split}

(75)

Now, we look into the Poisson brackets between target spacetime coordinates
X^{\mu}
and there momentum conjugate
\Pi^{\mu}
. They are

\{X^{\mu}(\sigma,\tau),X^{\nu}(\sigma^{\prime},\tau)\}_{P.B.}=\{\Pi^{\mu}(\sigma,\tau),\Pi^{\nu}(\sigma^{\prime},\tau)\}_{P.B.}=0,

(76a)

\{X^{\mu}(\sigma,\tau),\Pi^{\nu}(\sigma^{\prime},\tau)\}_{P.B.}=\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta(\sigma-\sigma^{\prime}).

(76b)

Using the mode expansion of
X^{\mu}
as given in (
73
) and the expression of canonical momentum density
\Pi^{\mu}
as given in (
74
), one can translate (
76
) to the Poisson bracket between the modes
\alpha,\tilde{\alpha}

\begin{split}\{\alpha_{m}^{\mu},\alpha_{n}^{\nu}\}=-im\delta_{m+n,0}\eta^{\mu\nu},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \{\tilde{\alpha}_{m}^{\mu},\tilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\nu}\}=-im\delta_{m+n,0}\eta^{\mu\nu},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \{\alpha_{m}^{\mu},\tilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\nu}\}=0.\end{split}

(77)

Using the mode expansion in (
73
), the constraints(
71
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle T_{++}=(\partial_{+}X)^{2}=\alpha^{\prime}\sum_{n}\mathcal{L}_{n}e^{-in\sigma^{+}},\qquad T_{--}=(\partial_{-}X)^{2}=\alpha^{\prime}\sum_{n}\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}e^{-in\sigma^{-}}.

(78a)

In the above, we have defined

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}\alpha_{m}\cdot\alpha_{n-m},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}\tilde{\alpha}_{m}\cdot\tilde{\alpha}_{n-m}.

(79)

The Poisson brackets of
\mathcal{L}_{n}
and
\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
can be calculated from the Poisson brackets of
\alpha,\tilde{\alpha}

\displaystyle\{\mathcal{L}_{n},\mathcal{L}_{m}\}=-i(n-m)\mathcal{L}_{n+m},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \{\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n},\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{m}\}=-i(n-m)\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n+m},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \{\mathcal{L}_{n},\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{m}\}=0.

(80)

This is of course the classical version of the Virasoro algebra, now arising out of the mode expansion of the solutions of the EOM and the constraints. The methods of relativistic 2d CFT, built out of the Virasoro algebra, would be central to the understanding of the quantum aspects of the closed bosonic string, which we now go on to describe.

3.3
Quantum Theory

In the quantum theory, all the observables are promoted to operators and consequently the Poisson brackets between those observables are replaced with commutators. The equal time commutator between
X(\sigma,\tau)
and
\Pi(\sigma,\tau)

\begin{split}\left[X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma),\Pi^{\nu}(\tau,\sigma^{\prime})\right]=i\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta(\sigma-\sigma^{\prime}).\end{split}

(81)

Similarly the Poisson brackets (
77
) are replaced by

\begin{split}[\alpha_{m}^{\mu},\alpha_{n}^{\nu}]=m\delta_{m+n,0}\eta^{\mu\nu},\qquad[\tilde{\alpha}_{m}^{\mu},\tilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\nu}]=m\delta_{m+n,0}\eta^{\mu\nu},\qquad[\alpha_{m}^{\mu},\tilde{\alpha}_{n}^{\nu}]=0.\end{split}

(82)

Consequently the Poisson brackets in (
80
) will be replaced by Virasoro algebra described in (
66
). Now, let us look into the Hilbert space of the theory. The ground state is defined as

\displaystyle\alpha^{\mu}_{0}\ket{0,k^{\mu}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}=\tilde{\alpha}^{\mu}_{0}\ket{0,k^{\mu}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}=\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}k^{\mu}\ket{0,k^{\mu}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}

(83a)

\displaystyle\alpha^{\mu}_{n}\ket{0,k^{\mu}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}=\tilde{\alpha}^{\mu}_{n}\ket{0,k^{\mu}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \forall n>0.

(83b)

One can construct the basis of the Hilbert space by acting the creation operators
\{\alpha_{-n}^{\mu},\tilde{\alpha}_{-n}^{\mu}\}
with
n>0
on the vacuum. A generic state in this basis will be

\displaystyle\ket{n_{L},n_{R}}=\sum_{j}\rho_{j}\Bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{p}\alpha^{a_{i}}_{-m_{i}}\prod_{j=1}^{q}\tilde{\alpha}^{b_{j}}_{-n_{j}}\Bigg)_{j}\ket{0,k^{\mu}}_{\alpha^{\prime}},\quad n_{L}=\sum_{i}^{p}a_{i}m_{i},\,n_{R}=\sum_{i}^{q}b_{i}n_{i}.

(84)

In the above
n_{L}
and
n_{R}
respectively are the eigenvalues of the number operator which determines the level of the state

\displaystyle N=\sum_{m>0}\alpha_{-m}\cdot\alpha_{m};\hskip 14.22636pt\widetilde{N}=\sum_{m>0}\tilde{\alpha}_{-m}\cdot\tilde{\alpha}_{m}.

(85)

Now, we need to impose the quantum version of the constraints (
71
) on this Hilbert space in order to find the physical states. The most general constraint one can impose is a quantum version of the vanishing of the stress tensor, where the corresponding matrix elements vanish. This is given by

\bra{phys^{\prime}}T_{++}\ket{phys}=\bra{phys^{\prime}}T_{--}\ket{phys}=0.

(86)

Rewriting (
245
) in terms of
\mathcal{L}_{n}
and
\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
one gets the following

\bra{phys^{\prime}}\mathcal{L}_{n}\ket{phys}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \bra{phys^{\prime}}\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\ket{phys}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall n\in\mathbb{Z}.

(87)

However, in usual tensile string theory one chooses to impose a more stringent constraint. We demand that the physical states belong to the highest weight representation of Virasoro algebra
9
9

9

Although string theory constructed on this constraint is the most studied, there are string theories defined on other kind of constraints which are consistent with the sandwich condition (
86
). In later part of the review we shall see that there is a variant of string theory which classically has the same Polyakov action but the quantum theory of which is constructed on a different representation of Virasoro algebra. A recent work
16
has aimed towards more general studies on the Virasoro sandwich constraint.
.

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{n}-a\delta_{n,0})\ket{phys}=(\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}-a\delta_{n,0})\ket{phys}=0.

(88)

In the above,
a
is the normal ordering constant of both
\mathcal{L}_{0}
and
\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
. Value of
a
is
1
and it can be shown from the Lorentz invariance of this theory in the target spacetime
10
10

10

The same calculation also simultaneously shows that the target spacetime dimension should be
D=26
(critical dimension). We do not provide the details of the calculations here. However, in upcoming sections we shall see that tensionless null strings too, have same critical dimensions which will be discussed in detail.
. The operators
\mathcal{L}_{0}
and
\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
can be expressed in terms of the number operators as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}=N+\frac{1}{2}\alpha^{2}_{0}=N+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{4}k^{2},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \bar{\mathcal{L}}_{0}=\widetilde{N}+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\alpha}^{2}_{0}=\widetilde{N}+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{4}k^{2}.

(89)

In the above, we have used the fact that
\alpha^{\mu}_{0}=\tilde{\alpha}^{\mu}_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}k^{\mu}
. Applying (
89
) to the physical state condition (
88
), and recalling that
m^{2}=-k^{2}
, one can see that the mass for a string state
\ket{n_{L},n_{R}}
is given by

\displaystyle m^{2}_{L}=\frac{4}{\alpha^{\prime}}(n_{L}-1),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ m^{2}_{R}=\frac{4}{\alpha^{\prime}}(n_{R}-1).

(90)

In the above,
m^{2}_{L}
is the mass-squared calculated from the
\mathcal{L}_{0}
physical state condition and
m^{2}_{R}
is the mass-squared calculated from the
\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
physical state condition. For sake of consistency we must have
m^{2}_{L}=m^{2}_{R}=m^{2}
which essentially leads us to level matching condition

\displaystyle n_{L}=n_{R}=n.

(91)

Hence, we end up with the following mass spectrum for tensile closed bosonic strings

\displaystyle m^{2}=\frac{4}{\alpha^{\prime}}(n-1).

(92)

From here one can deduce the nature of particles emerging from the bosonic string mass spectrum. Ground state (
n=0
) is tachyonic while first excited state
n=1
gives massless spin two particles which lead to gravitons, antisymmetric Kalb-Ramond background fields and dilatons.

For a quick recap of tensile open strings and RNS formalism of tensile superstrings one can see Appendices
A
and
B
.

4
A Summary of Carrollian Symmetry

In the introduction, we mentioned that on the worldsheet of the tensionless null string, the relativistic Virasoro algebra is replaced by the Conformal Carrollian algebra (CCA). As with the Virasoro algebra, these residual gauge symmetries which form the CCA, now dictate the structure of the theory of the null string. In this section, we will review the basic ingredients of Carrollian and Conformal Carrollian symmetries and focus on two dimensional Carrollian CFTs. These methods would be crucial for our analysis of the null string.

4.1
Non-Lorentzian limits: Galilean symmetry

The isometry group of flat Minkowski spacetimes in
D
dimensions is the Poincare group
ISO(D-1,1)
:

\displaystyle[P_{\mu},P_{\nu}]=0,\quad[J_{\mu\nu},P_{\rho}]=-2\eta_{\rho[\mu}P_{\nu]},\quad[J_{\mu\nu},J_{\rho\sigma}]=4\eta_{[\mu[\rho}J_{\sigma]\nu]}.

(93)

Here
P_{\mu}=-\partial_{\mu},J_{\mu\nu}=x_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}-x_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}
are generators of translations and Lorentz transformations respectively. We will be interested in understanding the non-relativistic limit of this algebra and this gives the Galilei algebra, the commutators of which are given by

\displaystyle[J_{ij},J_{kl}]=4\delta_{[i[k}J_{l]j]},\penalty\ \penalty\ \,[J_{ij},P_{k}]=-2\delta_{k[i}P_{j]},\penalty\ \penalty\ \,[J_{ij},G_{k}]=2\delta_{k[i}G_{j]},\penalty\ \penalty\ \,[G_{i},H]=-P_{i}.

(94)

The process of getting to the Galilean algebra from the Poincare algebra is called a Inönü-Wigner contraction
119
. As an illustrative example, the Lorentz boosts contract to the Galilean boosts as follows:

\displaystyle J_{0i}=t\partial_{i}+x_{i}\partial_{t}\to\frac{1}{\epsilon}t\partial_{i}+\epsilon x_{i}\partial_{t}\quad\implies G_{i}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon J_{0i}=t\partial_{i}.

(95)

This algebra admits a central extension where there is another non-vanishing commutator
[G_{i},P_{j}]=\delta_{ij}M
, where
M
is a constant which can be identified with the non-relativistic mass. The centrally extended Galilean algebra is sometimes called the Bargmann algebra.

Gravity in the non-relativistic world or Newtonian gravity admits a geometric formulation known as Newton-Cartan geometry
65
. (Pseudo)-Riemannian structures of general relativity are replaced by Newton-Cartan manifolds in Newtonian gravity. These structures form the basis of a geometric description of Galilean symmetry. A Newton-Cartan manifold is described by a doublet
\{h^{ab},\tau_{a}\}
, where
h^{ab}
is an inverse degenerate metric and
\tau_{a}
is a vector that generates its kernel:

h^{ab}\tau_{a}=0.

The isometry of the flat version of this structure

\displaystyle\pounds_{\xi}{h}^{ab}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \quad\pounds_{\xi}\tau_{a}=0.

(96)

is the Galilean algebra defined above. In the above
\pounds
denotes a Lie derivative.

4.2
Non-Lorentzian limits: Carrollian Symmetry

While this
c\to\infty
limit is the most well explored singular limit of relativistic physics, there is another singular limit which has been of great interest in recent years. This limit where
c\to 0
, initially introduced by Levy-Leblond
131
and Sen Gupta
153
. This leads to the Carroll algebra, the non-vanishing commutators of which are given by

\displaystyle[J_{ij},J_{kl}]=4\delta_{[i[k}J_{l]j]},\penalty\ \,[J_{ij},P_{k}]=-2\delta_{k[i}P_{j]},\penalty\ \,[J_{ij},C_{k}]=-2\delta_{k[i}C_{j]},\penalty\ \,[P_{i},C_{j}]=\delta_{ij}H.

(97)

To contrast it from the Galilean contraction, it is instructive to note that the Carrollian contraction (
t\to\epsilon t
,
x^{i}\to x^{i}
,
\epsilon\to 0
) of the Lorentz boosts leads to

\displaystyle J_{0i}=t\partial_{i}+x_{i}\partial_{t}\to\epsilon t\partial_{i}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}x_{i}\partial_{t}\quad\implies C_{i}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon J_{0i}=x_{i}\partial_{t}.

(98)

The Carroll algebra has certain peculiarities, e.g.
H=-\partial_{t}
, the Hamiltonian, is a central element. This in particular means Carroll boost commute with the Hamiltonian
[C_{i},H]=0
, which means boosting a system does not change its energy.

We briefly discuss the geometry behind the Carroll algebra, starting with Minkowski spacetime, and taking the limit
c\to 0
. For a more in-depth review of the geometric structures associated with Carroll symmetry, the reader is pointed to
73
. The Minkowski metric in the Carroll limit gives:

\displaystyle\eta_{\mu\nu}\rightarrow h_{\mu\nu}=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
0&\quad\mathbb{1}_{D-1}\end{bmatrix},\qquad-c^{2}\eta^{\mu\nu}\rightarrow v^{\mu}v^{\nu}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&\quad\mathbb{0}_{D-1}\end{bmatrix}\,.

(99)

So we see that in the Carroll limit,
D
-dimensional Lorentzian metric has become a degenerate rank
D-1
Carroll metric
h_{\mu\nu}
while The inverse metric
\eta^{\mu\nu}
has evolved into degenerate inverse metric of rank
1
, and hence has been expressed as
v^{\mu}v^{\nu}
. These satisfy:

h_{\mu\nu}v^{\nu}=0.

A generic Carrollian manifold, as opposed to the flat Carroll manifold obtained from Minkowski spacetime above, is defined as a manifold of dimension
D
endowed with a symmetric, degenerate rank
(D-1)
metric
h_{ab}
and a vector
v^{a}
that generates the kernel of
h
. The structure defined is a fibre bundle with the base space is formed by
D-1
spatial dimensions and a one-dimensional fibre which is the null direction. The isometry algebra of flat Carroll manifolds

\displaystyle\pounds_{\xi}h_{\mu\nu}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \quad\pounds_{\xi}v^{\mu}=0,

(100)

is identical to the Carroll algebra we have discussed above.

The Carrollian manifolds and their non-relativistic cousins, the Newton-Cartan manifolds are very similar to each other and are related to each other by a base
\leftrightarrow
fibre flip. Interestingly, for
D=2
, the base and fibre are both one dimensional and for the flat case, the symmetry algebras, the Galilean and Carrollian algebras are isomorphic. The isomorphism extends to the conformal variants, viz. the Galilean and Conformal Carrollian Algebras. We will see that this will play an important role in the discussion of tensionless strings.

In recent years, Carroll symmetry has found applications in various corners of the relativistic world. Generic null hypersurfaces exhibit Carrollian symmetry, and this fact plays a key role in null/tensionless string theory where the worldsheet of the string becomes null and we will observe Carrollian geometry on the worldsheet. Some of the classic examples of null hypersurface are light-cones, black hole event horizons, and null infinity of asymptotically flat spacetime. For a detailed review of Carrollian physics, the reader is referred to the recent review
11
.

4.3
Conformal Field Theory and Carroll Limit

One can generalize the above discussion of non-Lorentzian structures to involve conformal symmetries. In relativistic spacetimes of generic dimensions conformal transformations are those which transform the metric by a scale:

\displaystyle\pounds_{\xi}g_{\mu\nu}=\Lambda g_{\mu\nu}.

(101)

Conformal field theories are the field theories invariant under these transformations. The conformal isometries of flat spacetimes yields the relativistic conformal algebra. In two dimensions, this symmetry algebra enhances to form two copies of the Virasoro algebra:

\begin{split}[\mathcal{L}_{n},\mathcal{L}_{m}]=&(n-m)\mathcal{L}_{n+m}+\frac{c}{12}n(n^{2}-1)\delta_{n+m},\\
[\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n},\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{m}]=&(n-m)\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n+m}+\frac{\bar{c}}{12}n(n^{2}-1)\delta_{n+m},\\
[\mathcal{L}_{n},\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{m}]=&0.\end{split}

(102)

Here
c
and
\bar{c}
are the central charges of the Virasoro algebra.
We recall that, we have already encountered the generators (
64
) as the residual symmetry generators of bosonic closed string theory.

Carrollian Conformal Algebra:

The conformal isometries of Carrollian manifolds are given by:

\displaystyle\pounds_{\xi}h_{\mu\nu}=\lambda_{1}h_{\mu\nu},\qquad\pounds_{\xi}v^{\mu}={\lambda_{2}}v^{\mu},\qquad\frac{\lambda_{1}}{\lambda_{2}}=-\frac{z}{2}

(103)

In the above,
z
is the anisotropy parameter, also called a dynamical critical exponent, which when set to
z=1
gives an isotropic scaling of spatial and temporal directions. In this review, we will only be concerned with
z=1
. The conformal isometries of flat Carrollian manifolds generates the Conformal Carroll algebra. We will focus on dimensions
D=2
. Here the solution to the above equation reads:

\xi=[g(x^{1})+x^{0}f^{\prime}(x^{1})]\partial_{0}+f(x^{1})\partial_{1}.

(104)

For later purposes it is useful to rewrite the above as

L(f)=x^{0}\,f^{\prime}(x^{1})\,\partial_{0}+f(x^{1})\,\partial_{1},\quad\quad M(g)=g(x^{1})\partial_{0}.

(105)

In cylindrical coordinates
(x^{0},x^{1})=(\tau,\sigma)
with
\sigma\sim\sigma+2\pi
, the functions
f,g
are periodic and can be Fourier expanded. In terms of these modes, the generators of the Conformal Carroll algebra on the cylinder become:

\displaystyle L_{n}

\displaystyle=i\,e^{in\sigma}(\partial_{\sigma}+in\tau\,\partial_{\tau}),\quad M_{n}=i\,e^{in\sigma}\partial_{\tau}.

(106)

Here
\tau
is the null temporal direction and
\sigma
parametrizes the angular coordinate of
S^{1}
.
These generators (
106
) satisfy the
2d
Carrollian Conformal Algebra (CCA
2
):

\displaystyle\left[L_{n},L_{m}\right]

\displaystyle=(n-m)L_{n+m}+\frac{c_{L}}{12}(n^{3}-n)\delta_{n+m},

(107a)

\displaystyle\left[L_{n},M_{m}\right]

\displaystyle=(n-m){M}_{n+m}+\frac{c_{M}}{12}(n^{3}-n)\delta_{n+m},

(107b)

\displaystyle\left[M_{n},M_{m}\right]

\displaystyle=0.

(107c)

Here
c_{L}
and
c_{M}
are the central charges for the algebra. The 2d CCA is isomorphic to
3
-dimensional Bondi-van der Burgh-Metzner-Sachs
(BMS
3
) algebra
86
, which is the symmetry algebra of asymptotically flat 3d spacetimes at the null boundary
52
;
150
;
4
;
39
. This fact plays a pivotal role in the Carrollian framework of holography of asymptotically flat spacetime
30
;
24
;
86
;
18
;
17
;
80
. This algebra, as advertised before, will play the central role in the study of null strings since it appears as the worldsheet residual gauge symmetry algebra of the null strings.

Figure 2:
Null cylinder

The 2d CCA can be obtained from two copies of the Virasoro algebra by
the following contraction

L_{n}=\mathcal{L}_{n}-\mathcal{\bar{L}}_{-n},\quad\quad M_{n}=\epsilon(\mathcal{L}_{n}+\mathcal{\bar{L}}_{-n}),\qquad\epsilon\to 0.

(108)

It is also important to note that we can impose the Carroll limit as

\displaystyle\tau\to\epsilon\tau,\quad\sigma\to\sigma,\quad\epsilon\to 0

(109)

on the cylinder coordinates. Applying this, together with (
108
) together with on the expression of the vector fields representations of the Virasoro generators on the cylinder, one obtains (
106
).

Conformal Carrollian stress tensors:

We will be interested in understanding stress tensors for the residual symmetry algebra of the null string in our attempts to quantise the theory and hence it is important to first understand the notion of stress tensors in 2d Carrollian CFTs. Stress tensors in Carroll CFTs have been obtained intrisically
84
;
151
. Here, we derive this in the Carroll limit following (
108
) of a 2d relativistic CFT following
27
;
32
. The holomorphic and antiholomorphic stress tensors for a 2d relativistic CFT defined on the cylinder are

\displaystyle T_{cyl}=\sum_{n}{\mathcal{L}}_{n}e^{in\omega}-\frac{c}{24},\quad\bar{T}_{cyl}=\sum_{n}\bar{{\mathcal{L}}}_{n}e^{in\omega}-\frac{\bar{c}}{24}.

(110)

We now impose the limit (
108
) along with (
109
)
and define
11
11

11

This kind of linear combinations of stress tensors were first considered for the non-relativistic case
31
.

\displaystyle T_{1}(\sigma,\tau)=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\left(T_{cyl}-\bar{T}_{cyl}\right),\quad T_{2}(\sigma,\tau)=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon\left(T_{cyl}+\bar{T}_{cyl}\right).

(111)

This results in

\displaystyle T_{1}(\sigma,\tau)=\sum_{n}\left(L_{n}-in\tau M_{n}\right)e^{-in\sigma}+\frac{c_{L}}{24},\quad T_{2}(\sigma)=\sum_{n}M_{n}e^{-in\sigma}+\frac{c_{M}}{24}.

(112)

T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are the stress tensors of the 2d Carroll CFT (on the cylinder). We can now inverting the above relations to rewrite the generators of the algebra in terms of the stress tensors as follows

\displaystyle L_{n}=\int d\sigma\,(T_{1}+in\tau T_{2})e^{in\sigma},\quad M_{n}=\int d\sigma\,T_{2}e^{in\sigma}.

(113)

The central terms in (
112
) results in shifting of the zero modes
L_{0}
and
M_{0}
. The expressions for the stress tensors will show up prominently in our analysis of the constraints on the null worldsheet in the following sections.

4.4
Representation Theory of CCA

We now look at representations of CCA
2
and their relations with representations of Virasoro algebra. This would be central to our discussions of the quantum null strings.

Induced Representation:

The most extensively studied representation of Virasoro algebra is the highest weight representation (HWR). Here primary states are defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}\ket{h,\bar{h}}=h\ket{h,\bar{h}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \bar{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\ket{h,\bar{h}}=\bar{h}\ket{h,\bar{h}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{L}_{n}\ket{h,\bar{h}}=\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\ket{h,\bar{h}}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \forall n>0.

(114)

Descendant states are constructed by operating
\mathcal{L}_{n}
s and
\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
s (where
n<0
) on primary states. Together, the primary and its descendants form a Verma module, and different Verma modules build the entire Hilbert space.

The Carroll contraction (
108
) maps Virasoro highest weight states to the rest-frame states of the BMS
3
(or CCA
2
) induced representation
63
. Let us assume that in the Carroll limit, the Virasoro primary
\ket{h,\bar{h}}
maps to
\ket{M,s}
, where

\displaystyle L_{0}\ket{M,s}=s\ket{M,s},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ M_{0}\ket{M,s}=M\ket{M,s}.

(115)

Using (
114
) in (
108
) and (
115
) leads us to the following

\displaystyle\Bigg(\frac{M_{\pm n}}{\epsilon}\pm L_{\pm n}\Bigg)\ket{M,s}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ s=h-\bar{h}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ M=\epsilon(h+\bar{h}).

(116)

At
\epsilon\to 0
limit this leads to the following property of
\ket{M,s}

\displaystyle M_{n}\ket{M,s}=0\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \forall n\neq 0.

(117)

Such states are termed as rest-frame states of the induced representation of BMS
3
algebra
63
. A BMS induced module is constructed by operating
L_{n}
s (for all
n\neq 0
) on this state. For more details, the reader is referred to
41
;
143
;
63
.

Highest Weight Representation:

The CCA
2
also admits a highest weight representation. Here, following the ideas of the Virasoro algebra, Carroll primary states are defined as

\displaystyle L_{0}

\displaystyle\ket{h_{L},h_{M}}=h_{L}\ket{h_{L},h_{M}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ M_{0}\ket{h_{L},h_{M}}=h_{M}\ket{h_{L},h_{M}},

(118a)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ L_{n}\ket{h_{L},h_{M}}=M_{n}\ket{h_{L},h_{M}}=0\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \forall n>0.

(118b)

Together with its descendants, created by acting both
L_{n}
s and
M_{n}
s with
n<0
, this primary state construct a highest weight module module. As already highlighted, Virasoro HWR at Carroll limit leads to the induced representation and not the HWR of CCA
2
. However, Virasoro algebra also admits a different representation which at Carroll limit would lead us to the HWR of CCA
2
.

Virasoro Flipped Representation:

To understand the relativistic origin of the Carrollian HWR, we note that the Virasoro algebra allows an automorphism

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{n}=-\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{-n}.

(119)

Applying automorphism on the antiholomorphic sector, and keeping the holomorphic sector untouched, if we consider HWR of {
\mathcal{L}_{n},\bar{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{n}
}, we are led to a new representation, since HWR of
\bar{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{n}
are lowest weights in
\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
. Primary states of the HWR in {
\mathcal{L}_{n},\bar{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{n}
} is given by
