\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}\ket{h,\bar{h}}=h\ket{h,\bar{h}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \bar{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{0}\ket{h,\bar{h}}=\bar{h}\ket{h,\bar{h}},\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{n}\ket{h,\bar{h}}=\bar{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{n}\ket{h,\bar{h}}=0\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall n>0.

(120)

In terms of
\{\mathcal{L}_{n},\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\}
the definition (
120
) becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}\ket{h,\bar{h}}=h\ket{h,\bar{h}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \bar{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\ket{h,\bar{h}}=-\bar{h}\ket{h,\bar{h}},\penalty\ \penalty\

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{n}\ket{h,\bar{h}}=\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{-n}\ket{h,\bar{h}}=0\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall n>0.

(121)

This representation is called the flipped representation of Virasoro algebra. Tensile string theory constructed on Polyakov action has a cousin theory which classically shares the same Polyakov action but the quantum theory is constructed on this representation. The theory is called “twisted” string theory and has been studied in
129
;
69
;
130
.

Carroll Limit of Flipped Representation:

We will now see that applying the Carroll contraction (
108
) on the flipped representation of Virasoro algebra (
121
) gives HWR of CCA
2
. Performing the contraction (
108
) on the generators {
\mathcal{L}_{n},\bar{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{n}
}, we get:

\displaystyle L_{n}=\mathcal{L}_{n}-\bar{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{-n},\hskip 14.22636ptM_{n}=\epsilon(\mathcal{L}_{n}+\bar{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{-n}).

(122)

Rewriting this in terms of
\{\mathcal{L}_{n},\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\}
we get

\displaystyle L_{n}=\mathcal{L}_{n}+\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n},\hskip 14.22636ptM_{n}=\epsilon(\mathcal{L}_{n}-\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}).

(123)

This contraction maps HWR of Virasoro to HWR of CCA
2
. To see this explicitly, let us assume that the state
\ket{h,\bar{h}}
evolves to
\ket{h_{L},h_{M}}
, the eigenstate of
L_{0}
and
M_{0}
in Carroll limit
\epsilon\to 0
. Then one can see that

\displaystyle L_{n}\ket{h_{L},h_{M}}=(\mathcal{L}_{n}+\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n})\ket{h_{L},h_{M}}=0\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \forall n>0,

(124a)

\displaystyle M_{n}\ket{h_{L},h_{M}}=\epsilon(\mathcal{L}_{n}-\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n})\ket{h_{L},h_{M}}=0\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \forall n>0,

(124b)

\displaystyle h_{L}=h+\bar{h},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ h_{M}=\epsilon(h-\bar{h}).

(124c)

One can easily identify (
124
) as the HWR of CCA
2
(
118
). We shall see in later sections that this representation is important in the study of the quantum theory constructed on the flipped vacuum.

4.5
BCFT and Carroll limit

In a previous section we discussed CFTs on the entire 2d complex plane and on a cylinder. While CFTs defined on a cylinder are useful in the study of closed string theory, there are also open strings which have two ends and consequently, the worldsheets have two different boundaries, which also necessitates the study of CFTs on manifolds with boundaries. In the above context, one needs to study Boundary Conformal Field Theories (BCFTs). We begin by briefly looking at BCFTs defined on a cylinder with boundaries at
\sigma=0
and
\sigma=\pi
, and then proceed to understand their Carrollian version, since they will be relevant in the study of open null strings. Our approach, based on
22
, is a bit different from standard literature and in particular would be helpful for the Carrollian counterpart of BCFT.

Relativistic BCFT:

When boundaries are introduced on the manifold where a CFT is defined, some of the conformal symmetry generators distort the boundaries and can no longer be considered a part of the symmetry of the theory. The generators preserving the boundary conditions form the symmetry of the underlying theory with boundary. With the help of conformal transformations, complicated boundaries can be mapped onto the upper half plane (UHP). For 2d relativistic CFT on the UHP, the boundary preserving symmetries form one copy of Virasoro algebra. Let us begin with the generators of 2d CFTs defined on a cylinder as given in (
64
). One can rewrite these generators in a different basis, which will help us in segregating the boundary compatible generators from boundary incompatible ones.

\mathbb{L}_{n}=\mathcal{L}_{n}+\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\quad\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}✓}}\qquad\qquad\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}_{n}=\mathcal{L}_{n}-\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}\quad\text{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}✗}}

(125)

As demonstrated in
19
, on a cylinder
\mathbb{R}\times S^{1}
, the generators
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}_{n}
distort the boundaries
\sigma=0,\pi
, and hence incompatible with them. The surviving generators
\mathbb{L}_{n}
constitute one copy of Virasoro algebra. Throughout this section, we will denote the boundary compatible generators with
✓
and the incompatible generators with a

✗
.

Boundary Carroll Conformal Algebra:

We now consider CCA
2
with boundaries at
\sigma=0
and
\sigma=\pi
on a null cylinder
\mathbb{R}_{null}\times S^{1}
(see Fig.
3
). From (
106
), we see that with boundaries at
\sigma=0,\pi
,
L_{n}
are no longer allowed symmetry generators because of
\partial_{\sigma}
terms at both the boundaries. We rewrite these generators as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{n}=L_{n}-L_{-n}\quad\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}✓}}\qquad\qquad\mathcal{Q}_{n}=L_{n}+L_{-n}\quad\text{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}✗}}

(126)

The explicit forms of the generators are:

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{n}=-2\sin(n\sigma)\,\partial_{\sigma}-2n\tau\cos(n\sigma)\,\partial_{\tau}\,,\qquad\mathcal{Q}_{n}=2i\cos{(n\sigma)}\partial_{\sigma}-2in\tau\sin{(n\sigma)}\partial_{\tau}.

(127)

Figure 3:
Null cylinder with boundaries

As obvious from the above, the generators
\mathcal{O}_{n}
leave the
\sigma=0,\pi
boundaries undisturbed while
\mathcal{Q}_{n}
have
\partial_{\sigma}
at
\sigma=0,\pi
and hence, can no longer be allowed. In terms of the limit,

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{n}=\mathbb{L}_{n}-\mathbb{L}_{-n}\quad\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}✓}}\qquad\qquad\mathcal{Q}_{n}=\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}_{n}+\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}_{-n}\quad\text{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}✗}}

(128)

We again see that
\mathcal{O}_{n}
s arise from allowed BCFT generators
\mathbb{L}_{n}
, while
\mathcal{Q}_{n}
s come from the discarded ones
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}_{n}
. We also write generators
M_{n}
in a new basis

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{n}=M_{n}+M_{-n},\qquad\qquad\mathcal{R}_{n}=M_{n}-M_{-n}.

(129)

In explicit coordinate form, these are

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{n}=2\cos(n\sigma)\partial_{\tau}\qquad\mathcal{R}_{n}=-2\sin{(n\sigma)}\partial_{\tau}.

(130)

A priori both seem allowed since there are no errant
\partial_{\sigma}
terms. But from the limit, we see
\mathcal{R}_{n}
s arises from the discarded generators
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}_{n}
, while
\mathcal{P}_{n}
comes allowed ones
\mathbb{L}_{n}
s:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{n}=\epsilon(\mathbb{L}_{n}+\mathbb{L}_{-n})\quad\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.88,0}✓}}\qquad\qquad\mathcal{R}_{n}=\epsilon(\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}_{n}-\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}_{-n})\quad\text{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}✗}}

(131)

So we keep
\mathcal{P}_{n}
and discard
\mathcal{R}_{n}
. The boundary compatible generators constitute a hitherto unknown algebra called 2d Boundary Conformal Carrollian Algebra (BCCA
2
).

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{O}_{n},\,\mathcal{O}_{m}\right]

\displaystyle=(n-m)\mathcal{O}_{n+m}-(n+m)\mathcal{O}_{n-m},

(132a)

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{O}_{n},\,\mathcal{P}_{m}\right]

\displaystyle=(n-m)\mathcal{P}_{n+m}+(n+m)\mathcal{P}_{n-m}+\frac{c_{M}}{12}(n^{3}-n)(\delta_{n+m}+\delta_{n-m}),

(132b)

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{P}_{n},\,\mathcal{P}_{m}\right]

\displaystyle=0.

(132c)

In the upcoming sections we shall encounter this algebra as the worldsheet residual symmetry algebra of open null strings.

Superconformal Carrollian algebras

We have looked at conformal Carrollian symmetries without and with boundaries so far in our recap of Carrollian theories. We will see that these structures would appear on the worldsheet of closed and open bosonic null strings respectively. When we will move to tensionless superstring theory, we will encounter supersymmetric cousins of Carroll CFTs.

In Appendix C, we give details of Superconformal Carrollian symmetries. Among the important points are the existence of two different types of Superconformal Carrollian algebras, which we call Homogeneous and Inhomogeneous, and these algebras arise from different contractions of the parent Super Virasoro algebra. We give some details of this. We will find that these algebras are realised on the worldsheet of two different varieties of closed null superstrings. We then go on to describe Carrollian superconformal theories with boundaries and the Boundary Superconformal Carrollian algebras that would be important for open null superstring.

A homogeneous supersymmetric extension of BCCA
2
has been discussed in appendix
C.2
. We shall see later that this algebra appears as the residual gauge symmetry algebra of homogenous open null superstrings.

Armed with our history lesson and the brief encounters with string theory and Carrollian symmetries, we now venture into the heart of our review which will contain the details of the construction of the null tensionless string, first the classical aspects and then their quantum avatars.

Part II
Classical Null Strings

The main body of our review starts with the description of the classical aspects of the null tensionless string. We study the theories of the closed and open null (super)string in both the intrinsic and the limiting approach. We will see that the answers match very well in both approaches. In terms of the figure below, the square closes nicely in the classical regime.

Figure 4:
Classical analysis of the tensionless string

This part of our review is divided into sections as follows:

•

Bosonic Closed Null Strings:
Here we discuss the derivation of the ILST action which will form the basis of our intrinsic construction of the null string. The symmetries of the ILST action are discussed and the emergence of Conformal Carroll symmetries as residual gauge symmetries of the gauge fixed action is dealt with in some detail. We then discuss the tensionless limit on the worldsheet which allows us to check our intrinsic analysis.

•

Bosonic Open Null Strings:
After bosonic closed null strings, we turn our attention to open null strings. There are three options here and apart from the usual Dirichlet or Neumann boundary conditions, there is also a null boundary condition that can be chosen. We focus on the Dirichlet open string. The symmetry analysis yields the newly discovered Boundary Conformal Carrollian algebra on the worldsheet. We also address the limit from the tensile open string.

•

Closed Null Superstrings:
In close analogy with the bosonic null strings, closed null superstrings are then constructed. There are two variants here called the Homogeneous and Inhomogeneous null superstrings. Symmetries and mode expansions are addressed both from the intrinsic and limiting perspective.

•

Open Null Superstrings:
Finally we discuss open null superstrings and connect to Boundary Superconformal Carrollian symmetries.

5
Bosonic closed null strings

In this section we will discuss classical aspects of tensionless (null) limit of closed bosonic string theory. We first construct the action for bosonic null strings from the tensionless limit of Polyakov action following
120
;
133
. Then we go on to formulate the classical theory for closed null strings from first principles. We also complement this intrinsic viewpoint by taking appropriate limits on the corresponding results of the tensile closed string theory, a method introduced in
20
, which in turn implies that the contraction of the residual gauge symmetry of tensile strings leads to the conformal Carrollian symmetry.

5.1
Constructing Action of Null Strings

In Sec.
3.1
, we saw how the Hamiltonian formalism can be used to construct the einbein action for particles and Polyakov action for strings. In the current discussion we will see how these new actions are helpful to study the massless limit for point particles and consequently, the null limit of strings.

Massless limit of point particle action:

A salient feature of the einbein action for point particles (
46
) is that unlike (
7
), this action does not vanish at
m=0
. This action can also be interpreted as action of scalar fields in one dimensional space (worldline parametrised by
\tau
) coupled to gravity where
m^{2}
is the cosmological constant. The Lagrange multiplier
e(\tau)
here plays the role of the square root of the metric on the worldline. Putting
m=0
in this action, one obtains an action for massless point particles

\displaystyle I_{m=0}=\frac{1}{2}\int d\tau\penalty\ e^{-1}\dot{X}^{2}.

(133)

The EOM for the dynamical variable
e
from this action turns out to be
\dot{X}^{2}=0
, confirming that the action indeed describes a massless relativistic point particle. A generalisation of this analysis was performed for string theory in
120
, which subsequently allows one to write a tensionless limit of the string action.

Generalisation to strings – tensionless limit:

As outlined above, our objective now is to construct a string action that does not vanish in the limit
T=0
. To this end, we recall the phase space action for bosonic strings given in (
53
).
Integrating out the canonical momentum from this action yields

\displaystyle S_{PS}=\int\penalty\ d^{2}\sigma\penalty\ \frac{1}{2\lambda}\left[\dot{X}^{2}-2\rho(\dot{X}\cdot X^{\prime})+(\rho^{2}-\lambda^{2}T^{2})X^{\prime 2}\right].

(134)

Now once we fix the phase space action, looking at (
55
) together with (
56
), one can easily understand how to take the tensionless limit on the Polyakov action in a consistent manner. Equation (
55
) makes it clear that the worldsheet inverse dynamical metric
g^{\alpha\beta}
of the string itself also evolve along with tension of the string. Imposing a careful tensionless limit (
T\to\epsilon T
,
\epsilon\to 0
) on
g^{\alpha\beta}
as given in (
55
) will leave us with the following

\displaystyle g^{\alpha\beta}\xrightarrow{T\to 0}\begin{bmatrix}1&-\rho\\
-\rho&\penalty\ \penalty\ \rho^{2}\end{bmatrix}.

(135)

Consequently, the Polyakov action (
56
) can be equivalently written in terms of a tensor density, so that:

\displaystyle\frac{T}{2}\sqrt{-g}g^{\alpha\beta}\xrightarrow{T\to 0}V^{\alpha}V^{\beta},\qquad V^{\alpha}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}(1,-\rho),

(136)

where
V^{\alpha}
is a vector density connected to the geometry of the worldsheet. The action thus obtained is

\displaystyle S=\int d^{2}\sigma\hskip 2.84526ptV^{\alpha}V^{\beta}\partial_{\alpha}X^{\mu}\partial_{\beta}X^{\nu}\eta_{\mu\nu}.

(137)

This action is known as Isberg-Lindstrom-Sundborg-Theodoridis (ILST) action. Equation (
136
) implies that at tensionless limit the inverse metric density on the worldsheet has become a rank one degenerate matrix i.e. the string worldsheet acquires a null structure at tensionless limit. Hence, for flat target spacetime, the tensionless strings are synonymous to null strings
12
12

12

As mentioned in the introduction, one could have tensionless strings in AdS without null worldsheets, because of the existence of another scale, viz. the AdS radius.
.

Symmetries of bosonic null strings:

The worldsheet symmetries as we move from tensile to tensionless strings will be more explicit when we will look into the mode expansion of tensionless strings. Note that, just like the tensile string action, the null string action too is Poincare invariant in target spacetime. It is also worldsheet diffeomorphism (
\sigma^{\alpha}\to\sigma^{\alpha}+\epsilon^{\alpha}
) invariant where
X
and
V^{\alpha}
transform in the following way

\delta_{\xi}X^{\mu}=-\xi^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}X^{\mu},\qquad\delta_{\xi}V^{\alpha}=-V^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}\xi^{\alpha}+\xi^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}V^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{\beta}\xi^{\beta}\right)V^{\alpha}.

(138)

Since worldsheet diffeomorphism is a gauge symmetry, we now need to perform a gauge fixing. We choose to work with is the temporal gauge for
V^{\alpha}
, given by

\displaystyle V^{\alpha}=(v,0),\quad\quad\text{where}\quad v=\sqrt{\frac{1}{4\pi c^{\prime}}}=\text{const.}

(139)

The above choice of
v
is made for convenience. Equation (
139
) is the tensionless counterpart of conformal gauge in usual string theory
13
13

13

However, it is important to note that the gauge choice in (
139
) is far from unique; ILST action (
137
) can also be studied in different gauge choices as discussed in
12
. among the other gauge choices, the Ambitwistor gauge choice
68
is particularly important, about which we shall discuss later.
, where
c^{\prime}
is a constant with dimension
[c^{\prime}]=[L]^{2}
. We will see later that this
c^{\prime}
plays an important role in taking the tensionless limit.

Stress tensor and equations of motion:

The stress tensor of action (
137
) can be calculated as

\begin{split}T^{\alpha}_{\beta}&=\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial(\partial_{\alpha}\phi)}\partial_{\beta}\phi-\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}\mathcal{L}=V^{\alpha}V^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}X^{\mu}\partial_{\beta}X_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}V^{\rho}V^{\gamma}\partial_{\rho}X^{\mu}\partial_{\gamma}X_{\mu}\delta^{\alpha}_{\beta}.\end{split}

(140)

The gauge choice (
139
) results in the following energy momentum tensor

T^{0}_{0}=-T^{1}_{1}=\frac{v^{2}}{2}\dot{X}^{2}\equiv T_{2}(\sigma,\tau),\qquad T^{0}_{1}=v^{2}\dot{X}\cdot X^{\prime}\equiv T_{1}(\sigma,\tau).

(141)

It is straightforward to calculate the EOM for the action (
137
), which are:

\partial_{\alpha}(V^{\alpha}V^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}X^{\mu})=0,\qquad V^{\beta}\gamma_{\alpha\beta}=0,

(142)

where
\gamma_{\alpha\beta}
is the induced metric on the worldsheet defined in (
13
).
In the above, the equations on the left and right correspond to the EOM and the constraints, respectively. The gauge fixing (
139
) simplifies (
142
) to the following EOM:

\ddot{X}^{\mu}=0.

(143)

In the same gauge, the constraints reduce to

\displaystyle\dot{X}^{2}=0,\qquad\dot{X}\cdot X^{\prime}=0.

(144)

We identify them as the components of the stress-energy tensor
T^{0}_{0}
and
T^{0}_{1}
in (
141
).

Residual symmetry algebra:

Just like tensile string theory, for the null worldsheet as well, we can find a remnant gauge symmetry after the gauge fixing carried out in the previous section. We see that the vielbein (
139
) remains invariant under the following transformation

\displaystyle\sigma^{\alpha}\to\sigma^{\alpha}+\xi^{\alpha},\qquad\xi^{\alpha}=\left(f^{\prime}(\sigma)\tau+g(\sigma),f(\sigma)\right).

(145)

Where
f,g
are arbitrary functions of worldsheet variables.
A generic function
G(\sigma^{\alpha})
thus transforms under (
145
) as:

\delta G=\left[f^{\prime}(\sigma)\tau\partial_{\tau}+f(\sigma)\partial_{\sigma}+g(\sigma)\partial_{\tau}\right]G\penalty\ =\left[L(f)+M(g)\right]G.

(146)

Consequently, the generators can be defined as:

\displaystyle L(f)=f^{\prime}(\sigma)\tau\partial_{\tau}+f(\sigma)\partial_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n}a_{n}e^{in\sigma}(\partial_{\sigma}+in\tau\partial_{\tau})=-i\sum_{n}a_{n}L_{n},

(147a)

\displaystyle M(g)=g(\sigma)\partial_{\tau}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n}b_{n}e^{in\sigma}\partial_{\tau}=-i\sum_{n}b_{n}M_{n}.

(147b)

In the above, the Fourier modes of
f
and
g
can be taken as
f=\sum_{n}a_{n}e^{in\sigma},\penalty\ g=\sum_{n}b_{n}e^{in\sigma}
. Combining all these we can read off the differential generators of the residual gauge symmetry

\displaystyle L_{n}=ie^{in\sigma}(\partial_{\sigma}+in\tau\partial_{\tau}),\qquad M_{n}=ie^{in\sigma}\partial_{\tau}.

(148)

It is straightforward to check the algebra satisfied by these generators is given by

[L_{m},L_{n}]=(m-n)L_{m+n},\qquad[L_{m},M_{n}]=(m-n)M_{m+n},\qquad[M_{m},M_{n}]=0,

(149)

which can be identified as the classical part of the BMS
3
algebra where the central charges
c_{L}=c_{M}=0
.

5.2
Mode expansions

The EOM for the null string may look like that of a point particle, but the pullback fields are dependent on both worldsheet coordinates.
Hence the most generic solution of the EOM is given by

X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=x^{\mu}+\sqrt{\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}}A^{\mu}_{0}\sigma+\sqrt{\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}}B^{\mu}_{0}\tau+i\sqrt{\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}(A^{\mu}_{n}-in\tau B^{\mu}_{n})e^{-in\sigma}.

(150)

We further need to apply the closed string boundary condition given by

X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma+2\pi).

(151)

This implies vanishing of the linear zero mode term in
\sigma
in (
150
) i.e.
A^{\mu}_{0}=0
. Also the fact that
X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)
is real implies the hermiticty of the modes:

\displaystyle\big(A^{\mu}_{n}\big)^{\dagger}=A^{\mu}_{-n},\qquad\big(B^{\mu}_{n}\big)^{\dagger}=B^{\mu}_{-n}.

(152)

We can now use the mode expansion (
150
) on the two constraints (
144
):

\displaystyle T_{1}(\tau,\sigma)=

\displaystyle X^{\prime}\cdot\dot{X}=\sum_{n}\left[L_{n}-in\tau M_{n}\right]e^{-in\sigma}=0,

(153a)

\displaystyle T_{2}(\sigma)=

\displaystyle\dot{X}^{2}=\sum_{n}M_{n}e^{-in\sigma}=0,

(153b)

where
L_{n}
and
M_{n}
can be read off using the mode expansions

L_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}A_{-m}\cdot B_{m+n},\qquad M_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}B_{-m}\cdot B_{m+n}.

(154)

We identify that the mode expansion of the constraints in (
153
) as the mode expansion of stress tensor components of generic CCFTs as given earlier in (
112
).
Now, let us look into the momentum conjugate of
X^{\mu}
(i.e. the canonical momentum density) from the action (
137
) with gauge choice (
139
).

\begin{split}\Pi_{\mu}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}}{\partial\dot{X}^{\mu}}=\frac{1}{2\pi c^{\prime}}\dot{X}^{\nu}\eta_{\nu\mu},\qquad\implies\Pi^{\mu}=\frac{1}{2\pi c^{\prime}}\dot{X}^{\mu}.\end{split}

(155)

The total momentum of the string can be found in terms of the zero modes by integrating the canonical momentum over the entire string i.e.,

k^{\mu}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\sigma\penalty\ \Pi^{\mu}=\frac{1}{2\pi c^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\sigma\penalty\ \dot{X}^{\mu}=\frac{B^{\mu}_{0}}{\sqrt{2c^{\prime}}}\implies B^{\mu}_{0}=\sqrt{2c^{\prime}}k^{\mu}.

(156)

It is now imperative to understand the canonical structures of these modes.
Since the target spacetime for this theory remains flat, the equal time Poisson brackets satisfied by
X^{\mu}
and canonical momentum
\Pi^{\mu}
will be same as (
76
). Using these Poisson brackets, the relation (
155
) and the mode expansion of
X^{\mu}
given in (
150
), one can determine Poisson brackets for the oscillator modes (
A,B
). They are

\{A_{m}^{\mu},A_{n}^{\nu}\}_{PB}\penalty\ =\penalty\ \{B_{m}^{\mu},B_{n}^{\nu}\}_{PB}=0,\qquad\{A_{m}^{\mu},B_{n}^{\nu}\}_{PB}=-2im\delta_{m+n}\eta^{\mu\nu}.

(157)

Using (
157
), we can calculate the Poisson brackets for the generators
L_{n}
and
M_{n}

i\{L_{m},L_{n}\}_{PB}=(m-n)L_{m+n},\,i\{L_{m},M_{n}\}_{PB}=(m-n)M_{m+n},\,\{M_{m},M_{n}\}_{PB}=0.

(158)

This is of course the classical version of the residual symmetry algebra in (
149
) for the ILST action (
137
).

As we can see in (
157
), the modes (
A,B
) do not lead to the usual harmonic oscillator algebra. In order to get harmonic oscillator algebra let us consider the following combination of modes

C_{n}^{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}(A_{n}^{\mu}+B_{n}^{\nu}),\qquad\tilde{C}_{n}^{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}(-A_{-n}^{\mu}+B_{-n}^{\mu}).

(159)

The Poisson brackets between (
C,\tilde{C}
) now gives two decoupled sets of the oscillator algebra

\begin{split}\{C_{m}^{\mu},C_{n}^{\nu}\}=-im\delta_{m+n,0}\eta^{\mu\nu},\quad\{\tilde{C}_{m}^{\mu},\tilde{C}_{n}^{\nu}\}=-im\delta_{m+n,0}\eta^{\mu\nu},\quad\{C_{m}^{\mu},\tilde{C}_{n}^{\nu}\}=0.\end{split}

(160)

Since these oscilators are more useful, we can go ahead and rewrite the mode expansion (
150
) in terms of (
C,\tilde{C}
) as

\begin{split}X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=x^{\mu}+&\sqrt{\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}}(C^{\mu}_{0}-\tilde{C}^{\mu}_{0})\sigma+\sqrt{\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}}(C^{\mu}_{0}+\tilde{C}^{\mu}_{0})\tau\\
&+i\sqrt{\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\left[(C^{\mu}_{n}-\tilde{C}^{\mu}_{-n})-in\tau(C^{\mu}_{n}+\tilde{C}^{\mu}_{-n})\right]e^{-in\sigma}.\end{split}

(161)

Here again one can see that reality condtion of
X^{\mu}
needs

\displaystyle\big(C^{\mu}_{n}\big)^{\dagger}=C^{\mu}_{-n},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \big(C^{\mu}_{n}\big)^{\dagger}=C^{\mu}_{-n}.

(162)

Applying closed string boundary condition on this mode expansion leads to
C_{0}^{\mu}=\tilde{C}_{0}^{\mu}
. Also redoing the steps in (
156
) using this mode expansion will lead us to

\displaystyle C_{0}^{\mu}=\tilde{C}_{0}^{\nu}=\sqrt{\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}}k^{\mu}.

(163)

It is also illumination to re-express {
L_{n},M_{n}
} in terms of {
C,\tilde{C}
} modes, which ultimately give:

\displaystyle L_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}\left[C_{-m}\cdot C_{m+n}-\tilde{C}_{-m}\cdot\tilde{C}_{m-n}\right],

(164a)

\displaystyle M_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}\left[C_{-m}\cdot C_{m+n}+\tilde{C}_{-m}\cdot\tilde{C}_{m-n}+2C_{-m}\cdot\tilde{C}_{-m-n}\right].

(164b)

One should note the intriguing structure of the modes above. The generator
M_{n}
has a mixture of the
C,\tilde{C}
oscillators, that makes its action on a state non-trivial. We will come back to this when we discuss the quantum structure of this theory.

5.3
Taking tensionless limit on the worldsheet

Now that we have understood the intrinsic perspective on tensionless strings and associated degenerate structures in the last couple sections, its time to reaffirm those using consistent limits on the tensile worldsheet theory, as advertised before.

The infinite tension limit is the point-particle limit of string theory where the fundamental string shrinks to zero size. The tensionless limit, on the other hand, makes the string long and floppy. In terms of worldsheet coordinates (on the cylinder) this would be where the spatial direction becomes very large
\sigma\to\infty
keeping
\tau
fixed. For the closed string, this is equivalently done by rescaling

\tau\rightarrow\epsilon\tau,\quad\sigma\rightarrow\sigma,\quad\epsilon\to 0.

(165)

This is a Carrollian limit on the worldsheet where the worldsheet speed of light goes to zero and hence Carrollian structures appear as the worldsheet itself becomes a null surface.

Figure 5:
A heuristic description of the contraction on the worldsheet coordinates as the spatial coordinate becomes infinitely long, making the cylinder degenerate.

Limit on symmetries:

To see the effect of this scaling (
165
) on the underlying symmetries, let us remind the reader the form of the tensile Virasoro generators on cylinder (
64
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}e^{in(\tau+\sigma)}\left(\partial_{\tau}+\partial_{\sigma}\right),\quad\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}=\frac{i}{2}e^{in(\tau-\sigma)}\left(\partial_{\tau}-\partial_{\sigma}\right).

(166)

Under (
165
), the Virasoro generators expand as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{n}=\frac{i}{2}e^{in\sigma}\left(1+in\epsilon\tau\right)\left(\frac{\partial_{\tau}}{\epsilon}+\partial_{\sigma}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2}),\quad\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{-n}=\frac{i}{2}e^{in\sigma}\left(1-in\epsilon\tau\right)\left(\frac{\partial_{\tau}}{\epsilon}-\partial_{\sigma}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2}).

We now extract the leading and subleading terms of the contracted generators by defining the combinations:

\displaystyle M_{n}=\epsilon\left(\mathcal{L}_{n}+\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{-n}\right)=ie^{in\sigma}\partial_{\tau},\quad L_{n}=\mathcal{L}_{n}-\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{-n}=ie^{in\sigma}\left(\partial_{\sigma}+in\tau\,\partial_{\tau}\right).

(167)

These generators are identical to the residual symmetry algebra of tensionless strings we encountered earlier in the intrinsic analysis (
148
) and as we know, they satisfy the BMS
3
algebra (
149
) without the central extension.

Mode expansion from limit:

The tensionless limit on the worldsheet via the scalings can now be used to match up with the mode expansions from the EOM we derived earlier. It should be clear to the reader that when imposed on the closed tensile EOM in conformal gauge, i.e.
\Box X=0
, the scaling (
165
) simply gives rise to
\ddot{X}=0
, the tensionless version as has been derived intrinsically.
To investigate the mode expansions closely, let us recall the mode expansion of tensile closed bosonic string:

X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=x^{\mu}+2\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\alpha_{0}^{\mu}\tau+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\left[\alpha^{\mu}_{n}e^{-in(\tau+\sigma)}+\tilde{\alpha}^{\mu}_{n}e^{-in(\tau-\sigma)}\right].

(168)

Here
\alpha^{\prime}(\sim l_{s}^{2})
is related to string tension
T
as
\alpha^{\prime}=\frac{1}{2\pi T}
.
We take the tensionless limit on the worldsheet coordinates (
165
) along with a scaling on the string coupling:

\alpha^{\prime}\to\frac{c^{\prime}}{\epsilon},

(169)

where
c^{\prime}
is same dimensionful constant earlier introduced in (
139
). Clearly
\alpha^{\prime}\to c^{\prime}/\epsilon
implies the small tension regime, i.e.
T\to\epsilon T
, (
\epsilon\to 0
), or equivalently the infinite limit on the string length.
In this limit the tensile mode expansion takes the following form:

X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=x^{\mu}+\sqrt{2\epsilon c^{\prime}}\alpha_{0}^{\mu}\tau+i\sqrt{\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\left[\frac{\alpha_{n}^{\mu}-\tilde{\alpha}_{-n}^{\mu}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}-in\tau\sqrt{\epsilon}(\alpha_{n}^{\mu}+\tilde{\alpha}_{-n}^{\mu})\right]e^{-in\sigma}.

(170)

Comparing the limiting perspective in (
170
) and the intrinsic one in (
150
), we find the relation between (
\alpha,\tilde{\alpha}
) and (
A
,
B
) as:

A_{n}^{\mu}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}(\alpha_{n}^{\mu}-\tilde{\alpha}_{-n}^{\mu}),\qquad B_{n}^{\mu}=\sqrt{\epsilon}(\alpha_{n}^{\mu}+\tilde{\alpha}_{-n}^{\mu}).

(171)

Applying the canonical Poisson brackets between (
\alpha,\tilde{\alpha}
) to (
171
) and dropping higher order terms, we retrieve the Poisson brackets between the modes
(A,B)
of the null string (
157
).
We recall the expression of worldsheet Virasoro generators for the tensile string Polyakov action in terms of (
\alpha,\tilde{\alpha}
) from (
79
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}\alpha_{-m}\cdot\alpha_{m+n},\qquad\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}\tilde{\alpha}_{-m}\cdot\tilde{\alpha}_{m+n}.

Applying (
171
) on the expression of
L,M
in terms of the oscillators
A,B
given by (
154
), we recover the Inönü–Wigner contraction of the Virasoro algebra to the BMS algebra, now from the underlying oscillator modes:

\displaystyle L_{n}=\mathcal{L}_{n}-\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{-n},\qquad M_{n}=\epsilon(\mathcal{L}_{n}+\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{-n}).

(172)

All of the above serve as checks of the proposal of the tensionless limit in terms of a Carrollian limit on the worldsheet coordinates.
In passing, we also mention the relation between the oscillators (
C,\tilde{C}
) defined in (
159
) to the tensile oscillators:

\displaystyle C^{\mu}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\sqrt{\epsilon}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\Big)\alpha^{\mu}_{n}+\frac{1}{2}\Big(\sqrt{\epsilon}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\Big)\tilde{\alpha}^{\mu}_{-n},

(173a)

\displaystyle\tilde{C}^{\mu}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\sqrt{\epsilon}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\Big)\alpha^{\mu}_{-n}+\frac{1}{2}\Big(\sqrt{\epsilon}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\Big)\tilde{\alpha}^{\mu}_{n}.

(173b)

Since the transformation from (
\alpha,\tilde{\alpha}
) basis to (
C,\tilde{C}
) basis in (
173
) preserves the Poisson brackets (or canonical commutation relations), the transformation is a
Bogoliubov transformation
on the string worldsheet. This Bogoliubov transformation will play a crucial role in quantum aspects, especially the vacuum structure, of tensionless string theory, as we will see in later sections.

6
Bosonic open null strings

We now move our attention to open null strings constructed very recently in
22
. For the intrinsic analysis, we begin with the same ILST action constructed in (
137
). Understandably, for closed null strings boundary terms were not an issue, but for open strings, one has to be careful about boundary contributions when considering variation of the action. In general the vanishing condition for the boundary term reads:

\int_{\partial B}d\tau\,n_{\alpha}V^{\alpha}V^{\beta}(\partial_{\beta}X^{\nu})\eta_{\mu\nu}\,\delta X^{\mu}=0.

(174)

Here
\partial B
is the boundary of the worldsheet located at
\sigma=0,\pi
, whose normal direction is
n_{\alpha}
, see (
3
) for a visual description.
The above boundary term vanishes under the following boundary conditions:

\displaystyle\delta X^{\mu}\big|_{\sigma=0,\pi}

\displaystyle=0\qquad\text{Dirichlet,}

(175a)

\displaystyle V^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}X^{\nu}\big|_{\sigma=0,\pi}

\displaystyle=0\qquad\text{Neumann,}

(175b)

\displaystyle n_{\alpha}V^{\alpha}\big|_{\sigma=0,\pi}

\displaystyle=0\qquad\text{Null.}

(175c)

The first two conditions correspond, respectively to the usual Dirichlet and Neumann boundary conditions familiar from the tensile open string. The third condition (
175c
), however, is a new possibility that emerge only in the null string limit. In this review, however, we will focus on the Dirichlet boundary condition. The Neumann and, in particular, the null boundary conditions are intriguing and require further investigation.

Mode expansion and residual symmetry:

To analyse the residual worldsheet symmetry algebra for open bosonic null strings, we take a sightly different approach compared to the closed null strings. We recall the most general solution to the gauge fixed EOM (
143
) is

\begin{split}X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=x^{\mu}+&\sqrt{\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}}(C^{\mu}_{0}-\tilde{C}^{\mu}_{0})\sigma+\sqrt{\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}}(C^{\mu}_{0}+\tilde{C}^{\mu}_{0})\tau\\
&+i\sqrt{\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\left[(C^{\mu}_{n}-\tilde{C}^{\mu}_{-n})-in\tau(C^{\mu}_{n}+\tilde{C}^{\mu}_{-n})\right]e^{-in\sigma}.\end{split}

(176)

We now apply the Dirichlet boundary condition at
\sigma=0
and
\pi
, which requires

C^{\mu}_{n}=-\tilde{C}^{\mu}_{n}.

(177)

This results in the following mode expansion for the null open string,

\displaystyle X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=x_{0}^{\mu}+\sqrt{2c^{\prime}}

\displaystyle C^{\mu}_{0}\sigma+i\sqrt{\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\big[(C^{\mu}_{n}+C^{\mu}_{-n})-in\tau(C^{\mu}_{n}-C^{\mu}_{-n})\big]e^{-in\sigma}.

(178)

Here
(C_{n}^{\mu})^{\dagger}=C_{-n}^{\mu}
ensures that the modes are real. To determine the algebra satisfied by the generators of constraints, we first compute
\dot{X}^{\mu},X^{\prime\mu}
from (
178
) and substitute them into the constraint equations (
144
). This results in

\displaystyle\dot{X}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m}\mathcal{P}_{m}e^{-im\sigma}=0,

(179a)

\displaystyle\dot{X}\cdot{X}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m}\big(\mathcal{O}_{m}-im\tau\mathcal{P}_{m}\big)e^{-im\sigma}=0\,,

(179b)

where the generators
(\mathcal{O}_{n},\mathcal{P}_{m}
) are defined as follows

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{n}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m}\frac{1}{2}(C_{m}^{\mu}-C_{-m}^{\mu})(C_{n-m}^{\nu}+C_{m-n}^{\nu})\eta_{\mu\nu},

(180a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{m}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n}\frac{1}{4}(C_{n}^{\mu}-C_{-n}^{\mu})(C_{m-n}^{\nu}-C_{n-m}^{\nu})\eta_{\mu\nu}.

(180b)

Using the Poisson brackets between
(C,\tilde{C})
oscillators, given in (
160
), it is straightforward to verify that the generators of the constraints satisfy

\displaystyle i\left\{\mathcal{O}_{n},\,\mathcal{O}_{m}\right\}

\displaystyle=(n-m)\mathcal{O}_{n+m}-(n+m)\mathcal{O}_{n-m},

(181a)

\displaystyle i\left\{\mathcal{O}_{n},\,\mathcal{P}_{m}\right\}

\displaystyle=(n-m)\mathcal{P}_{n+m}+(n+m)\mathcal{P}_{n-m},

(181b)

\displaystyle i\left\{\mathcal{P}_{n},\,\mathcal{P}_{m}\right\}

\displaystyle=0.

(181c)

The above algebra (in commutator form) corresponds to the classical part of the Boundary Conformal Carrollian Algebra (BCCA) (
132
) shown earlier from the field theoretic arguments.

Limiting analysis:

As expected, the intrinsic formulation of the open null string can be recovered from the tensile theory by taking an ultra-relativistic limit on the tensile open string theory. We recall the standard mode expansion of the tensile open string,

\displaystyle X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=x^{\mu}_{0}+\sqrt{2\alpha^{\prime}}\alpha_{0}^{\mu}\sigma+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\Big[\alpha^{\mu}_{n}e^{-in(\tau+\sigma)}+\alpha^{\mu}_{-n}e^{in(\tau-\sigma)}\Big],

(182)

and perform the same ultra-relativistic scaling as we did for the closed case,

\tau\to\epsilon\tau\qquad\sigma\to\sigma\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha^{\prime}\to c^{\prime}/\epsilon\qquad\epsilon\to 0\,.

(183)

Expanding (
182
) to first order in
\epsilon
and comparing with (
178
), we obtain the following Bogoliubov transformation

C^{\mu}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\sqrt{\epsilon}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\Big)\,\alpha^{\mu}_{n}-\frac{1}{2}\Big(\sqrt{\epsilon}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\Big)\,{\alpha}^{\mu}_{-n}.

(184)

We recall the expression of single copy of Virasoro generators (in a different basis)
14
14

14

In this review, for open tensile string theory, we use a different basis for the generators of the constraints. Dirichlet and Neumann boundary conditions identify (
\alpha
and
\tilde{\alpha}
) oscillators resulting in only single copy of Virasoro generators.
given in appendix (
495
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}\alpha_{m}\cdot\alpha_{n-m}.

(185)

Using (
184
) in (
180
) and applying the above expression of single copy of Virasoro generator we obtain the following ultra-relativistic contraction

\mathcal{O}_{n}=\mathbb{L}_{n}-\mathbb{L}_{-n},\qquad\mathcal{P}_{n}=\epsilon(\mathbb{L}_{n}+\mathbb{L}_{-n}).

(186)

Using the above Inönü-Wigner contraction on a single Virasoro, we can obtain the BCCA for bosonic open null strings. This concludes our discussion on bosonic null strings, where we heavily relied on the ILST formalism.

7
Closed null superstrings

This section covers the supersymmetric versions of closed null strings. The discussion in this section is mainly based on works
21
;
8
. Earlier studies on the supersymmetric extensions of the BMS
3
algebra showed that there could be two possibilities, namely the
homogeneous
and
inhomogeneous
version of the algebra. The earliest mention of homogeneous super-BMS
3
algebra, albeit in a different guise, can be traced back to
94
, the inhomogeneous super-BMS
3
algebra was proposed in
8

15
15

15

The Galilean (isomorphic) version of super BMS
3
was first constructed in
138
.
. In what follows we will review the classical aspects of both homogeneous and inhomogeneous null superstrings which can also be understood directly from judiciously taken tensionless limit of tensile RNS superstrings.

7.1
Homogeneous null superstring

We begin this section by discussing how to appropriately introduce fermions on the null worldsheet. We follow the formalism of supersymmetrisation of bosonic theory in tensile RNS superstring case and introduce
\psi^{\mu}
as the fermionic partners
16
16

16

To be precise
\psi^{\mu}
is fermionic density on the worldsheet with weight
-\frac{1}{4}
.
of bosonic field
X^{\mu}
and
\chi
as the fermionic partner of the vector density
V^{\alpha}
in the ILST action. This
\chi
is related to a field analogous to gravitino
\chi_{\alpha}
through the relation
\chi=V^{\alpha}\chi_{\alpha}
. In case of the tensile superstring, the fermionic field
\psi^{\mu}
is a massless Majorana fermion, and the corresponding worldsheet action takes the form of a massless Dirac action written using a Majorana representation of the two-dimensional gamma matrices. In the present case, the purely fermionic sector of the action retains the Dirac-like structure, with the gamma matrices now satisfying a modified Clifford algebra appropriate for the Carrollian (degenerate) worldsheet geometry
17
17

17

See
5
for a detailed analysis of Carroll Clifford algebras in general dimensions. We will see that the representation of gamma matrices for homogenous and inhomogeneous null superstring are different.
. The action thus obtained for null superstring following
132

S=\int d^{2}\xi\penalty\ \penalty\ \left[\left(V^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}X^{\mu}+i\chi\psi^{\mu}\right)\cdot\left(V^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}X_{\mu}+i\chi\bar{\psi}_{\mu}\right)+i\bar{\psi}^{\mu}\rho^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\psi_{\mu}\right].

(187)

The above action displays diffeomorphism invariance as well as supersymmetry on the worldsheet. Variation of the fields under the worldsheet diffeomorphism (
\sigma^{\alpha}\to\sigma^{\alpha}+\xi^{\alpha}
) is given below

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}X^{\mu}=-\xi^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}X^{\mu},\qquad\delta_{\xi}V^{\alpha}=-V^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}\xi^{\alpha}+\xi^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}V^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\partial_{\beta}\xi^{\beta}\right)V^{\alpha},

(188a)

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}\psi^{\mu}=-\xi^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}\psi^{\mu},\qquad\penalty\ \hskip 1.13809pt\delta_{\xi}\chi=-\xi^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\chi+\frac{1}{4}\left(\partial_{\alpha}\xi^{\alpha}\right)\chi.

(188b)

Under supersymmetry transformation parametrized by
\varepsilon
, the fields vary as followed

\displaystyle\delta_{\varepsilon}X^{\mu}

\displaystyle=i\varepsilon\psi^{\mu},\qquad\delta_{\varepsilon}\psi^{\mu}=-\varepsilon V^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}X^{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}i\varepsilon\left(\psi^{\mu}\chi\right),

(189a)

\displaystyle\delta_{\varepsilon}V^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=iV^{\alpha}\left(\varepsilon\chi\right),\qquad\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \delta_{\varepsilon}\chi=V^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\varepsilon.

(189b)

Just like its bosonic counterpart, the worldsheet diffeomorphism invariance necessitates gauge fixing, which is the null equivalent of the conformal gauge for the tensile superstring:

V^{\alpha}=(1,0),\quad\quad\chi=0.

(190)

The gamma matrices on the worldsheet of tensile superstrings belong to the Majorana representation of the relativistic Clifford algebra with
\eta^{\alpha\beta}
as the gauge fixed worldsheet inverse metric

\displaystyle\{\rho^{\alpha},\rho^{\beta}\}=2\eta^{\alpha\beta}\mathbb{1}.

(191)

However, since on the null worldsheet the metric is degenerate, in order to study fermions in such worldsheet, one needs a modified version of Clifford algebra. In (
191
), replacing
\eta^{ab}
with a suitable combination of the gauged fixed vector densities
V^{\alpha}=(1,0)
, one can formulate a modified Clifford algebra on the null worldsheet

\displaystyle\{\rho^{\alpha},\rho^{\beta}\}=2V^{\alpha}V^{\beta}\mathbb{1},\qquad V^{\alpha}=(1,0).

(192)

The most obvious representation of (
192
) is a simple choice
\rho^{\alpha}=V^{\alpha}\mathbb{1}
, i.e., with the gauge choice
V^{\alpha}=(1,0)
we have the following gamma matrices

\displaystyle\rho^{0}=\mathbb{1},\qquad\rho^{1}=\mathbb{O}.

(193)

With these gauge choices and the simple representation of the Clifford matrices, the action (
187
) reduces to the following

S=\int d^{2}\xi\left[\dot{X}^{2}+i\psi\cdot\dot{\psi}\right].

(194)

Just like its bosonic counterpart, the null superstring gauge fixed action also has residual gauge symmetries. Applying the gauge choices in (
188
) and (
189
) one finds that the action (
194
) is invariant under the following worldsheet gauge transformation parametrized by
\xi
and
\varepsilon

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}X

\displaystyle=\xi^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}X,\qquad\delta_{\xi}\psi=\xi^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\psi+\frac{1}{4}(\partial_{\alpha}\xi^{\alpha})\psi,

(195a)

\displaystyle\delta_{\epsilon}X

\displaystyle=\bar{\varepsilon}\psi,\qquad\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \hskip 1.99168pt\delta_{\varepsilon}\psi^{\mu}=-i\rho^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}X\varepsilon.

(195b)

In (
195
), the parameters
\xi
and
\varepsilon
follow certain restrictions which is found by applying the gauge (
190
) on (
188a
) and (
189b
)

\dot{\xi^{0}}=\left(\xi^{1}\right)^{\prime},\qquad\dot{\xi^{1}}=0,\qquad\dot{\varepsilon^{\alpha}}=0.

(196)

Solving the above differential equations one finds how the diffeomorphism and supersymmetry transformations are restricted due to gauge fixing

\xi^{0}=f^{\prime}(\sigma)\tau+g(\sigma),\qquad\xi^{1}=f(\sigma),\qquad\varepsilon^{\pm}=\varepsilon^{\pm}(\sigma).

(197)

As demonstrated in
18
, the generators of this transformation in superspace satisfy
18
18

18

Details of superspace formalism of homogenous null superstrings are presented in Appendix (
D.2
)

\begin{split}&[L_{n},L_{m}]=(n-m)L_{n+m},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ [L_{n},M_{m}]=(n-m)M_{n+m},\\
&[L_{n},Q^{\alpha}_{r}]=\Big(\frac{n}{2}-r\Big)Q^{\alpha}_{n+r},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \{Q^{\alpha}_{r},Q^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{s}\}=\delta^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}M_{r+s},\\
&[M_{m},M_{n}]=[M_{m},Q^{\alpha}_{r}]=0,\end{split}

(198)

which forms the homogeneous super‑Conformal Carrollian algebra (SCCA
H
), without the central extension. The centrally extended algebra is given in Appendix (
C.1
).

We now turn to deriving the above symmetry algebra using mode expansions. After gauge fixing, the EOM and the constraints obtained from (
187
) simplify considerably and take the following form

\displaystyle\ddot{X}^{\mu}=0,\qquad\dot{\psi}^{\mu}=0,

(199a)

\displaystyle\dot{X}^{2}=0,\qquad\dot{X}\cdot X^{\prime}+\frac{i}{2}\bar{\psi}\cdot\psi^{\prime}=0,\qquad\psi\cdot\dot{X}=0.

(199b)

Now, let us look into the solutions of the EOM (
199
) in the NS-NS sector. The Fourier expansion of the bosonic field solution remains same as (
150
). The mode expansion for solutions for the equation
\dot{\psi}^{\mu}=0
is given by

\displaystyle\psi^{\mu}_{+}(\tau,\sigma)=\sqrt{c^{\prime}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\beta^{\penalty\ \mu+}_{r}e^{-ir\sigma},\qquad\psi^{\mu}_{-}(\tau,\sigma)=\sqrt{c^{\prime}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\beta^{\penalty\ \mu-}_{r}e^{-ir\sigma}.

(200)

The fermionic fields satisfy the Poisson brackets,

\displaystyle\quad\{\psi^{\mu}_{\alpha}(\sigma),\psi^{\nu}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(\sigma^{\prime})\}=\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta(\sigma-\sigma^{\prime}).

(201)

This leads to the following anticommutation relation between the fermionic modes
\beta^{\pm}

\displaystyle\{\beta_{r}^{\mu\alpha},\beta_{s}^{\nu\alpha^{\prime}}\}=\delta^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\delta_{r+s}\eta^{\mu\nu}.

(202)

Using the mode expansions of bosonic fields in (
150
) and fermionic fields in (
200
) we determine the mode expansion of the constraints as

\displaystyle\dot{X}^{2}

\displaystyle=4c^{\prime}\sum_{n}M_{n}e^{-in\sigma},

(203a)

\displaystyle\dot{X}\cdot X^{\prime}+\frac{i}{2}\bar{\psi}\cdot\psi^{\prime}

\displaystyle=4c^{\prime}\sum_{n}\Big[L_{n}-in\tau M_{n}\Big]e^{-in\sigma},

(203b)

\displaystyle\psi_{\pm}\cdot\dot{X}

\displaystyle=4c^{\prime}\sum_{r}Q^{\pm}_{r}e^{-ir\sigma}.

(203c)

In (
203
), the expressions of the symmetry generators
L_{n}
,
M_{n}
and
Q^{\pm}
in terms of modes are given by

\displaystyle L_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}A_{-m}\cdot B_{m+n}+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{r}(2r+n)\Big(\beta^{+}_{-r}\cdot\beta^{+}_{r+n}+\beta^{-}_{-r}\cdot\beta^{-}_{r+n}\Big),

(204a)

\displaystyle M_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}B_{-m}\cdot B_{m+n},

(204b)

\displaystyle Q^{\pm}_{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}B_{-m}\cdot\beta^{\pm}_{m+r}.

(204c)

Using the Poisson brackets (
157
) and (
201
) one can find the Poisson brackets satisfied by
L_{n}
,
M_{n}
and
Q^{\pm}
. The Poisson brackets correspond to the residual symmetry algebra in (
198
).

Limiting approach:

We have formulated homogenous null superstring from first principles so far. We now derive the same results by taking appropriate limit on the tensile RNS superstrings. We already know how the bosonic fields behave in the tensionless limit. Looking at the tensile mode expansion (
507
) of the worldsheet fermions one can see that for homogeneous case, at tensionless limit (
\alpha^{\prime}\rightarrow\frac{c^{\prime}}{\epsilon},\penalty\ \epsilon\to 0
) there should be an overall scaling in the fermions in order to avoid divergence and the scaling is
\psi_{\text{tensionless}}=\sqrt{\epsilon}\,\psi_{\text{tensile}}
. Let us recall the the mode expansion for tensile RNS superstring

\displaystyle\psi^{\mu}_{+}(\tau,\sigma)=\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}b^{\mu}_{r}e^{-ir(\tau+\sigma)},\quad\psi^{\mu}_{-}(\tau,\sigma)=\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\tilde{b}^{\mu}_{r}e^{-ir(\tau-\sigma)}.

(205)

Under Carroll limit this mode expansion evolves into (
200
) in the following way

\displaystyle\psi_{+}^{\mu}(\sigma,\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\epsilon}\sqrt{2\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{r}b^{\mu}_{r}e^{-ir\sigma}(1-i\epsilon r\tau)\approx\sqrt{2c^{\prime}}\sum_{r}b^{\mu}_{r}e^{-ir\sigma}

(206a)

\displaystyle\psi_{-}^{\mu}(\sigma,\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\epsilon}\sqrt{2\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{r}\tilde{b}^{\mu}_{r}e^{+ir\sigma}(1-i\epsilon r\tau)\approx\sqrt{2c^{\prime}}\sum_{r}\tilde{b}^{\mu}_{-r}e^{-ir\sigma}.

(206b)

In the above, the tensionless fermionic modes are connected to the tensile fermionic modes in the following way

\displaystyle\beta^{\mu+}_{r}=b^{\mu}_{r},\qquad\beta^{\mu-}_{r}={\tilde{b}}^{\mu}_{-r}.

(207)

We recall that in tensile RNS superstrings, (reviewed in Appendix
496
), the energy-momentum tensor components and the supercurrents of the tensile superstrings lead us to the following expressions of the super-Virasoro generators in terms of oscillator modes (see (
509
))

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}\alpha_{-m}\cdot\alpha_{m+n}+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}(2r+n)b_{-r}\cdot b_{r+n},

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{r}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}\alpha_{-m}\cdot b_{r+m},

and similar expression for
\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
s and
\bar{\mathcal{Q}}_{r}
s in terms of the right-moving modes. Now, using (
207
) along with the relation between the tensile and tensionless bosonic modes as given in (
171
) into the tensile constraints in (
204
), one can check that the connection between tensionless SCCA
H
generators and super-Virasoro generators are connected through the Inönü-Wigner contraction identical to

\displaystyle L_{n}=\mathcal{L}_{n}-\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{-n},\quad M_{n}=\epsilon(\mathcal{L}_{n}+\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{-n}),\quad Q^{+}_{r}=\sqrt{\epsilon}\mathcal{Q}_{r},\quad Q^{-}_{r}=\sqrt{\epsilon}\bar{\mathcal{Q}}_{r}.

(208)

This confirms the consistency of the intrinsic analysis of homogenous null superstrings with the tensionless limit of tensile RNS superstrings.

7.2
Inhomogeneous null superstring

As discussed in
8
;
9
, there is another variant of null superstrings called the inhomogeneous null superstring. In order to formulate action of this theory one needs to recall the modified 2d Clifford algebra for the Carrollian case given in (
192
). One can see that the representation (
193
) is not unique, and other representations are possible keeping the nilpotency of one of the matrices. To this end, let us consider the following alternative

\rho^{0}=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{bmatrix},\qquad\rho^{1}=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}.

(209)

The inhomogeneous spinors corresponding to these gamma matrices are not necessarily real and as shown in
8
, the tensionless limit of RNS superstring will lead us to the null strings with complex spinors. Let us begin with the following complex spinors

\displaystyle\psi^{\mu}=\begin{bmatrix}\psi^{\mu}_{0}\\
\psi^{\mu}_{1}\end{bmatrix}.

(210)

One can construct a null fermionic string action by adding this fermion to the gauge fixed ILST action. The fermionic part of the action remain same as that of the massless Dirac action with (
209
) as gamma matrices . The final action we obtain is

S=\int d^{2}\sigma\left[\dot{X}^{2}+i(\psi_{0}^{*}\cdot\dot{\psi_{0}}+\psi_{1}^{*}\cdot\dot{\psi_{1}}-\psi_{1}^{*}\cdot\psi_{0}^{\prime})\right].

(211)

As one can see, unlike the action (
194
), this action is asymmetric in its two spinor components. This action is invariant under the following worldsheet gauge transformations

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}X\hskip 1.42262pt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\xi^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}X,\qquad\delta_{\xi}\psi_{0}=\xi^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\psi_{0}+\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\alpha}\xi^{\alpha}\psi_{0},

(212a)

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}\psi_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\xi^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\psi_{1}\penalty\ +\penalty\ \frac{1}{4}\partial_{\alpha}\xi^{\alpha}\psi_{1}\penalty\ +\penalty\ \frac{1}{2}(\partial_{1}\xi^{0})\psi_{0}

(212b)

\displaystyle\delta_{\varepsilon}X\hskip 1.42262pt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i(\varepsilon^{1*}\psi_{0}+\varepsilon^{0*}\psi_{1}),\quad\delta_{\varepsilon}\psi_{0}=-\varepsilon^{1}\dot{X},\quad\delta_{\varepsilon}\psi_{1}=-\varepsilon^{0}\dot{X}-\varepsilon^{1}X^{\prime}.

(212c)

One can see that in (
212
), the two spinor components transform in two different ways. Their transformation comes from the transformation of the two-component fermion (
210
) under worldsheet diffeomorphism and Weyl transformation

\delta_{\xi}\psi=\xi^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\psi+\frac{1}{4}(\partial_{\alpha}\xi^{\alpha})\psi-\frac{1}{4}(\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}\xi_{\beta})\rho^{0}\rho^{1}\psi.

(213)

In the above
\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta}
is the antisymmetric tensor on the worldsheet. The parameters
\xi
and
\varepsilon
in the transformations (
212
) follow the following restrictions in order for the action (
211
) to be invariant

\displaystyle\partial_{0}\xi^{0}=\partial_{1}\xi^{1},\qquad\penalty\ \penalty\ \hskip 1.42262pt\partial_{0}\xi^{1}=0,

(214a)

\displaystyle\partial_{0}\varepsilon^{1*}=\partial_{0}\varepsilon^{0}=\partial_{1}\varepsilon^{1}=\partial_{1}\varepsilon^{0*},

(214b)

\displaystyle\partial_{0}\varepsilon^{0*}=\partial_{0}\varepsilon^{1}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \hskip 2.84526pt\psi_{0}=\psi_{1}^{*}.

(214c)

As shown in
8
, generators of these residual symmetries in superspace satisfy inhomogeneous Superconformal Carrollian Algebra (SCCA
I
)

\displaystyle[L_{m},L_{n}]=(m-n)L_{m+n},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ [L_{m},M_{n}]=(m-n)M_{m+n},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ [L_{m},G_{r}]=\Big(\frac{m}{2}-r\Big)G_{m+r},

\displaystyle[L_{m},H_{r}]=\Big(\frac{m}{2}-r\Big)H_{m+r},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ [M_{m},G_{r}]=\Big(\frac{m}{2}-r\Big)H_{m+r},\,

\displaystyle\{G_{r},G_{s}\}=2L_{r+s},\qquad\{G_{r},H_{s}\}=2M_{r+s}.

(215)

The centrally extended SCCA
I
is given in (
C.1
). We now turn to obtain the same algebra using different approach. The EOM corresponding to the action (
211
) are given by

\ddot{X}^{\mu}=0,\qquad\dot{\psi}_{0}^{\mu}=0,\qquad\dot{\psi}_{1}^{\mu}={\psi^{\prime}}_{0}^{\mu}.

(216)

Solution for
X^{\mu}
remains same as (
150
). In NS-NS sector, solution of (
216
) can be mode expanded as

\displaystyle\psi_{0}^{\mu}(\sigma,\tau)=\sqrt{c^{\prime}}\sum_{r}\beta^{\mu}_{r}e^{-ir\sigma},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \psi_{1}^{\mu}(\sigma,\tau)=\sqrt{c^{\prime}}\sum_{r}[\gamma^{\mu}_{r}-ir\tau\beta^{\mu}_{r}]e^{-ir\sigma}.

(217)

The fermionic Poisson brackets in (
201
) essentially lead us to the following non-vanishing anticommutators between the fermionic modes

\{\gamma_{r}^{\mu},\beta_{s}^{\nu}\}=2\delta_{r+s}\eta^{\mu\nu}.

(218)

The constraints of this theory, which can be obtained from the energy momentum tensor components and the supercurrents of the action (
211
), are given by

\displaystyle\dot{X}\cdot X^{\prime}+\frac{i}{4}

\displaystyle\Big[\psi_{0}^{\prime}\cdot\psi_{1}+\psi^{\prime}_{0}\cdot\psi_{1}\Big]=0,\qquad\dot{X}^{2}+\frac{i}{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\cdot\psi_{0}=0

(219)

\displaystyle\psi_{0}\cdot X^{\prime}+\psi_{1}\cdot\dot{X}=0,\qquad\psi_{0}\cdot\dot{X}=0.

Using the mode expansion (
150
) and (
216
) one finds

\displaystyle\dot{X}\cdot X^{\prime}+\frac{i}{4}\Big[\psi_{0}^{\prime}\cdot\psi_{1}+\psi^{\prime}_{0}\cdot\psi_{1}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4c^{\prime}\sum_{n}\Big[L_{n}-in\tau M_{n}\Big]e^{-in\sigma},

(220a)

\displaystyle\dot{X}^{2}+\frac{i}{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\cdot\psi_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4c^{\prime}\sum_{n}M_{n}e^{-in\sigma},

(220b)

\displaystyle\psi_{0}\cdot X^{\prime}+\psi_{1}\cdot\dot{X}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4c^{\prime}\sum_{r}\Big[G_{r}-ir\tau H_{r}\Big]e^{-ir\sigma},

(220c)

\displaystyle\psi_{0}\cdot\dot{X}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4c^{\prime}\sum_{r}H_{r}e^{-ir\sigma},

(220d)

where
L_{n}
,
M_{n}
,
G_{r}
and
H_{r}
are given by

\displaystyle L_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}A_{-m}\cdot B_{m+n}+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{r}(2r+n)\Big(\beta_{-r}\cdot\gamma_{r+n}+\gamma_{-r}\cdot\beta_{r+n}\Big),

(221a)

\displaystyle M_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}B_{-m}\cdot B_{m+n}+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{r}(2r+n)\beta_{-r}\cdot\beta_{r+n},

(221b)

\displaystyle G_{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}(A_{-m}\cdot\beta_{m+r}+B_{-m}\cdot\gamma_{m+r}),

(221c)

\displaystyle H_{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}(B_{-m}\cdot\beta_{m+r}).

(221d)

Using the algebra between the modes
A
,
B
,
\beta
and
\gamma
, one can find that these generators satisfy the SCCA
I
given in (
7.2
).

Limiting approach:

Let us now see the same theory emerge from a limit of tensile superstrings. Looking into the gauge fixed action of tensile superstrings from
(
496
) one can see that as long as
\psi_{0}=\psi_{1}^{*}
(see (
214
)), the limit on the fermionic side which will take us from RNS superstring to the inhomogeneous tensionless superstring action (
211
) is
\psi_{0}\to\epsilon\psi_{0},\penalty\ \psi_{1}\to\psi_{1}
, where

\displaystyle\psi_{0}=\frac{\psi_{+}+i\psi_{-}}{\sqrt{2}},\quad\psi_{1}=\frac{\psi_{+}-i\psi_{-}}{\sqrt{2}}.

(222)

The fermionic mode expansion for tensile string (see (
507
)) scale at tensionless limit as below

\displaystyle\psi_{0}^{\mu}(\sigma,\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon}{\sqrt{2}}\Big(\psi^{\mu}_{+}+i\psi^{\mu}_{-}\Big)=\sqrt{c^{\prime}}\sum_{r}\Big[\sqrt{\epsilon}(b^{\mu}_{r}+i\tilde{b}^{\mu}_{-r})+\epsilon^{3/2}(\ldots)\Big]e^{-ir\sigma},

(223a)

\displaystyle\psi_{1}^{\mu}(\sigma,\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Big(\psi_{+}^{\mu}-i\psi_{-}^{\mu}\Big)=\sqrt{c^{\prime}}\sum_{r}\Big[\frac{b^{\mu}_{r}-i\tilde{b}^{\mu}_{-r}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}-ir\tau\sqrt{\epsilon}(b^{\mu}_{r}+i\tilde{b}^{\mu}_{-r})\Big]e^{-ir\sigma}.

(223b)

From here one can easily deduce how the fermionic modes are being scaled at tensionless limit

\displaystyle\gamma^{\mu}_{r}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\Big(b^{\mu}_{r}-i{\tilde{b}}^{\mu}_{-r}\Big),\quad\beta^{\mu}_{r}=\sqrt{\epsilon}(b^{\mu}_{r}+i{\tilde{b}}^{\mu}_{-r}).

(224)

Using (
224
) along with (
171
) on (
221
) one can find that the tensionless constraint modes in (
221
) are connected to the tensile super-Virasoro generators outlined in (
509
) through the same Inönü-Wigner contraction connecting homogeneous Superconformal Carrollian generators to the super-Virasoro generators given in (
C.1
).

Real fermion from exotic tensile parents:

In continuation of the previous discussion, let us mention another intriguing result on tensionless superstrings in brief. In
9
, it was shown that there could be two cousins of tensile string theory which at tensionless limit reduce to inhomogeneous null strings with real fermions. One of them had a gauge fixed action identical to the usual tensile superstring action (see (
496
) for example), with a slight twist: one of the fermion components
\psi_{-}
is anti-hermitian. Making this choice for
\psi_{-}
does not affect the hermiticity of the action. And in fact from the mode expansion one could show this anti-hermiticity of
\psi_{-}
seamlessly translates into the hermiticity properties of inhomogeneous tensionless oscillators that appear in mode expansion of
\psi_{0}
and
\psi_{1}
i.e. we could have
\gamma^{\dagger}_{-r}=-\gamma_{r}
and
b^{\dagger}_{-r}=-b_{r}
.

Apart for this twisted superstring,
9
also discussed another version of tensile superstring theory which reduces to inhomogeneous null superstrings in tensionless limit. This theory can be written as a supersymmetric extension of tensile twisted string theory
68
;
129
which is constructed on the flipped representation of Virasoro algebra (see around (
119
) for a discussion). Note that just like Virasoro algebra, the super-Virasoro algebra too, allows the same automorphism (
119
), and the parent theory consequently leads to twisted tensile superstring theory. The consequences of this parent theory are very interesting, but we will not go into that for brevity.

8
Open null superstrings

In this section, we review the open null superstring theory satisfying Dirichlet boundary conditions at both the endpoints of the string. This analysis is based on the recent work
19
. Here we only cover the homogeneous null open superstrings. The open version of inhomogeneous null superstrings is yet to be formulated.

We begin with the boundary term of (
187
). Its variation is given by

\begin{split}\delta S_{bdy}=-\int d^{2}\sigma\,\Big[\,\partial_{\alpha}\,&\left(V^{\alpha}\,V^{\beta}\,\partial_{\beta}X_{\mu}\delta X^{\mu}+iV^{\alpha}V^{\beta}\chi_{\beta}\psi_{\mu}\delta X^{\mu}\right)-i\partial_{\alpha}\left(\bar{\psi}^{\mu}\rho^{\alpha}\delta\psi_{\mu}\right)\Big].\end{split}

(225)

The variation of the bosonic part of (
225
) was discussed in Sec.
6
. The fermionic part can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\int d^{2}\sigma\,\partial_{\alpha}\left(\bar{\psi}^{\mu}\rho^{\alpha}\,\delta\psi_{\mu}\right)=\oint_{c}dl\penalty\ (\,\bar{\psi}^{\mu}\,n_{\alpha}\rho^{\alpha}\,\delta\psi_{\mu}\,).

(226)

This term can be put to zero by imposing a fermionic analogue of null boundary condition in (
175c
)

\displaystyle n_{\alpha}\rho^{\alpha}=0.

(227)

Like the bosonic string, we will here focus on Dirichlet boundary conditions. The gauge choice remains same as (
190
), along with gamma matrices identical to (
193
). Consequently, residual symmetry transformation in (
195
), EOM and constraints in (
199
) remain unchanged. (
190
) and (
193
) imply

\displaystyle\oint_{c}dl\penalty\ n_{a}(\,\bar{\psi}\,\rho^{a}\,\delta\psi\,)=\int d\sigma\,(\psi_{+}\,\delta\psi_{+}+\,\psi_{-}\,\delta\psi_{-})\Big|_{\tau=-\infty}^{\tau=\infty}=0.

(228)

Since this boundary term trivially vanishes, we cannot obtain any boundary condition from it. However, one can still impose boundary conditions on the fermions consistent with the gauge‑fixed supersymmetry transformations (
195
):

\delta_{\varepsilon}X\Big|_{\sigma=0,\pi}=0,\qquad\implies\varepsilon^{+}\,\psi_{+}=-\,\varepsilon^{-}\,\psi_{-}.

(229)

It is important to keep in mind that like tensile open superstring, here too, existence of boundary in the worldsheet breaks half of the worldsheet supersymmetry. Following the steps outlined in (
B.2
), one can find the Neveu-Schwarz sector of homogeneous null open superstrings satisfying the Dirichlet boundary condition

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{+}(0)\,=\varepsilon^{-}(0)\,\implies\psi_{+}(0)=-\,\psi_{-}(0),

(230a)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{+}(\pi)=-\varepsilon^{-}(\pi)\,\implies\psi_{+}(\pi)=\psi_{-}(\pi).

(230b)

Applying the constraints (
230
) to the fermionic mode expansion in (
200
) we find

\displaystyle\beta^{\penalty\ \mu+}_{r}=-\beta^{\penalty\ \mu-}_{-r}\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall r.

(231)

The above identification in (
200
) results into the mode expansions of
\psi_{\pm}
taking the following form

\displaystyle\psi^{\mu}_{+}(\tau,\sigma)=

\displaystyle\sqrt{c^{\prime}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\beta^{\penalty\ \mu+}_{r}e^{-ir\sigma},\quad\psi^{\mu}_{-}(\tau,\sigma)=-\sqrt{c^{\prime}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\beta^{\penalty\ \mu+}_{-r}e^{-ir\sigma}.

(232)

Using the fermionic Poisson brackets we arrive at the following anticommutators between the fermionic modes,

\{\beta_{r}^{\mu+},\beta_{s}^{\nu+}\}=\delta_{r+s}\eta^{\mu\nu}.

(233)

For bosonic field
X^{\mu}
, the identification between
C
and
\tilde{C}
, open string mode expansion, and the Poisson bracket remain same as (
177
), (
178
), and (
181
) respectively. Substituting the mode expansions of the null open superstring in (
178
) and (
232
) to the supersymmetric constraint equations in (
199b
) we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,\dot{X}^{2}

\displaystyle=c^{\prime}\sum_{p}\mathcal{P}_{p}e^{-ip\sigma}

(234a)

\displaystyle\dot{X}\cdot X^{\prime}+\frac{i}{2}\,\bar{\psi}\cdot\psi^{\prime}

\displaystyle=c^{\prime}\sum_{p}\left(\mathcal{O}_{p}-i\,p\,\tau\mathcal{P}_{p}\right)e^{-ip\sigma}

(234b)

\displaystyle\psi_{+}\cdot\dot{X}=\psi_{-}\cdot\dot{X}

\displaystyle=c^{\prime}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{H}_{r}e^{-ir\sigma}.

(234c)

In the above, the generators
\mathcal{O}_{p}
,
\mathcal{P}_{p}
and
\mathcal{H}_{r}
in terms of the bosonic modes
C
and fermionic modes
\beta
become

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{p}=\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n}(C_{n}^{\mu}-C_{-n}^{\mu})(C_{p-n}^{\nu}+C_{n-p}^{\nu})

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +\frac{1}{4}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\left[(2r+p)\,(\beta^{\mu+}_{-r}\beta^{\nu+}_{r+n})-(2r-p)\,(\beta^{\mu+}_{-r}\beta^{\nu+}_{r-n})\right]\eta_{\mu\nu}

(235a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{p}=\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\sum_{n}(C_{n}^{\mu}-C_{-n}^{\mu})(C_{p-n}^{\nu}-C_{n-p}^{\nu})\,\eta_{\mu\nu}

(235b)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{r}=\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n}(C_{n}^{\mu}-C_{-n}^{\mu})\,\beta^{\nu+}_{r+n}\eta_{\mu\nu}.

(235c)

The commutation and anticommutation relations in (
181
) and (
233
) can be used to show that the generators in (
235
) satisfy

\begin{split}&[\mathcal{O}_{n},\mathcal{O}_{m}]=(n-m)\mathcal{O}_{n+m}-(n+m)\mathcal{O}_{n-m},\\
&[\mathcal{O}_{n},\mathcal{P}_{m}]=(n-m)\mathcal{P}_{n+m}+(n+m)\mathcal{P}_{n-m},\\
&[\mathcal{O}_{r},\mathcal{H}_{s}]=\Big(\frac{r}{2}-s\Big)\mathcal{H}_{r+s}+\Big(\frac{r}{2}+s\Big)\mathcal{H}_{s-r},\\
&\{\mathcal{H}_{r},\mathcal{H}_{s}\}=\mathcal{P}_{r+s},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ [\mathcal{H}_{r},\mathcal{P}_{s}]=[\mathcal{P}_{r},\mathcal{P}_{s}]=0,\\
\end{split}

(236)

which forms the classical part of Homogeneous Boundary Superconformal Carrollian Algebra (BSCCA
H
). The centrally extended algebra is given in (
527
).

Limiting approach:

Now that we looked into the theory from intrinsic perspective, let us turn our attention to the homogenous Carroll limit on the results in the parent tensile theory and see whether they match with the corresponding results derived in the previous section. Limiting analysis of the bosonic field mode expansion remains identical to the same covered in section
6
. Let us begin by recalling mode expansion of the fermionic field
\psi^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)
on the tensile worldsheet from (
518b
)

\displaystyle\psi^{\mu}_{+}(\tau,\sigma)=\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}b^{\mu}_{r}e^{-ir(\tau+\sigma)},\quad\psi^{\mu}_{-}(\tau,\sigma)=-\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}b^{\mu}_{r}e^{-ir(\tau-\sigma)}.

(237)

Applying the limit
\tau\to\epsilon\tau,\penalty\ \penalty\ \alpha^{\prime}\to\frac{c^{\prime}}{\epsilon}\penalty\ \penalty\ \sigma\to\sigma,\penalty\ \penalty\ \epsilon\to 0
along with the homogeneous limit
\psi_{\text{tensionless}}=\sqrt{\epsilon}\,\psi_{\text{tensile}}
on this mode expansion takes us to the following

\displaystyle\psi^{\mu}_{+}(\tau,\sigma)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\epsilon\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}b^{\mu}_{r}e^{-ir\sigma}(1-i\epsilon r\tau)\approx\sqrt{c^{\prime}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}b^{\mu}_{r}e^{-ir\sigma}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)

(238a)

\displaystyle\psi^{\mu}_{-}(\tau,\sigma)

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\epsilon\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}b^{\mu}_{r}e^{ir\sigma}(1-i\epsilon r\tau)\approx-\sqrt{c^{\prime}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}b^{\mu}_{-r}e^{-ir\sigma}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)

(238b)

Comparing (
238
) with the fermionic mode expansion in (
232
), we make the following identification

\displaystyle\beta^{\mu+}_{r}=b^{\mu}_{r}.

(239)

We recall that in tensile string theory (briefly reviewed in Appendix
B.2
), expressions of super-Virasoro generators in terms of tensile oscillators as obtained from the tensile string energy momentum tensor components are given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}\alpha_{-m}\cdot\alpha_{m+n}+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}(2r+n)b_{-r}\cdot b_{r+n},

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{r}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}\alpha_{-m}\cdot b_{r+m}.

Substituting (
184
) and (
239
) into (
235
), we see that the generators
\mathcal{O}_{n}
,
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, and
\mathcal{H}_{r}
are connected to their tensile counterparts
\mathbb{L}_{n}
and
\mathbb{Q}_{r}
through the Inönü-Wigner contraction identical to
contraction (
528
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{n}=\mathbb{L}_{n}-\mathbb{L}_{-n},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ P_{n}=\epsilon\penalty\ (\mathbb{L}_{n}+\mathbb{L}_{-n}),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{H}_{r}=\sqrt{\epsilon}\mathbb{Q}_{r},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \epsilon\to 0.

(240)

From here one can see that the intrinsic analysis provides us with the results consistent with appropriate limit from tensile superstring theory.

Part III
Quantum Null Strings

We now begin our journey into the strange world of quantum null strings. Here, unlike the more straight-forward case of the classical null string, we encounter surprises at almost every step. We first see that upon careful canonical quantisation, not one, but three consistent theories emerge from the ILST action. We first account for the various novel features of the quantum null string before addressing the spectrum of each quantum theory.

Figure 6:
The menagerie of quantum null string theories

This part of our review is divided into sections as follows:

•

From one classical to three quantum theories
: This section examines the canonical quantisation of the classical null string given by by ILST action. The careful imposition of constraints on the worldsheet leads to three consistent quantum null strings. We describe how this comes about and then consider quantisation in the Light Cone coordinates. This helps us figure out the critical dimension of these three theories by considering the closure of the Lorentz algebra in the target spacetime.

•

Novelties of Quantum Null Strings
: The three quantum null strings come with a lot of surprising properties. In this section, we collect the most intriguing of these properties. We show how an open string arises from a closed string when the tension is dialled to zero and discuss null string complementarity, a rather unexpected observer dependent phenomenon of the emergence of the open string in which one can either end up with a D-instanton or a spacefilling D-25 brane from the closed string in the null limit. We show how perturbative closed string degrees of freedom condense in the tensionless limit to form the open string. We then look at Rindler physics on accelerated string worldsheets which is a natural setting for explaining some of these aparently bizairre features.

•

Physical Hilbert spaces for three vacua
: As mentioned above and also shown in Fig
6
, from a single classical theory, we arrive at three different quantum null string theories. These are built of separate vacua called the Induced, Flipped and Oscillator vacua. In this section, we consider in detail the spectra of these three theories.

•

Compactification: Circle and Torus
: This section studies all three quantum theories first in target spacetimes compactified on circle and then generalises the study to compactification on a
d
dimensional torus. While studying compactification on torus we also introduce a constant Kalb-Ramond background along the torus. We compare the results in these three theories with tensionless limit of the corresponding results in their tensile counterparts. This section concludes with a discussion on the role of T-duality (for circle compactification) and
O(d,d,\mathbb{Z})
duality (for toroidal compactification with background field) in null strings.

9
From one classical to three quantum theories

The classical theory of null strings that we have encountered in the previous part of our review is based on the ILST action. We saw that various quantities in the classical null string could be derived intrinsically from the ILST action, and could be equivalently derived in a Carroll limit of the corresponding tensile quantities.

We now attempt to consistently quantise the theory of null strings based on the ILST action. We will take recourse to the method of Old Covariant quantisation (OCQ). We will see that careful canonical quantisation from the ILST string leads to three different quantum null strings. In the second part of this section, we will consider Light Cone quantisation (LCQ) of these three theories and compute their critical dimensions. Interestingly these three theories admit different central charges for their residual symmetry algebra which has been demonstrated in the Appendix
G
.

A quick reminder of basics:

Our discussion of quantised tensionless bosonic string theory would be based on
7
;
29
. In order to quantise the tensionless theory we need to promote the dynamical variables
X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)
and their canonical momenta
\Pi^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)
to operators. We have seen that these fields were expressed in terms of tensionless oscillator modes (
A,B
) or alternatively (
C,\tilde{C}
). As a result of quantisation, these modes (
A,B
) or (
C,\tilde{C}
) would be promoted to operators. As in the tensile string, here too, equal time commutators between
X^{\mu}
and
\Pi^{\mu}
will be

\begin{split}\left[X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma),\Pi^{\nu}(\tau,\sigma^{\prime})\right]=i\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta(\sigma-\sigma^{\prime}).\end{split}

(241)

Consequently the Poisson brackets between the (
A,B
) as given in (
157
) will be replaced by the following commutator brackets:

\displaystyle[A^{\mu}_{m},A^{\nu}_{n}]=[B^{\mu}_{m},B^{\nu}_{n}]=0,\hskip 22.76219pt[A^{\mu}_{m},B^{\nu}_{n}]=2m\delta_{m+n}\eta^{\mu\nu}

(242)

Or, alternatively

\displaystyle[C^{\mu}_{m},C^{\nu}_{n}]=[\tilde{C}^{\mu}_{m},\tilde{C}^{\nu}_{n}]=m\delta_{m+n},\hskip 22.76219pt[C^{\mu}_{m},\tilde{C}^{\nu}_{n}]=0.

(243)

We note again that
(A,B)
are not harmonic oscillator like, while
(C,\tilde{C})
are.
After quantisation, the worldsheet residual symmetry algebra becomes the centrally extended BMS
3
algebra

\begin{split}[L_{m},L_{n}]&=(m-n)L_{m+n}+\frac{c_{L}}{12}m(m^{2}-1)\delta_{m+n,0},\\
[L_{m},M_{n}]&=(m-n)M_{m+n}+\frac{c_{M}}{12}m(m^{2}-1)\delta_{m+n,0},\\
[M_{m},M_{n}]&=0.\end{split}

(244)

As we will see, depending on the physical state conditions we are going to impose, there will be multiple versions of quantum tensionless string theory and the values of (
c_{L},c_{M}
) will be different for each of those theories.

9.1
Three quantum null string theories

Let us recall the constraints on
X^{\mu}
from our classical analysis as given in (
153
) which state that the components of energy momentum tensor
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
vanish. Along with
X^{\mu}
,
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
too become operators and hence, in quantum theory, the constraints have to be imposed on these operators. However, imposing the entire operator to be zero is too strong constraint to build a quantum theory. The most general constraint that could lead to sensible quantum theories is

\bra{phys^{\prime}}T_{1}\ket{phys}=\bra{phys^{\prime}}T_{2}\ket{phys}=0.

(245)

Rewriting (
245
) in terms of
L_{n}
and
M_{n}
one gets

\bra{phys^{\prime}}L_{n}\ket{phys}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \bra{phys^{\prime}}M_{n}\ket{phys}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall n\in\mathbb{Z}.

(246)

We call the above
sandwich conditions
. There are three distinct conditions on an operator
F_{n}
, which are consistent with the sandwich condition on
F_{n}
. They are listed below:

F_{n}\ket{phys}=0,\hskip 14.22636pt\forall n>0,

(247a)

F_{n}\ket{phys}=0,\hskip 14.22636pt\forall n\neq 0,

(247b)

F_{n}\ket{phys}\neq 0,\hskip 5.69054pt\text{but}\hskip 5.69054pt\bra{phys^{\prime}}F_{n}\ket{phys}=0,\hskip 14.22636pt\forall n.

(247c)

When we try to quantise a theory with BMS
3
symmetry, we need to build theories where conditions (
246
) are met. Hence, following (
247
), we can have nine possible conditions which are compatible with the sandwich conditions (
246
). They are listed below:

\displaystyle L_{n}\ket{phys}=0,(n>0),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \begin{cases}M_{m}\ket{phys}=&0,(m>0)\\
M_{m}\ket{phys}=&0,(m\neq 0)\\
M_{m}\ket{phys}=&0,(\forall\penalty\ m)\end{cases};

(248a)

\displaystyle L_{n}\ket{phys}=0,(n\neq 0),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \begin{cases}M_{m}\ket{phys}=&0,(m>0)\\
M_{m}\ket{phys}=&0,(m\neq 0)\\
M_{m}\ket{phys}=&0,(\forall\penalty\ m)\end{cases};

(248b)

\displaystyle L_{n}\ket{phys}=0,(\forall\penalty\ n),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \begin{cases}M_{m}\ket{phys}=&0,(m>0)\\
M_{m}\ket{phys}=&0,(m\neq 0)\\
M_{m}\ket{phys}=&0,(\forall\penalty\ m)\end{cases}.

(248c)

In
7
, all this conditions has been discussed in detail. It has been shown that out of the conditions listed above, only three are consistent with BMS
3
algebra. These three conditions lead us to three distinct quantum theories constructed on Flipped, Induced and Oscillator vacuum respectively. They are listed below:

\displaystyle\textit{Flipped:}\qquad\qquad\penalty\ L_{n}\ket{phys}=\penalty\ M_{n}\ket{phys}=0\quad\forall\penalty\ n>0,

(249a)

\displaystyle\textit{Induced:}\qquad\qquad L_{n}\ket{phys}\neq 0,\penalty\ M_{n}\ket{phys}=0\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \forall\penalty\ n\neq 0,

(249b)

\displaystyle\textit{Oscillator:}\qquad\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ L_{n}\ket{phys}\neq 0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ M_{n}\ket{phys}\neq 0\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \forall\penalty\ n\hskip 8.53581pt\text{(but \eqref{constraint} holds)}.

(249c)

The remaining possibilities listed in (
248
) turn out to be inconsistent. This stems from the fact that the constraints imposed by (
L_{n},M_{n}
) cannot be chosen independently; rather, they are tied together by underlying BMS structure. Among the various choices, however, there is one notable exception. The condition specified in (
248b
),

L_{n}\ket{phys}=0,\quad M_{n}\ket{phys}=0,\qquad\qquad(\forall n,m\neq 0)

(250)

corresponds to a special case, which may be interpreted as an induced vacuum with zero momentum. This choice is hence admissible. A systematic demonstration for the inconsistency of the remaining constraints can be found in
7
. For completeness, we explicitly demonstrate the inconsistency for one representative case in appendix
F
. In what follows, we determine the critical dimensions of the three theories listed in (
249
).

9.2
Lightcone quantisation and critical dimensions

We now review light-cone gauge quantisation of these three quantum theories which will help us to determine the critical dimension of them. Light-cone gauge quantisation for induced theory was originally addressed in
134
, while the same for flipped theory was first addressed in
94
. Recent work
29
rigorously performed light-cone quantisation for all three theories including the oscillator theory. Our discussion of light-cone quantisation is mainly based on
6
.

We begin by introducing the light-cone coordinates
X^{\pm}
as

\displaystyle X^{\pm}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big(X^{0}\pm X^{D-1}\big).

(251)

In these coordinates, the Minkowski metric becomes

\displaystyle\eta_{+-}=-1,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \eta_{ij}=\delta_{ij}.

(252)

We apply the light-cone gauge on
X^{+}
in the same way it is applied for tensile string

\displaystyle X^{+}=x^{+}+c^{\prime}k^{+}\tau.

(253)

Applying the gauge (
253
) into the mode expansion (
150
) implies that

\displaystyle A^{+}_{n}=B^{+}_{n}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \forall n\neq 0.

(254)
