Applying them on the constraints (
144
) and using the mode expansion of
X
in (
150
) one gets the following

A^{-}_{m}=\frac{1}{B_{0}^{+}}\sum_{n\neq 0}:A^{i}_{n}B^{i}_{m-n}:\penalty\ ,\quad B^{-}_{m}=\frac{1}{2B_{0}^{+}}\sum_{n}:B^{i}_{n}B^{i}_{m-n}:\penalty\ .

(255)

In the above “: :” denotes normal ordering. Clearly the modes
A^{-}_{m}
and
B^{-}_{m}
are dependent on the modes in the transverse directions. They also satisfy the following algebra called the central algebra

\displaystyle\left[A^{-}_{m},A^{-}_{n}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{2(m-n)}{B^{+}_{0}}A^{-}_{m+n}+\frac{\tilde{c}_{L}}{12}(m^{3}-m)\delta_{m+n,0},

(256a)

\displaystyle\left[A^{-}_{m},B^{-}_{n}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{2(m-n)}{B^{+}_{0}}B^{-}_{m+n}+\frac{\tilde{c}_{M}}{12}(m^{3}-m)\delta_{m+n,0},

(256b)

\displaystyle\left[B^{-}_{m},B^{-}_{n}\right]

\displaystyle=0.

(256c)

Here
\tilde{c}_{L}
and
\tilde{c}_{M}
are central charges, values of which depend on the normal ordering. The zero modes
A^{-}_{0}
and
B^{-}_{0}
from earlier classical studies should be
A^{-}_{0}
and
B^{-}_{0}=\sqrt{2c^{\prime}}k^{-}
. However, for quantum theory one must needs to take into account the normal ordering ambiguity. Comparing (
256
) with (
107
), one can express the normal ordering of
A^{-}_{0}
and
B^{-}_{0}
in terms of normal orderings of
L_{0}
and
M_{0}
as

A^{-}_{0}=\frac{2a_{L}}{B^{+}_{0}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ B^{-}_{0}=\sqrt{2c^{\prime}}k^{-}+\frac{a_{M}}{B^{+}_{0}}.

(257)

In the above,
a_{L}
and
a_{M}
respectively are the normal ordering ambiguities of
L_{0}
and
M_{0}
which will be fixed. We also rewrite the commutators using
\eta^{\mu\nu}
in light-cone coordinates

\left[A^{i}_{m},B^{j}_{n}\right]=2n\delta^{ij}\delta_{m,-n}\penalty\ ,\quad\left[x^{i},k^{j}\right]=i\delta^{ij}\penalty\ ,\quad\left[x^{-},k^{+}\right]=-i\penalty\ .

(258)

Now, in order to make the null string theory to be Lorentz invariant, we need to ensure that the gauge choice (
253
) is invariant under the same. For infinitesimal Lorentz transformation

\delta X^{\mu}={\omega^{\mu}}_{\nu}X^{\nu},

variation of the ILST action is given by

\begin{split}\delta S&=\frac{1}{2\pi c^{\prime}}\int d^{2}\sigma\left(V^{a}V^{b}{\omega^{\mu}}_{\nu}\partial_{a}X^{\nu}\partial_{b}X_{\mu}\right)=-\frac{1}{2}\int d\tau\penalty\ \partial_{\tau}\left(\omega_{\mu\nu}J^{\mu\nu}\right).\end{split}

(259)

Here
J^{\mu\nu}
is the generator of the Lorentz transformation given by

J^{\mu\nu}\equiv\int d\sigma\left(X^{\mu}\Pi^{\nu}-X^{\nu}\Pi^{\mu}\right).

(260)

In order to ensure the theory is Lorentz invariant, it must be ensured that
J^{\mu\nu}
satisfy the Lorentz algebra

\left[J^{\mu\nu},J^{\rho\sigma}\right]=-i\left(J^{\mu\rho}\eta^{\nu\sigma}-J^{\nu\rho}\eta^{\mu\sigma}-J^{\mu\sigma}\eta^{\nu\rho}+J^{\nu\sigma}\eta^{\mu\rho}\right).

(261)

In
29
, all these commutators are calculated and it was found that all the commutators vanish except the
\left[J^{i-},J^{j-}\right]
. Hence, one needs to impose that
\left[J^{i-},J^{j-}\right]=0
, and that will be possible only for a certain value of normal ordering
a
and target spacetime dimension
D
.

The Lorentzian generator
J^{i-}
is given by

J^{i-}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\sigma:\left(X^{i}\Pi^{-}-X^{-}\Pi^{i}\right):=L^{i-}+S^{i-},

(262)

where
L^{i-}
and
S^{i-}
are given by

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ L^{i-}=x^{i}k^{-}-x^{-}k^{i},

(263a)

\displaystyle S^{i-}=-\frac{i}{2}

\displaystyle\sum_{m\neq 0}\frac{1}{m}\left(:A^{i}_{-m}B^{-}_{m}:+:A^{-}_{m}B^{i}_{-m}:\right)\penalty\ .

(263b)

Since it is straightforward to see that
\left[L^{i-},L^{j-}\right]=0
, we have

\left[J^{i-},J^{j-}\right]=\left[L^{i-},S^{j-}\right]+\left[S^{i-},L^{j-}\right]+\left[S^{i-},S^{j-}\right].

(264)

Induced Vacuum:

This vacuum correspond to the physical state condition (
249b
) where the vacuum is defined as

B^{i}_{m}|0\rangle_{I}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \forall\penalty\ m\neq 0.

(265)

For this definition of the vacuum the normal ordering in (
255
) becomes

A^{-}_{m}=\frac{1}{B_{0}^{+}}\sum_{n\neq 0}A^{i}_{n}B^{i}_{m-n},\quad B^{-}_{m}=\frac{1}{2B_{0}^{+}}\sum_{n}B^{i}_{n}B^{i}_{m-n},

(266)

and as a result one can see that the central charges in (
256a
) vanishes. Normal ordering for
S^{i-}
is given by

S^{i-}=-\frac{i}{2}\sum_{m\neq 0}\frac{1}{m}\left(A^{i}_{-m}B^{-}_{m}+A^{-}_{m}B^{i}_{-m}\right).

(267)

Using (
267
), one can calculate the commutator in (
264
) as

\begin{split}\left[J^{i-},J^{j-}\right]=\sum_{m\neq 0}\frac{1}{mB^{+}_{0}}\left(A^{i}_{-m}B^{j}_{m}+B^{i}_{-m}A^{j}_{m}\right)\left(B^{-}_{0}-\sqrt{2c^{\prime}}k^{-}\right)+\sum_{m\neq 0}\frac{1}{mB^{+}_{0}}B^{i}_{-m}B^{j}_{m}A^{-}_{0}.\end{split}

(268)

In order to make the r.h.s. of (
268
) vanish for all the physical states one must have the following

\displaystyle A^{-}_{0}|phys\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(269)

\displaystyle B^{-}_{0}|phys\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2c^{\prime}}k^{-}|phys\rangle.

(270)

Comparing this with (
257
) one can see that the normal ordering ambiguities
a_{L}
and
a_{M}
from (
257
) vanish and there is no constraint on the target spacetime dimension.

Flipped Vacuum:

Theory constructed on this vacuum is based on the physical state condition (
249a
), and the vacuum in this theory is given by

A^{i}_{m}|0\rangle_{F}=0,B^{i}_{m}|0\rangle_{F}=0\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \forall\penalty\ m>0.

(271)

For this definition of vacuum the normal ordering in (
255
) becomes the following

\displaystyle A^{-}_{m}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{B_{0}^{+}}\left(\sum_{n<0}A^{i}_{n}B^{i}_{m-n}+\sum_{n>0}B^{i}_{m-n}A^{i}_{n}\right),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \forall m\neq 0

(272a)

\displaystyle A^{-}_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{B_{0}^{+}}\left(\sum_{n<0}A^{i}_{n}B^{i}_{-n}+\sum_{n>0}B^{i}_{-n}A^{i}_{n}\right),

(272b)

\displaystyle B^{-}_{m}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2B_{0}^{+}}\sum_{n}B^{i}_{n}B^{i}_{m-n}.

(272c)

The central charges in (
256a
) for this vacuum is given by

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{L}=\frac{8(D-2)}{(B_{0}^{+})^{2}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \tilde{c}_{M}=0.

(273)

Normal ordering of
S^{i-}
in this theory is given by

S^{i-}=-\frac{i}{2}\sum_{m>0}\frac{1}{m}\left(A^{i}_{-m}B^{-}_{m}+B^{i}_{-m}A^{-}_{m}\right)-\frac{i}{2}\sum_{m<0}\frac{1}{m}\left(B^{-}_{m}A^{i}_{-m}+A^{-}_{m}B^{i}_{-m}\right)\penalty\ .

(274)

Using (
274
) one can calculate
\left[J^{i-},J^{j-}\right]

\displaystyle\left[J^{i-},J^{j-}\right]=\left[L^{i-},S^{j-}\right]+\left[S^{i-},L^{j-}\right]+\left[S^{i-},S^{j-}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\neq 0}\frac{1}{mB^{+}_{0}}\left(A^{i}_{-m}B^{j}_{m}+B^{i}_{-m}A^{j}_{m}\right)\left(B^{-}_{0}-\sqrt{2c^{\prime}}k^{-}\right)+\left(\frac{D-2}{6}-4\right)\sum_{m\neq 0}\frac{m}{(B^{+}_{0})^{2}}B^{i}_{-m}B^{j}_{m}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\left(A^{-}_{0}-\frac{D-2}{6B^{+}_{0}}\right)\sum_{m\neq 0}\frac{1}{mB^{+}_{0}}B^{i}_{-m}B^{j}_{m}\penalty\ .

(275)

In order to fix the r.h.s. of (
9.2
) to zero we need the following

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 26,

(276a)

\displaystyle A^{-}_{0}|phys\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{B^{+}_{0}},\penalty\ \penalty\ {\rm\text{and}}

(276b)

\displaystyle B^{-}_{0}|phys\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2c^{\prime}}k^{-}|phys\rangle.

(276c)

Comparing (
276
) with (
257
) one can easily see that
a_{L}=2
,
a_{M}=0
. Also, the critical dimension for this theory is 26.

Oscillator Vacuum:

The oscillator vacuum correspond to the physical state condition described in (
249c
). The vacuum in this case is defined in terms of {
C,\tilde{C}
} in the same line as the definition of tensile string vacuum

C^{i}_{m}|0\rangle_{c}=0\quad{\rm and}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \tilde{C}^{i}_{m}|0\rangle_{c}=0\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \forall m>0.

(277)

It turns out that for this theory, it is more convenient to work in the harmonic oscillators {
C,\tilde{C}
}. Calculating the central algebra in (
256
) for this vacuum in terms of {
C,\tilde{C}
} gives

\displaystyle\left[C^{-}_{m},C^{-}_{n}\right]=

\displaystyle\frac{m-n}{2B^{+}_{0}}\left(3C^{-}_{m+n}+\tilde{C}^{-}_{-m-n}\right)+\frac{(D-2)}{6(B^{+}_{0})^{2}}(m^{3}-m)\delta_{n,-m}\penalty\ ,

(278a)

\displaystyle\left[C^{-}_{m},\tilde{C}^{-}_{-n}\right]=

\displaystyle-\frac{(m-n)}{2B^{+}_{0}}\left(C^{-}_{m+n}-\tilde{C}^{-}_{-m-n}\right)\penalty\ ,

(278b)

\displaystyle\left[\tilde{C}^{-}_{-m},\tilde{C}^{-}_{-n}\right]=

\displaystyle-\frac{(m-n)}{2B^{+}_{0}}\left(C^{-}_{m+n}+3\tilde{C}^{-}_{-m-n}\right)-\frac{(D-2)}{6(B^{+}_{0})^{2}}(m^{3}-m)\delta_{n,-m}.

(278c)

We also need to re-express
S^{i-}
in terms of {
C,\tilde{C}
} and find the appropriate normal ordering for the oscillator vacuum. The normal ordering for
S^{i-}
is

S^{i-}=-i\sum_{m>0}\frac{1}{m}\left(C^{i}_{-m}C^{-}_{m}-\tilde{C}^{-}_{-m}\tilde{C}^{i}_{m}\right)-i\sum_{m<0}\frac{1}{m}\left(C^{-}_{m}C^{i}_{-m}-\tilde{C}^{i}_{m}\tilde{C}^{-}_{-m}\right)\penalty\ .

(279)

Using (
279
), one can calculate the r.h.s. of (
264
) and find the following

\begin{split}\left[J^{i-},J^{j-}\right]&=\left[L^{i-},S^{j-}\right]+\left[S^{i-},L^{j-}\right]+\left[S^{i-},S^{j-}\right]\\
&=\frac{1}{\left(B^{+}_{0}\right)^{2}}\left(\frac{D-2}{6}-4\right)\sum_{m<0}m\left(C^{i}_{-m}C^{j}_{m}-\tilde{C}^{i}_{m}\tilde{C}^{j}_{-m}\right)\\
&+\frac{1}{B^{+}_{0}}\sum_{m\neq 0}\frac{1}{m}\left(C^{i}_{-m}C^{j}_{m}-\tilde{C}^{i}_{m}\tilde{C}^{j}_{-m}\right)\left(2B^{-}_{0}-2\sqrt{2c^{\prime}}k^{-}-\frac{D-2}{6B^{+}_{0}}\right)\\
&+\frac{1}{B^{+}_{0}}\sum_{m\neq 0}\frac{1}{m}B^{i}_{-m}B^{j}_{m}A^{-}_{0}\penalty\ .\\
\end{split}

(280)

The r.h.s. of (
280
) vanishes for the following choice of
a
,
b
and
D

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 26,

(281a)

\displaystyle A^{-}_{0}|phys\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,\penalty\ \penalty\ {\text{ and}}

(281b)

\displaystyle B^{-}_{0}|phys\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\sqrt{2c^{\prime}}k^{-}+\frac{2}{B^{+}_{0}}\right)|phys\rangle.

(281c)

Comparing with (
257
), one can clearly see that for this theory
a_{L}=0
and
a_{M}=2
.

So, in summary, in this section we have seen that there are three quantum tensionless string theories that emerge from the ILST string. We have computed their critical dimensions now by considering the string to be propagating in flat target spacetime and attempting to close the spacetime Poincare algebra. We have seen that:

•

Induced theory:
This is built on the Induced vacuum and follows the induced representations of the underlying BMS
3
algebra on the worldsheet. Our lightcone analysis told us that there is
no restriction on the critical dimension
. In particular, if you start with usual tensile string theory, which is consistent only in
D=26
, the Induced null string can sit as a limit of this theory. We will see while examining the Hilbert space of the theory that this indeed is the most natural candidate when we are taking a very high energy or a tensionless limit of tensile critical string theory.

•

Flipped theory:
In this theory, we use the highest weight vacuum and this theory, as we will see in the coming sections, can be thought about as coming from a rather intriguing “flipped” tensile theory which is built on a curious vacuum which is the highest weight vacuum for the holomorphic sector and the lowest weight for the antiholomorphic sector. In our analysis above, we found that this is consistent in
D=26
. Going by this alone, we could envision the Flipped quantum null string also to appear in a corner of critical tensile quantum string theory. We should add that our analysis of the spectrum of the theory, which we will address in the coming sections, indicates that this may not be the right expectation, although we should not rule out some new duality that relates this to usual string theory and its high energy limit.

•

Oscillator theory:
By far the most interesting vacuum seems to be the oscillator vacuum and the theory built on it does not seem to be related to usual string theory at first glance. We will see however that we can make sense of this in terms of accelerated worldsheets in the coming sections. Again, the theory is consistent only in
D=26
and this is indication that this could reside as a tensionless limit of a critical tensile theory.

The above picture reflects the fact that based on the critical dimensions alone, these three quantum null string theories could arise as corners, albeit strange corners, of critical tensile string theory.

Figure 7:
Three quantum avatars of a single classical theory

10
Novelties of Quantum Null Strings

The quantum theory of null tensionless strings is full of surprising unexpected features at every turn. We have already seen that there are three vacua and hence three consistent quantum theories built out of a single classical theory for the bosonic null string. We now take the reader through some of the most intriguing features of the quantum null strings.

10.1
Open strings from closed strings in tensionless limit

As is common knowledge in interacting (tensile) string theory, open strings can join to form closed strings and closed strings can split up into open strings. Open string amplitudes at one loop produce closed strings. In the tensionless limit, even without interactions, something remarkable happens. As tension decreases, closed strings grow long and floppy and one would expect that something akin to the figure below happens.

Figure 8:
From closed strings to open strings - route 1

So one might expect the emergence of open strings from closed string in the tensionless limit. Below, following
15
, we will show that this intuition can be made mathematically precise.

Bogoliubov transformations on the worldsheet:

The fundamental observation behind most of the material in this section is the relation between the tensile
\alpha
oscillators and the tensionless
C
oscillators, given by:

C^{\mu}_{n}=\cosh\theta\penalty\ \alpha_{n}^{\mu}+\sinh\theta\penalty\ \tilde{\alpha}^{\mu}_{-n},\qquad\tilde{C}^{\mu}_{n}=\sinh\theta\penalty\ \alpha^{\mu}_{-n}+\cosh\theta\penalty\ \tilde{\alpha}^{\mu}_{n},

(282)

where, the relation between
\theta
and
\epsilon
is as follows

\cosh\theta=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\sqrt{\epsilon}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\Big),\qquad\sinh\theta=\frac{1}{2}\Big(\sqrt{\epsilon}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\Big).

(283)

These are Bogoliubov transformations and preserve the canonical structure even as the relations become singular as
\epsilon\to 0
. It is important to stress here that the relations above only hold for very small values of
\epsilon
. We will return to the full evolution of the tensile to tensionless oscillators in a later section below.
It is evident that the tensionless oscillators
(C,\tilde{C})
are linear combinations of both creation and annihilation operators of the tensile theory. Consequently, the tensionless vacuum

\ket{0}_{C}:\qquad C^{\mu}_{\ n}\ket{0}_{C}=0=\tilde{C}^{\mu}_{\ n}\ket{0}_{C}\qquad\forall n>0.

(284)

is not same as the tensile vacuum defined by

\ket{0}_{\alpha}:\qquad\alpha^{\mu}_{\ n}\ket{0}_{\alpha}=0=\tilde{\alpha}^{\mu}_{\ n}\ket{0}_{\alpha}\qquad\forall n>0.

(285)

This distinction in the vacua of oscillators would play a crucial role in what follows.

Boundary states and Induced vacuum:

Now we briefly recall the definition of boundary states in conventional tensile string theory. A boundary state on the worldsheet is described by a coherent state in the closed string Hilbert space and encode the CFT description of a D-brane in string theory. For a boundary at
\tau=0
, the allowed conditions are

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}X|B_{N}\rangle

\displaystyle=0\equiv(\alpha_{n}+\tilde{\alpha}_{-n})|B_{N}\rangle=0:\qquad\text{Neumann},

(286a)

\displaystyle\partial_{\sigma}X|B_{D}\rangle

\displaystyle=0\equiv(\alpha_{n}-\tilde{\alpha}_{-n})|B_{D}\rangle=0:\qquad\text{Dirichlet}.

(286b)

Solving these conditions yields Neumann and Dirichlet boundary states:

\displaystyle|B_{N}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}_{N}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\exp\left[-\frac{\alpha_{-n}\cdot\tilde{\alpha}_{-n}}{n}\right]|0\rangle_{\alpha},

(287a)

\displaystyle|B_{D}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}_{D}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\exp\left[\frac{\alpha_{-n}\cdot\tilde{\alpha}_{-n}}{n}\right]|0\rangle_{\alpha}.

(287b)

In the above,
\mathcal{N}_{N}
and
\mathcal{N}_{D}
are normalization constants.

We remind the reader that the quantum null string had three different choices of vacuum and hence three different theories were built out of the ILST action. One of these was the so-called induced vacuum corresponding to the induced representation of the underlying BMS algebra on the worldsheet. The vacuum in the induced representation
\ket{0}_{I}
is given by:

B_{n}^{\mu}\ket{0}_{I}=0,\qquad\forall n.

(288)

In terms of
\{C,\tilde{C}\}
(
159
), this can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\big(C^{\mu}_{n}+\tilde{C}^{\mu}_{-n}\big)\ket{0}_{I}=0\qquad\forall n,

(289)

which corresponds to the Neumann boundary state condition (
286
) in the (
C,\tilde{C}
) basis:

\displaystyle\ket{0}_{I}=\mathcal{N}_{c}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\exp\Big[-\frac{1}{n}C_{-n}\cdot\tilde{C}_{-n}\Big]\ket{0}_{c}.

(290)

So we see that in terms of the
C
oscillators, the induced vacuum is a Neumann boundary state.

Connecting vacua:

The Bogoliubov transformations between the tensile
\alpha
and tensionless oscillators
C
oscillators also allows us to connect the vacua of the two theories. To see this, we express the
\alpha
-oscillators in terms of
C
oscillators as

\displaystyle\alpha^{\mu}_{n}=e^{iF}C_{n}e^{-iF},\qquad\tilde{\alpha}^{\mu}_{n}=e^{iF}\tilde{C}_{n}e^{-iF},

(291)

where

F=i\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\theta\left[C_{-n}\cdot\tilde{C}_{-n}-C_{n}\cdot\tilde{C}_{n}\right],\qquad\ \tanh\theta=\frac{\epsilon-1}{\epsilon+1}.

(292)

We can now express one vacuum in terms of the other:

\displaystyle|0\rangle_{\alpha}=\exp\penalty\ (iF)\penalty\ |0\rangle_{c}=\left(\frac{1}{\cosh\theta}\right)^{1+1+\cdots}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\exp\left[\frac{\tanh\theta}{n}C_{-n}\tilde{C}_{-n}\right]|0\rangle_{c}

(293)

Using zeta function regularization (
1+1+1+\ldots\infty=\zeta(0)=-\frac{1}{2}
) we obtain

|0\rangle_{\alpha}=\sqrt{\cosh\theta}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\exp\left[\frac{\tanh\theta}{n}\,C_{-n}\cdot\tilde{C}_{-n}\right]|0\rangle_{c}.

(294)

In the tensionless limit,
\epsilon\to 0
, we have
\tanh\theta=-1
, and hence:

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}|0\rangle_{\alpha}={\mathcal{N}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\exp\left[-\,\frac{1}{n}C_{-n}\cdot\tilde{C}_{-n}\right]|0\rangle_{c}

(295)

This is precisely the induced vacuum that we encountered earlier (
290
). So we have

\displaystyle\ket{0}_{I}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}|0\rangle_{\alpha}={\mathcal{N}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\exp\left[-\,\frac{1}{n}C_{-n}\cdot\tilde{C}_{-n}\right]|0\rangle_{c}

(296)

The induced vacuum thus corresponds to the Neumann boundary state, representing an open string free to move in all spacetime directions or a space-filling D-25 brane. Thus as the tension is dialled to zero, the closed string vacuum evolves into an
open string
description
15
19
19

19

Transition from closed string to open string at tensionless limit has also been studied in presence of constant Kalb-Ramond background in a very recent work
82
.
.

Figure 9:
Emergence of spacefilling D-branes

Null string complementarity:

The equations relating the tensionless oscillators
C
to the tensile operators
\alpha
can be also be used to write the tensionless vacuum in terms of the tensile vacuum, effectively inverting (
293
). This gives

\displaystyle|0\rangle_{c}=\mathcal{N}^{\prime}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\exp\left[-\frac{\tanh\theta}{n}\,\alpha_{-n}\cdot\tilde{\alpha}_{-n}\right]|0\rangle_{\alpha}.

(297)

Notice that this has a crucial minus sign inside the exponential. In the limit of
\epsilon\to 0
, the vacua are related as:

\displaystyle|0\rangle_{c}=\mathcal{N}_{D}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\exp\left[\frac{1}{n}\,\alpha_{-n}\cdot\tilde{\alpha}_{-n}\right]|0\rangle_{\alpha}.

(298)

So we see that the tensionless vacuum in terms of the tensile theory is now a
Dirichlet
boundary state in all directions. This is thus a
D
-instanton in the tensile theory.

We have a rather remarkable observation at this point, which we call
null string complementarity
. The picture is as follows. A tensionless observer, whose vacuum is
\ket{0}_{c}
, sees the tensile vacuum evolve into a spacefilling D-25 brane, i.e, an open string with Neumann boundary conditions in all directions. A tensile observer, whose vacuum is
\ket{0}_{\alpha}
, instead sees the tensionless string collapse to a spacetime point, corresponding to D-instantons. Thus, depending on the observer, we have two very different views of the transition from closed to open strings as the tension is dialled to zero: either as D-instanton emerges or a space-filling D-25 brane. We refer to this striking observer-dependent reinterpretation as null string complementarity.

Worldsheet condensation:

We now follow the fate of the tensile closed string perturbative states in tensionless limit
15
. Consider a level matched perturbative physical state in the tensile closed string theory:

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi_{n}}=\xi_{\mu\nu}\alpha^{\mu}_{-n}\tilde{\alpha}^{\nu}_{-n}\ket{0}_{\alpha}.

(299)

In the above
\xi_{\mu\nu}
is polarisation tensor. We have seen that in the tensionless limit, the tensile vacuum evolves into the induced vacuum of the tensionless string, i.e. in strict
\epsilon\to 0
limit,
\ket{0}_{\alpha}\to\ket{0}_{I}
. We now assume that close to the tensionless point,
\ket{0}_{\alpha}
near
\epsilon\to 0
can be expanded as

\displaystyle\ket{0}_{\alpha}=\ket{0}_{I}+\epsilon\ket{I_{1}}+\epsilon^{2}\ket{I_{2}}+\cdots

(300)

We now observe that in terms of the
(A,B)
oscillators, using the above expansion and the definition of the induced vacuum
\ket{0}_{I}
, we have the following expressions:

\displaystyle B_{n}\ket{0}_{I}=0,\hskip 14.22636pt\forall n\neq 0

(301a)

\displaystyle A_{n}\ket{0}_{I}=-B_{n}\ket{I_{1}}\hskip 22.76219ptA_{-n}\ket{0}_{I}=B_{-n}\ket{I_{1}}\hskip 25.60747pt\forall n>0

(301b)

\displaystyle A_{n}\ket{I_{1}}=-B_{n}\ket{I_{2}}\hskip 22.76219ptA_{-n}\ket{I_{1}}=B_{-n}\ket{I_{2}}\hskip 27.0301pt\forall n>0.

(301c)

Now, we let’s look at the evolution of the perturbative physical states
\ket{\Psi_{n}}
. Rewriting {
\alpha,\tilde{\alpha}
} in terms of {
A,B
} and using (
300
) and (
299
), we get

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi_{n}}=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big(B_{-n}+\epsilon A_{-n}\Big)\Big(B_{n}-\epsilon A_{n}\Big)\Big(\ket{0}_{I}+\epsilon\ket{I_{1}}+\epsilon^{2}\ket{I_{2}}+\cdots\Big).

(302)

Using (
157
) and (
301
), we see that remarkably the level matched perturbative state
\ket{\Psi_{n}}
will condense to the induced vacuum:

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi_{n}}\to\Xi\ket{0}_{I},\hskip 14.22636pt\Xi=2n\eta^{\mu\nu}\xi_{\mu\nu}.

(303)

Notice that we have made no assumption on the level
n
of the state. This establishes that for any
n
the state
\ket{\Psi_{n}}
at tensionless limit will evolve into the induced vacuum. We thus see that in the tensionless limit, all perturbative states over the tensile vacuum reduce to the tensionless vacuum resulting in a Bose-Einstein like condensation on the string worldsheet.

Figure 10:
Bose-Einstein like condensation on the string worldsheet at
T\to 0
.

Connections to Hagedorn Physics:

It is very well known that a gas of strings is heated to higher and higher temperatures, something very interesting happens. The string partition function diverges at the Hagedorn temperature and it is believed that string theory evolves into a new phase where new degrees of freedom appear. In the theory of free strings, one can show that it becomes thermodynamically favourable to form a long open string at very high temperatures near the Hagedorn temperature. Interestingly, this very high temperature limit, the strings become effectively tensionless. If
\mathcal{T}
is the temperature of the gas of strings and
\mathcal{T}_{H}
is the Hagedorn temperature, the tension of the heated string goes as
146
;
144

\displaystyle T(\mathcal{T})=T_{0}\sqrt{1-\left(\frac{\mathcal{T}}{\mathcal{T}_{H}}\right)^{2}}.

(304)

In the above,
T_{0}=\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}
is the original tension of unheated string. In this case, we see that in our parametrization

\displaystyle\epsilon=\sqrt{1-\left(\frac{\mathcal{T}}{\mathcal{T}_{H}}\right)^{2}}.

(305)

The previous observations of the emergence of the open string from the closed string and the condensation of the perturbative closed string states to the induced vacuum all are in keeping with the thermodynamic picture of the emergence of the long open string as the temperature of the gas of closed strings is turned up and close to the Hagedorn temperature. The picture we are advocating here is that if one keeps heating a gas of closed strings, the tension decreases and the strings become longer and longer and ultimately all closed string degrees of freedom condense on the worldsheet to form a long open string, which is the same as long anticipated from thermodynamic considerations. The tensionless limit clarifies the worldsheet phenomenon that leads to the formation of this open string from closed strings.

10.2
Null strings and worldsheet Rindler structures

As we have seen in the earlier section, a lot of the most interesting new features of the quantum null strings arise from the fact that the tensile oscillators and the tensionless oscillators are connected by Bogoliubov transformations on the worldsheet. We stressed earlier that these Bogoliubov transformations were valid only very near the tensionless point where
\epsilon
was close to zero. We now wish to understand a complete flow between a tensile and tensionless theory. As we know that the oscillators are Bogoliubov transforms of one another at the very end, it is tempting to formulate the entire flow in terms of Bogoliubov transformations which would reduce to the map (
282
) as
\epsilon\to 0
.

The most natural place where Bogoliubov transformations occur is when one thinks of accelerated observers in flat spacetimes and hence the map between Rindler and Minkowski spacetimes. In the limit of infinite acceleration, the Rindler observer hits the horizon of Rindler spacetime. For the context of the string, we could envision these structures arising on the worldsheet and hence consider accelerating worldsheets. We are thus led to an understanding of tensionless limit of string theory from the perspective of increasing acceleration of a family of worldsheets
10
. Below, following
10
, we show explicitly that the null string emerges when the Rindler horizon of the worldsheet is reached. The Rindler horizon is approached in two distinct ways: evolution in the acceleration at fixed time and evolution in time at fixed acceleration.

Accelerated worldsheets and the horizon limit:

We begin with the metric described by a uniformly accelerated observer in Minkowski spacetime, which takes the Rindler form,

dS_{Rind}^{2}=e^{2a\rho}(-d\eta^{2}+d\rho^{2}).

(306)

Minkowski and Rindler spacetimes are related by the following coordinate transformation,

t=\frac{1}{a}e^{a\rho}\sinh(a\eta),\qquad x=\frac{1}{a}e^{a\rho}\cosh(a\eta),

(307)

where
a
is the acceleration. We now consider a massless scalar field theory in the Rindler spacetime
47
. Since the Rindler spacetime is conformally flat, the EOM for the scalar field is identical to that in flat spacetime.

(-\partial_{t}^{2}+\partial_{x}^{2})\phi=0=(-\partial_{\eta}^{2}+\partial_{\rho}^{2})\phi.

(308)

The corresponding Minkowski mode expansion is

\displaystyle\phi(\sigma,\tau)=\phi_{0}+\sqrt{2\alpha^{\prime}}\alpha_{0}\tau+\sqrt{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{n>0}\left[\alpha_{n}u_{n}+\alpha_{-n}u_{n}^{*}+\tilde{\alpha}_{n}\tilde{u}_{n}+\tilde{\alpha}_{-n}\tilde{u}_{n}^{*}\right]

(309)

where the oscillators
\alpha_{n},\tilde{\alpha}_{n}
annihilate the Minkowski vacuum and satisfy the following relations

[\alpha_{n},\alpha_{m}]=n\delta_{n+m},\qquad[\tilde{\alpha}_{n},\tilde{\alpha}_{m}]=n\delta_{n+m},\qquad[\alpha_{n},\tilde{\alpha}_{m}]=0.

(310)

In Rindler coordinates, the mode expansion takes the form

\displaystyle\phi(\rho,\eta)=\phi_{0}+\sqrt{2\alpha^{\prime}}\beta_{0}\rho+\sqrt{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{n>0}[\beta_{n}U_{n}+\beta_{-n}U_{n}^{*}+\tilde{\beta}_{n}\tilde{U}_{n}+\tilde{\beta}_{-n}\tilde{U}_{n}^{*}].

(311)

Here, the oscillators
(\beta_{n},\tilde{\beta_{n}})
annihilate the Rindler vacuum
\ket{0}_{R}
. Unlike Minkowski modes, the functions
U_{n}
and
\tilde{U}_{n}
are defined only in the left and right Rindler wedges, respectively. So, we label them as
U_{n}^{(L)}
and
\tilde{U}_{n}^{(R)}
. To define operators valid across the horizon, we introduce standard analytically continued combinations
161
:

U_{n}^{(R)}-e^{-\frac{\pi n}{a}}U_{-n}^{(L)*},\quad U_{-n}^{(R)*}-e^{\frac{\pi n}{a}}U_{n}^{(L)}.

(312)

These combinations allow us to relate
\alpha
oscillators in Minkowski space to
\beta
oscillators in Rindler spacetime through Bogoliubov transformations
161
:

\displaystyle\beta_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cfrac{e^{\pi n/2a}}{\sqrt{2\sinh\frac{\pi n}{a}}}\penalty\ \alpha_{n}-\frac{e^{-\pi n/2a}}{\sqrt{2\sinh\frac{\pi n}{a}}}\penalty\ \tilde{\alpha}_{-n},

(313a)

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\cfrac{e^{-\pi n/2a}}{\sqrt{2\sinh\frac{\pi n}{a}}}\penalty\ \alpha_{-n}+\cfrac{e^{\pi n/2a}}{\sqrt{2\sinh\frac{\pi n}{a}}}\penalty\ \tilde{\alpha}_{n}.

(313b)

In the limit of large acceleration, (
313
) reduces to

\displaystyle\beta_{n}^{\infty}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\pi n}{2a}}+\sqrt{\frac{2a}{\pi n}}\right)\alpha_{n}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\pi n}{2a}}-\sqrt{\frac{2a}{\pi n}}\right)\tilde{\alpha}_{-n},

(314a)

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}_{n}^{\infty}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{\pi n}{2a}}-\sqrt{\frac{2a}{\pi n}}\right)\alpha_{-n}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{\frac{2a}{\pi n}}+\sqrt{\frac{\pi n}{2a}}\right)\tilde{\alpha}_{n}.

(314b)

Comparing the above transformations in the large acceleration limit (
314
) with the Bogoliubov transformations between
\alpha
oscillators and
C
oscillators in the tensionless limit (
173
), we can identify

C_{n}=\beta_{n}^{\infty},\qquad\tilde{C}_{n}=\tilde{\beta}_{n}^{\infty},\qquad\text{where}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \epsilon=\frac{\pi n}{2a}.

(315)

Thus, the large-acceleration limit of the Rindler worldsheet reproduces precisely the tensionless oscillators. The map between increase in the acceleration and decrease in tension is summarised below

\displaystyle a=0

\displaystyle:\qquad\{\beta_{n},\tilde{\beta}_{n}\}\to\{\alpha_{n},\tilde{\alpha}_{n}\}

(316a)

\displaystyle 0<a<\infty

\displaystyle:\qquad\{\beta_{n}(a),\tilde{\beta}_{n}(a)\}

(316b)

\displaystyle a\to\infty

\displaystyle:\qquad\{\beta_{n},\tilde{\beta}_{n}\}\to\{C_{n},\tilde{C}_{n}\}

(316c)

The vacuum – emergent boundary state:

Now that we have obtained the
\beta
oscillators for accelerated worldsheet, we define the vacuum (
\ket{0}_{R}
) as:

\displaystyle\beta^{\mu}_{n}|0\rangle_{R}=(\alpha^{\mu}_{n}+\tanh\theta_{n}\ \tilde{\alpha}^{\mu}_{-n})|0\rangle_{R}=0,\ \qquad n>0;

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}^{\mu}_{n}|0\rangle_{R}=(\tilde{\alpha}^{\mu}_{n}+\tanh\theta_{n}\ \alpha^{\mu}_{-n})|0\rangle_{R}=0.

(317)

In the limit (
a\to\infty
),
\tanh{\theta}_{n}=-1
. This maps to the tensionless limit (
\epsilon\to 0
), resulting in a squeezed state

\displaystyle|0\rangle_{c}=\lim_{a\to\infty}|0\rangle_{R}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}_{\alpha}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\exp\left[\frac{1}{n}\alpha_{-n}\cdot\tilde{\alpha}_{-n}\right]|0\rangle_{\alpha}.

(318)

This is exactly the Dirichlet boundary state in all directions defined in (
286
). Hence, as stated earlier, from the point of view of the usual string vacuum (
\ket{0}_{\alpha}
), tensionless vacuum is a
D-instantons
. This picture fits very well with the Rindler worldsheets as we will explain below.

Figure 11:
Increasing accelerated worldsheets.

An inertial closed string worldsheet corresponds to a cylinder, which is recovered in the limit of zero acceleration,
a\to 0
. As the acceleration is increased, the worldsheet deviates from this cylindrical shape and is successively mapped to a family of curved (hyperbolic) surfaces, each more distorted than the previous one. In the limit
a\to\infty
, the worldsheet degenerates to lightcone (a null surface). Since the boundary states in (
286
) are defined at
\tau=0
, it is useful to examine how
\tau=0
slices behave along this trajectory. For small acceleration,
\tau=0
section is a circle of finite radius. As the acceleration increases, the radius steadily decreases. In the extreme limit of
a\to\infty
, the circle shrinks to a point. This limiting pointlike configuration is the spacetime interpretation of the D-instanton discussed earlier. The complementary phenomenon, the emergence of the open string arises when the Rindler horizon is reached at fixed acceleration, a view point we now turn to.

Rindler time evolution and the horizon limit:

As discussed above, the Rindler horizon can be approached in two independent ways. The first involved increasing the acceleration at fixed Rindler time. We now focus on the second route: keeping the acceleration constant while allowing the Rindler time
\eta
to evolve. In this picture, the horizon is reached in the
\eta\to\infty
limit. This limit may be implemented equivalently through the scaling

\eta\to\eta,\qquad\rho\to\epsilon\rho,\qquad\epsilon\to 0.

(319)

Under this scaling, the 2d conformal generators of the Rindler theory reduce to

\displaystyle L_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{k}-\bar{{\mathcal{L}}}_{-k}=i^{k}e^{-k\eta}(\partial_{\eta}-k\rho\partial_{\rho}),

(320)

\displaystyle M_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\epsilon({\mathcal{L}}_{k}+\bar{{\mathcal{L}}}_{-k})=-i^{k}e^{-k\eta}\partial_{\rho}

(321)

which closes to form the classical part of BMS
3
algebra (
244
) (with
c_{L}=c_{M}=0
), the symmetry algebra characterizing the null strings
6
.
We now describe a complementary viewpoint of the emergence of the open string from the closed string associated with this time-evolution picture.

Figure 12:
Equal time slices in Rindler spacetimes

In Rindler spacetime, surfaces of constant Rindler time are straight lines passing through the origin, distinguished by their slope. As shown in Fig. (
12
), the right Rindler wedge contains a family of such lines with slopes increasing for
\eta_{1}<\eta_{2}<\eta_{3}
. In the limit
\eta_{\infty}
, these lines approach the Rindler horizon. In the left wedge, the Rinlder time runs in the opposite direction, and the continuation of these constant-
\eta
lines extends into the third quadrant. We now relate this with the string worldsheet at constant acceleration. Increasing
\eta
planes intersects the constant hyperboloid at increasing angles. The
\eta
evolution of the closed string progressively distorts the spatial section of the closed string worldsheet. At early Rindler time, the (
\eta=0
) slice describes an ordinary closed string of finite radius. As time evolves, the effective tension decreases and the spatial profile of the string becomes elongated. The shape continuously stretches and in the limit
\eta\to\infty
, the spatial cross-section degenerates into a straight line. At this point the worldsheet becomes null and the closed string effectively transitions into an open, tensionless configuration. This picture is identical to the original one we presented as Fig
8
.

Figure 13:
Equal time-slices of a Rindler worldsheet

Thus approaching the worldsheet horizon by evolving in Rindler time provides gives a very clear but different picture of how closed strings can appear as open strings.
20
20

20

An analysis with time evolving strings and the Milne patch on the worldsheet was constructed with in direct analogy with this construction of accelerated worldsheets in
124
.

Rindler spacetime and Carroll strings

In the previous subsections, we saw that introducing a Rindler structure on the closed string worldsheet leads to a number of remarkable phenomena, including the emergence of null strings and the observer-dependent picture captured by null string complementarity. A natural question then arises:
under what physical circumstances does such a Rindler structure arise on the worldsheet of a string propagating in spacetime?

The answer comes from a simple observation: the near-horizon region of any non-extremal black hole is described by a Rindler spacetime. As a string moves closer to the black hole horizon, it propagates in a region whose geometry is approximately Rindler. This Rindler structure of the target spacetime in turn induces a corresponding Rindler structure on the worldsheet
6
. From the worldsheet perspective, approaching the spacetime horizon translates into an increase in the effective worldsheet acceleration, causing the worldsheet to deviate more and more from its cylindrical shape. In the limiting process where the string reaches the horizon, the worldsheet becomes null, precisely the situation encountered in our Rindler-worldsheet analysis, and the string becomes effectively tensionless.

In string theory, Carrollian geometry appears in two complementary contexts. Our review as so far been about Carrollian structures appearing on the worldsheet. Strings can also propagate in target Carrollian spacetimes. This is what we will focus on in the part
IV
of our review. Bringing these insights together, we see the following unified picture.

•

A string falling toward a black hole horizon encounters a Rindler region.

•

This induces a Rindler worldsheet, whose approach to the worldsheet horizon mirrors the tensionless limit.

•

In this null limit, the worldsheet naturally acquires a Carrollian structure, placing the near-horizon dynamics of the string within the framework of Carroll strings.

In the part
IV
, we make this connection explicit by analysing closed strings near black hole horizons and demonstrating that their residual worldsheet symmetry (electric) becomes the BMS
3
algebra, precisely the residual worldsheet symmetry of tensionless strings. We also study the classical string solutions in these near-horizon regimes.

Now we go back to the three quantum null strings and their formulation in flat target spacetimes. We will focus on understanding the spectra of these three theories in the coming sections.

11
Physical Hilbert spaces for three vacua

In this section we discuss the physical Hilbert spaces corresponding to the oscillator, induced and flipped vacuum, primarily based on
7
. We have already established how general physical state conditions need to be imposed on the states built out on these vacua. In what follows we use these conditions accordingly.

11.1
Oscillator Vacuum

We start from the physical state condition for the oscillator vacuum, where the physical state condition that works (
249
) is the full sandwich condition, which is the weakest among all three conditions elucidated before. For the algebra generators, the sandwich condition can be written as:

\displaystyle\bra{phys^{\prime}}L_{n}-a_{L}\delta_{n,0}\ket{phys}

\displaystyle=0

(322a)

\displaystyle\bra{phys^{\prime}}M_{n}-a_{M}\delta_{n,0}\ket{phys}

\displaystyle=0.

(322b)

As already shown via light-cone quantisation in the earlier section, the normal ordering constants for oscillator vacuum are
a_{L}=0
and
a_{M}=2
. This provides us with the recipe to construct physical states in this case.

Construction of Hilbert space:

Before discussing the Hilbert space for this theory, let us first recall the expressions of
L_{n}
s and
M_{n}
s in terms of {
C,\tilde{C}
} from (
164
), which we will use in the sandwich condition (
322
). Of course the subtlety in this case is in the zero modes, the expression of which in terms of {
C,\tilde{C}
} becomes,

\displaystyle L_{0}=\mathcal{N}-\widetilde{\mathcal{N}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ M_{0}=c^{\prime}k^{2}+\mathcal{N}+\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}+X+X^{\dagger},

(323a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}=\sum_{m>0}

\displaystyle C_{-m}\cdot C_{m};\hskip 14.22636pt\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}=\sum_{m>0}\tilde{C}_{-m}\cdot\tilde{C}_{m};\hskip 14.22636ptX=\sum_{m>0}C_{m}\cdot\tilde{C}_{m}.

(323b)

Note the structure of
X
which contains a mixture of the {
C,\tilde{C}
} modes.
As discussed in (
277
), the oscillator vacuum is, by definition, annihilated by all the
C_{n}
s and
\tilde{C}_{n}
s for
n>0
. It also is eigenstate of
C_{0}
and
\tilde{C}_{0}
:

\displaystyle C^{\mu}_{0}\ket{0;k}_{c}=\tilde{C}^{\mu}_{0}\ket{0;k}_{c}=\sqrt{\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}}k^{\mu}\ket{0;k}_{c},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ C^{\mu}_{n}\ket{0;k}_{c}=\tilde{C}^{\mu}_{n}\ket{0;k}_{c}=0,\hskip 14.22636pt\forall n>0.

(324)

Like tensile string vacuum, this vacuum too, is normalised as

\displaystyle\braket{0;k^{\prime}|0;k}=(2\pi)^{D}\delta^{D}(k^{\prime}-k).

(325)

It is important to note that the algebra satisfied by {
C,\tilde{C}
} is same as the algebra satisfied by the tensile oscillators {
\alpha,\tilde{\alpha}
}. Hence, looking at (
324
) one can clearly see that, just like the tensile case,
\mathcal{N}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
are number operators, while {
C_{n},\tilde{C}_{n}
} for
n>0
(
n<0
) play the role of annihilation (creation) operators on the eigenstates of
\mathcal{N}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
. The Hilbert space of the theory can be spanned by the eigenstates of
\mathcal{N}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
as basis. A generic eigenstate of
\mathcal{N}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
can be constructed from the oscillator vacuum defined in (
324
) as

\ket{r,s}=\sum_{j}\rho_{j}\Bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{p}C^{a_{i}}_{-m_{i}}\prod_{j=1}^{q}\tilde{C}^{b_{j}}_{-n_{j}}\Bigg)_{j}\ket{0,k^{\mu}}_{c}.

(326a)

r=\sum_{i}^{p}a_{i}m_{i},\hskip 22.76219pts=\sum_{i}^{q}b_{i}n_{i}.

(326b)

In the above,
a_{i}
and
b_{j}
respectively are powers of the
C_{-m_{i}}
and
\tilde{C}_{-n_{j}}
. The eigenvalues of state
\ket{r,s}
with
\mathcal{N}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}
are
r
and
s
respectively.
\rho_{j}
is polarisation tensor appropriate for the state. We define level
l
of such state as
l=r+s
. These states are orthonormal by construction i.e.
\braket{r^{\prime},s^{\prime}|r,s}=\delta_{r^{\prime}r}\delta_{s^{\prime}s}
.

Physical Hilbert space:

After constructing the Hilbert space, we would like to impose the physical state conditions (
322
) on them. Here we make the usual assumption that the oscillator vacuum is a physical state
21
21

21

However, this assumption is not always made. We will see in our analysis of flipped vacuum theory that the vacuum itself is no longer a physical state there.
to start with. Now, we apply the
L_{n}
physical state condition from (
322a
) on
\ket{phys}=\ket{phys^{\prime}}=\ket{0,k_{0}^{\mu}}
. For
n\neq 0
the sandwich conditions (
322a
) trivially satisfy due to the definition of vacuum (
324
). For
n=0
we have

\displaystyle\bra{0,k^{\mu}_{0}}L_{0}\ket{0,k_{0}^{\mu}}=a_{L}=0.

(327)

Hence, the oscillator vacuum satisfies the entire set of
L_{n}
physical state conditions given in (
322a
). Applying the
L_{0}
physical state condition on a generic state
\ket{r,s}
, and further putting
a_{L}=0
, we get

\begin{split}\bra{r,s}L_{0}\ket{r,s}=\bra{r,s}\big(&\mathcal{N}-\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}\big)\ket{r,s}=r-s=0\\
\implies&r=s.\end{split}

(328)

This is nothing but the level matching condition for
\ket{r,s}
, which says only level matched states are physical here. We are still left with the sandwich conditions (
322a
) with
n\neq 0
to be satisfied. Using the expression of
L_{n}
in (
164a
), it is straightforward to show that
L_{n}
acts on
\ket{r,s}
as below (see
7
)

\displaystyle L_{n}\ket{r,s}=\ket{r-n,s}-\ket{r,s+n}.

(329)

That means if we operate
L_{n}
(
n\neq 0
) on a level matched state
r=s
, then we get linear combination of states
\ket{r-n,s}
and
\ket{r,s+n}
. None of these states are level matched, which means both states are orthogonal to any level-matched state and as a result, their inner product with level matched states vanish. Hence the condition (
322a
), sandwiched between two separate level matched physical states, having
n\neq 0
is automatically satisfied without imposing any additional constraint.

Similarly, using the expression of
M_{n}
from (
164b
) one can see that action of this operator on
\ket{r,s}
is given by

\displaystyle M_{n}\ket{r,s}=\ket{r-n,s}+\ket{r,s+n}+\sum_{m\neq 0}\ket{r+m,s+m+n}

(330)

Clearly,
M_{n}
with
n\neq 0
after acting on the level matched states will give an infinite combination of non-level matched states only and hence, once again, the corresponding physical state conditions in (
322b
) are trivially satisfied.

At this juncture, all we are left with is the
M_{0}
physical state condition from (
322b
), which will give us the mass of a level matched state
\ket{n,n}
. However, this part turns out to be trickier since the state
\ket{n,n}
is not an eigenstate of
M_{0}
, so the action is not straightforward. Following the analysis in
7
we split the operator
M_{0}
as expressed in (
323a
) into two parts; one part that is diagonalised in the basis
\ket{n,n}
via the number operators (denoted by
\mathcal{D}_{0}
), and the other part that cannot be diagonalised in that basis (denoted by
\mathcal{K}_{0}
). In terms of expressions:

M_{0}=\mathcal{D}_{0}+\mathcal{K}_{0},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{D}_{0}=c^{\prime}k^{2}+\mathcal{N}+\widetilde{\mathcal{N}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{K}_{0}=X+X^{\dagger}.

(331)

Now, we apply the
M_{0}
physical state condition as given via the sandwich in (
322b
) in this split form (
331
) on two physical level matched states on two sides

\displaystyle\bra{n^{\prime},n^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}_{0}\ket{n,n}+\bra{n^{\prime},n^{\prime}}\mathcal{K}_{0}\ket{n,n}=a_{M}\braket{n^{\prime},n^{\prime}|n,n}.

(332)

Since the diagonal part is readily evaluated, let us look at the
\mathcal{K}_{0}
sandwiched part above. We can rewrite it as

\displaystyle\bra{n^{\prime},n^{\prime}}\mathcal{K}_{0}\ket{n,n}=\bra{n^{\prime},n^{\prime}}X+X^{\dagger}\ket{n,n}=\bra{n^{\prime},n^{\prime}}\sum_{m\neq 0}C_{m}\cdot\tilde{C}_{m}\ket{n,n}.

(333)

In the last step above, we have used the expression of
X
as given in (
323b
), also the fact that

\displaystyle X^{\dagger}=\sum_{m>0}C_{-m}\cdot\tilde{C}_{-m}=\sum_{m<0}C_{m}\cdot\tilde{C}_{m}.

For
n^{\prime}=n
, things are easier to understand and we see that

\displaystyle\bra{n,n}\mathcal{K}_{0}\ket{n,n}=0.

(334)

In the above we used the fact that the states
\ket{n,n}
are orthonormal to each other, and

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{0}\ket{n,n}=\sum_{m\neq 0}C_{m}\cdot\tilde{C}_{m}\ket{n,n}=\sum_{m\neq 0}\ket{n-m,n-m}.

(335)

Now, let us consider the final bit, i.e. the matrix elements of
\mathcal{K}_{0}
given in (
333
) with
n^{\prime}\neq n
. Equation (
335
) implies that for
n^{\prime}\neq n
, there must be a state in the summation at the r.h.s. for which
n-m=n^{\prime}
. Consequently, the matrix element will be

\displaystyle\bra{n^{\prime},n^{\prime}}\mathcal{K}_{0}\ket{n,n}\sim\delta^{D}(k^{\prime}-k).

(336)

The implication is that this matrix element will vanish only if the momentum of the states
\ket{n^{\prime},n^{\prime}}
and
\ket{n,n}
are different. For that case we will have

\displaystyle\bra{n^{\prime},n^{\prime}}\mathcal{D}_{0}\ket{n,n}=a_{M}\braket{n^{\prime},n^{\prime}|n,n}.

(337)

We know that the operator
\mathcal{D}_{0}
is diagonalized in the basis
\ket{n,n}
. Taking that into account we are allowed to impose the following condition on the state
\ket{n,n}

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{0}\ket{n,n}=a_{M}\ket{n,n}

(338)

Using the expression of
\mathcal{D}_{0}
as given in (
331
) and the facts that
k^{2}=-m^{2}
and
a_{M}=2
, we find the following mass of
\ket{n,n}
.

\displaystyle m^{2}\ket{n,n}=\frac{1}{c^{\prime}}(2n-2)\ket{n,n}.

(339)

Once we impose this mass shell condition on
\ket{n,n}
, it will automatically ensure that the states
\ket{n,n}
and
\ket{n^{\prime},n^{\prime}}
for
n\neq n^{\prime}
will essentially have different momentum (since their mass
m^{2}=-k^{2}
are different), which consequently ensures the condition
\bra{n^{\prime},n^{\prime}}\mathcal{K}_{0}\ket{n,n}=0
. Hence, the mass shell condition (
339
) is equivalently consistent with the
M_{0}
physical state condition.

11.2
Induced Vacuum

This quantum theory for tensionless strings constructed on induced vacuum is based on physical states outlined in (
249b
). The physical state condition to be satisfied here can be written in terms of the genrators:

\displaystyle\bra{phys^{\prime}}L_{n}\ket{phys}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\forall n,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ M_{n}\ket{phys}=0,\hskip 14.22636pt\forall n\neq 0.

(340)

One can see that the physical state condition outlined in (
340
) exactly aligns with the condition (
117
). This clearly shows us that the physical states in this theory, in fact, come from the direct tensionless/worldsheet Carrollian limit of the physical states of the tensile Bosonic string theory. We will clearly show this below.

The induced vacuum comes from the direct Carrollian limit of the tensile bosonic string vacuum. To see this, recall the vacuum in tensile bosonic closed string theory defined via highest weight conditon is written as:

\displaystyle\alpha^{\mu}_{n}\ket{0,k^{\mu}}_{\alpha}=\tilde{\alpha}^{\mu}_{n}\ket{0,k^{\mu}}_{\alpha}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\forall n>0.

(341)

Now, let us further recall how oscillators
\{A,B\}
are connected to
\{\alpha,\tilde{\alpha}\}
through the Carroll limit from (
171
). Inverting (
171
) and using the same in (
341
) we get the condition:

\displaystyle\Big(\sqrt{\epsilon}A_{n}^{\mu}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}B_{n}^{\mu}\Big)\ket{0,k^{\mu}}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=0

(342a)

\displaystyle\Big(-\sqrt{\epsilon}A_{-n}^{\mu}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}B_{-n}^{\mu}\Big)\ket{0,k^{\mu}}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=0\qquad\forall n>0.

(342b)

Taking tensionless or worldsheet Carrollian limit (
\epsilon\to 0
) on (
342a
) gives us the following conditions

\displaystyle B^{\mu}_{n}\ket{0,k^{\mu}}_{I}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\forall n\neq 0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ B^{\mu}_{0}\ket{0,k^{\mu}}_{I}=\sqrt{2c^{\prime}}k^{\mu}\ket{0,k^{\mu}}_{I}.

(343)

Using the expression of
M_{n}
s in terms of
B_{n}
s as given in (
154
), it is straightforward to show that this state satisfies (
340
), and hence, is a physical state. The action of
M_{0}
on this state will give us the mass of the state. In the light of the fact that
B
’s commute with each other, it is obvious that the normal ordering constant
a_{M}
vanishes for this theory. Keeping this is mind we rewrite the
M_{0}
physical state condition as:

\displaystyle M_{0}\ket{0,k^{\mu}}_{I}=\sum_{n}B_{-n}\cdot B_{n}\ket{0,k^{\mu}}_{I}=\left(\sum_{n\neq 0}B_{-n}\cdot B_{n}+B^{2}_{0}\right)\ket{0,k^{\mu}}_{I}=0.

(344)

Using (
343
) in (
344
), we get

\displaystyle B^{2}_{0}\ket{0,k^{\mu}}_{I}=2c^{\prime}k^{2}\ket{0,k^{\mu}}_{I}=0.

(345)

Keeping in mind the mass shell
k^{2}=-m^{2}
, one can see that the induced vacuum described above is massless.
We are yet to apply the sandwich physical state conditions involving
L_{n}
s on this state. Applying those conditions on the induced vacuum
\ket{0,k^{\mu}}_{I}
one gets

\displaystyle\bra{0,k^{\mu}}L_{n}\ket{0,k^{\mu}}=\bra{0,k^{\mu}}A_{n}\cdot B_{0}\ket{0,k^{\mu}}=c^{\prime}k\cdot\bra{0,k^{\mu}}A_{n}\ket{0,k^{\mu}}=0.

(346)

The periodicity condition,
A^{\mu}_{0}=0
, implies that the the induced vacuum trivially satisfies the
L_{0}
sandwich physical state condition.
The fact that the induced vacuum is massless can be understood from limiting perspective as well. We recall that the tensile string vacuum is tachyonic with the following mass

\displaystyle m^{2}\ket{0,k}_{\alpha^{\prime}}=-\frac{4}{\alpha^{\prime}}\ket{0,k}_{\alpha^{\prime}}.

(347)

At tensionless limit (
\alpha\to c^{\prime}/\epsilon
,
\epsilon\to 0
) this mass eigenvalue vanishes, confirming the mass derived earlier intrinsically.

11.3
Flipped Vacuum

The quantum theory for tensionless strings constructed on the flipped vacuum has physical states outlined in (
249a
). Let us rewrite that below including the
L_{0}
and
M_{0}
conditions

\displaystyle(L_{n}-a_{L}\delta_{n,0})\ket{phys}=(M_{n}-a_{M}\delta_{n,0})\ket{phys}=0\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \forall\penalty\ n\geq 0.

(348)

We have already seen earlier that for this theory to be Lorentz invariant in target spacetime, we must impose
a_{L}=2
and
a_{M}=0
. In the upcoming discussion, the same will be shown from the limiting analysis from its parent tensile counterpart.

One can easily identify that (
348
) is identical to the highest weight representation of BMS
3
as given in (
118
). This is a clear indication of the fact that the flipped vacuum theory which we are going to study here (which is constructed on the highest weight representation of BMS
3
) comes from the tensionless (
\epsilon\to 0
) limit of the twisted string theory; a fact that will be more explicit in the upcoming discussions.

Hilbert Space Structure:

The flipped vacuum defined in terms of new set of oscillators {
C,\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
} is

\displaystyle C^{\mu}_{n}\ket{0,k}_{A}=\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\mu}_{n}\ket{0,k}_{A}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\forall n>0.

(349)

In the above, the oscillator
C_{n}
is same as that used in the study of oscillator vacuum while
\tilde{\mathcal{C}_{n}}
is defined as

\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{n}=\tilde{C}_{-n}.

(350)

The implication of (
350
) is that, in the ‘tilde’ sector, the creation and annihilation operators flip their roles.
The mode expansion of
X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)
as given in (
161
) can be rewritten in terms of {
C,\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
} as

\displaystyle X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=x^{\mu}+2\sqrt{\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}}C^{\mu}_{0}\tau+i\sqrt{\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\left[(C^{\mu}_{n}-\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\mu}_{-n})-in\tau(C^{\mu}_{n}+\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\mu}_{-n})\right]e^{-in\sigma}.

(351)

The zero modes are given by
C^{\mu}_{0}=\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\mu}_{0}=\sqrt{\frac{c^{\prime}}{2}}k^{\mu}
. The commutation relations of these new oscillators are given by

\displaystyle[C^{\mu}_{m},C^{\nu}_{n}]=m\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta_{m+n}\hskip 14.22636pt[\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\mu}_{m},\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\nu}_{n}]=-m\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta_{m+n},\hskip 14.22636pt[C^{\mu}_{m},\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\nu}_{n}]=0.

(352)

Now, we shall write the generators {
L_{n},M_{n}
} in terms of the oscillators {
C,\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
}

\begin{split}L_{n}&=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}\left[C_{-m}\cdot C_{m+n}-\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{m}\cdot\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{-m+n}\right],\\
M_{n}&=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}\left[C_{-m}\cdot C_{m+n}+\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{m}\cdot\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{-m+n}+2C_{-m}\cdot\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{m+n}\right].\end{split}

(353)

Hence,
L_{0}
and
M_{0}
take the following form, which is closely related to the ones in (
323a
)

L_{0}=\mathcal{N}+\bar{\mathcal{N}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ M_{0}=c^{\prime}k^{2}+\mathcal{N}-\bar{\mathcal{N}}+X+Y.

(354)

where
\mathcal{N},\bar{\mathcal{N}},X
and
Y
are defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}=\sum_{m>0}C_{-m}\cdot C_{m},\qquad\bar{\mathcal{N}}=-\sum_{m>0}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{-m}\cdot\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{m}

(355a)

\displaystyle\penalty\ X=\sum_{m>0}C_{-m}\cdot\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{m},\qquad Y=\sum_{m>0}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{-m}\cdot C_{m}.

(355b)

Here again, the operators {
\mathcal{N},\bar{\mathcal{N}}
} are number operators and the Hilbert space is spanned by the eigenstates of them. The eigenstates are constructed by acting creation operators {
C_{-n},\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{-n}
} on the flipped vacuum defined in (
349
) much in the same way as in (
326a
).

Physical Hilbert space:

Let us first look into the
L_{0}
constraint from (
348
). Using the expression of
L_{0}
as given in (
354
), we get the following

\displaystyle(\mathcal{N}+\bar{\mathcal{N}}-a_{L})\ket{phys}=0.

(356)

In the light of the fact that
a_{L}=2
for this theory (this comes from both intrinsic as well as limiting perspective) only states with
r+s=2
(
r,s
are eigenvalues of
\mathcal{N},\bar{\mathcal{N}}
respectively) can be physical. The implication is that, for this theory, the flipped vacuum (
349
) itself is no longer a physical state! It is important to note, however, that, this is true only if the target spacetime is non-compact. In upcoming sections we shall see that compactification allows states of other levels to be physical state too.

The most general state with
r+s=2
is given by

\begin{split}\ket{2}&=a_{\mu}C^{\mu}_{-2}\ket{0}_{A}+e_{\mu\nu}C^{\mu}_{-1}C^{\nu}_{-1}\ket{0}_{A}+h_{\mu\nu}C^{\mu}_{-1}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\nu}_{-1}\ket{0}_{A}\\
&+b_{\mu}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\mu}_{-2}\ket{0}_{A}+f_{\mu\nu}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\mu}_{-1}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\nu}_{-1}\ket{0}_{A}+j_{\mu\nu}C^{\mu}_{-1}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\nu}_{-1}\ket{0}_{A}.\end{split}

(357)

In the above we have assumed
h_{\mu\nu}
(
j_{\mu\nu}
) to be symmetric (antisymmetric). The physical state conditions (
348
) impose constraints on the coefficients in (
357
). For any state with
r+s=2
, the conditions in (
348
) with
n>2
will be trivially satisfied and hence, would not add any constraint on the coefficient. The
L_{0}
condition has already been used. Hence we have five more non-trivial physical state conditions left, i.e. the
M_{0}
,
L_{1}
,
M_{1}
,
L_{2}
and
M_{2}
conditions.
Using these conditions, one gets the following constraints:

a_{\mu}=b_{\mu}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ e_{\mu\nu}=f_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{2}h_{\mu\nu},\penalty\ \penalty\ e_{\mu\nu}k^{\mu}=j_{\mu\nu}k^{\mu}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ k^{2}=0.

(358)

Hence the final level 2 physical state becomes

\ket{2}=e_{\mu\nu}\Big[C^{\mu}_{-1}C^{\nu}_{-1}\ket{0}_{A}+2C^{\mu}_{-1}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\nu}_{-1}\ket{0}_{A}+\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\mu}_{-1}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\nu}_{-1}\ket{0}_{A}\Big]+j_{\mu\nu}C^{\mu}_{-1}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\nu}_{-1}\ket{0}_{A}.

(359)

The constraint
k^{2}=0
in (
358
) implies that the physical state (
359
) is massless. This state has vanishing norm and can be identified as CCA null state having weight
h_{L}=2
,
h_{M}=0
discussed in
11
.

Limiting analysis of tensile states:

We already saw evidence of the fact that the tensionless flipped theory comes from tensionless limit of twisted string theory. In this section that will be demonstrated more explicitly. As shown in
129
, twisted string theory too, allows only level 2 states as physical states. However, those states have non-zero norm. Hence, the idea of non-null physical states evolving into null states at tensionless limit might sound strange. However, in
7
, this evolution has been explained. The discussion in this section follows the analysis given in
7
.

It is important to highlight here first that for tensile twisted string theory too, in the right moving sector the role of creation and annihilation operators flip. The vacuum for this theory is defined as

\displaystyle\alpha^{\mu}_{n}\ket{0,k^{\mu}}_{A}=\tilde{\alpha}^{\mu}_{-n}\ket{0,k^{\mu}}_{A}=0\hskip 14.22636pt\forall n>0.

(360)

We define the creation operator in the left moving sector of this theory as

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}_{-n}=-\tilde{\alpha}^{\mu}_{n}.

(361)

Let us consider the following state from the physical state spectrum of twisted string theory.

\displaystyle\ket{\Psi}=\sigma_{\mu\nu}\alpha^{\mu}_{-1}\bar{\alpha}^{\nu}_{-1}\ket{0}_{A}.

(362)

Now, we recall from (
173
) how the tensile operators {
\alpha,\tilde{\alpha}
} are connected to tensionless operators {
C,\tilde{C}
}. Using that relation and taking into account (
349
) and (
361
), one can see the evolution of the state (
362
) at tensionless limit.

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\ket{\Psi}=\sigma_{\mu\nu}\Big[\cosh{\theta}\hskip 2.84526ptC^{\mu}_{-1}-\sinh{\theta}\hskip 2.84526pt\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\mu}_{-1}\Big]\Big[\sinh{\theta}\hskip 2.84526ptC^{\nu}_{-1}-\cosh{\theta}\hskip 2.84526pt\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\nu}_{-1}\Big]\ket{0}_{A}=\ket{\phi_{1}}+\ket{\phi_{2}}+\ket{\phi_{3}}.

(363)

In the above, we have defined

\cosh\theta=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{\epsilon}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right),\qquad\sinh\theta=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sqrt{\epsilon}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right).

(364)

\ket{\phi_{1}},\ket{\phi_{2}}
and
\ket{\phi_{3}}
respectively are

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\epsilon}{2\sqrt{2}}\sigma_{\mu\nu}\left[C^{\mu}_{-1}C^{\nu}_{-1}-2C^{\mu}_{-1}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\nu}_{-1}+\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\mu}_{-1}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\nu}_{-1}\right]\ket{0}_{A},

(365a)

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\epsilon\sqrt{2}}\sigma_{\mu\nu}\left[C^{\mu}_{-1}C^{\nu}_{-1}+2C^{\mu}_{-1}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\nu}_{-1}+\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\mu}_{-1}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\nu}_{-1}\right]\ket{0}_{A},

(365b)

\displaystyle\ket{\phi_{3}}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\mu\nu}\left[C^{\mu}_{-1}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\nu}_{-1}-C^{\nu}_{-1}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\mu}_{-1}\right]\ket{0}_{A}.

(365c)

One can check that, all these three states have vanishing norm. However, the norm of
\ket{\Psi}
is preserved because
\braket{\phi_{1}|\phi_{2}}\neq 0
and that is where the non-vanishing part of
\braket{\Psi|\Psi}
comes. Taking that into account one can calculate the norm of
\ket{\Psi}
as below

\displaystyle\braket{\Psi|\Psi}=\sigma^{\mu\nu}\sigma_{\mu\nu}.

(366)

It is interesting to note from (
365
) and (
359
) that the state
\ket{\phi_{1}}
doesn’t correspond to allowed form of physical states (
359
) derived from intrinsic perspective. When we look into
\epsilon\to 0
limit, this state
\ket{\phi_{1}}
contains a
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
term and hence, gets suppressed. The remaining states correspond to (
359
) and they form the null state.

Connection to ambitwistors

The limited mass spectrum with only the level two massless states and origin in an unfamiliar twisted string theory may seem like the Flipped null string is a rather exotic and uninteresting object. It turns out to be quite the opposite. This theory is intimately connected to the bosonic version of the Ambitwistor string
139
and this connection was first established in
68
.

The Ambitwisor string is famously the worldsheet origin of the Cachazo–He–Yuan (CHY) formulae
57
. The CHY formulae capture the tree level amplitudes for massless particles (gluons, gravitons). The Ambitwistor partition function localizes completely and ambitwistor string amplitudes at tree level precisely give the CHY formulae. As just mentioned, the Ambitwistor string in just the null string in the flipped vacuum. With the restriction of
h_{M}=0
and
c_{M}=0
, the 2d Conformal Carroll algebra is known to exhibit a truncation to a chiral CFT
25
;
23
. The ambitwisor string seems to use this truncation to move from a null string to a chiral string
67
. Since this is a discussion which will take us a bit away from our focus in this review, we will not address this in the main body of our article. However, we provide a detailed summary of this connection between the Ambitwistor string and the null string in Appendix
E
.

11.4

We have explored various features of the three quantum null strings in this and the previous sections. We now provide a quick summary of these theories built on the three different vacua.
