In this framework, the saddle point on the string worldsheet is mapped to a point on the celestial sphere, and each subleading correction in
1/\alpha^{\prime}
maps to a corresponding correction in the large energy expansion of the celestial amplitude. This establishes a link between the
2d
worldsheet description of strings and
2d
conformal structure underlying celestial holography.

From the tensionless string theory point of view, this framework provides a clear physical interpretation of the high energy expansion. In the high energy or tensionless limit (
\alpha^{\prime}\to\infty
), the string spectrum contains an infinite tower of states that become light, including higher spin fields, so that the usual effective field theory description breaks down. The leading saddle-point contribution captures the classical dynamics of the tensionless string, while subleading corrections encode the contributions of these light higher-spin modes. The fact that this structure is mirrored in the celestial description suggests that 2D conformal symmetry of the string worldsheet, which contracts to BMS
3
(or Conformal Carrollian) symmetry in the tensionless regime may be realized as the symmetry of the celestial CFT governing flat-space scattering, thereby linking worldsheet dynamics, higher-spin physics, and celestial holography in a unified framework.

16
Conclusions

We have taken the reader on a journey in this review, starting out in the 1970s with the seminal work of Schild and the early developments of the null strings, through to the fundamentally important work of ILST, which formed the basis of a lot of the more recent developments, and finally in the main body of our review, we saw that the emergent symmetries on the worldsheet where Conformal Carroll symmetries and their supersymmetric cousins and these underlying symmetry structures helped build the theories of closed and open null tensionless (super)strings and the myriad of novel quantum phenomena that emerged from them.

It should be very clear to anyone reading our review and familiar with usual tensile string theory that there is a vast ocean of things that one needs to address in order to get the theory of null strings anywhere close to its tensile parent. We will thus end our review with a list of the most pressing questions that we believe need to answered right away.

Open questions

•

Quantisation of bosonic closed null strings:
The careful canonical quantisation of closed bosonic null strings yielded the three vacua of the null strings on which three different quantum theory were built. We have reviewed how all of them seem to be consistent in the correct number of dimensions by the closing of the Poincare algebra in lightcone quantisation. However, a more careful analysis in terms of path integrals and BRST methods is warranted to make our claims completely airtight. As we have also reviewed briefly in Sec.
15.4
, there has been an attempt at path integral quantisation, but this led to some odd properties like problems with defining the path integral for the oscillator vacuum and a set of curious dimensions for the null superstring,
which possibly indicates some subtleties about the null string in the path integrals that need reexamination. A BRST analysis would of course be even more robust. It may be useful to take a closer look at this in the light of the modern language of Carrollian worldsheet structures.

•

Quantising bosonic open null strings:
The theory of open null strings is very new and demands a careful quantisation. It would be interesting to see if all the vacua of the closed strings remain consistent for the open strings or not. Here it is imperative to also explore the different types of open boundary conditions we encountered. We only dealt with the Dirichlet open strings and it is natural to ask what differences one encounters for the Neumann and particularly the null boundary condition. Even in the context of the Dirichlet conditions, we encountered a new algebra, the one we called the Boundary Conformal Carroll algebra. One needs to study the representation of this carefully and build the quantum aspects of a theory with this as the underlying algebra. A recent analysis of aspects of the representation theory of the BCCA can be found in
55
. Clearly there is a lot to be understood here.

•

D-branes in tensionless string theory:
A closely related question is what are D-branes in null string theory. Given that the different vacua arise as D-branes of the original theory, the interpretations and indeed the construction of D-branes should be a rich and layered question. Also, we have seen a closed to open transition in the tensionless limit. It would be of interest to understand what the open-closed duality means in this context.

•

Quantising closed and open null superstrings:
All our comments from above about the quantisation of both closed and open strings hold when we move to superstring. The canonical quantisation itself is a difficult task as finding the various different vacua would mean hunting through many more possibilities. The previous questions about open strings and D-branes are also very important in this context.

•

Dualities in tensionless strings:
The five different tensile superstrings are connected by a web of dualities. It is but natural to ponder what happens to the tensionless version of this web. From the bosonic quantum structure of the null theory, it seems that the web will get a lot more intricate.

•

Connections to high energy scattering:
One of the principle motivations for attempting to understand the tensionless limit of string theory is to get a handle on the very high energy behaviour of the theory. From the seminal work of Gross and Mende, we know of the simplifications in this limit and the structures of the string amplitudes at very high energies. What has been missing is the construction of worldsheet methods that would lead to this very high energy sector directly. The worldsheet theory of the null string holds great promise in this regard. The key question is the construction of vertex operators in the null string. Vertex operators for a Carrollian scalar in the highest weight vacuum were constructed in
111
(see also
72
). But for the Gross-Mende analysis of the null string, one needs to consider vertex operators in the induced vacuum, which is the natural limit from the tensile theory. This should lead to interesting differences
26
.

•

Connections to flat holography:
Consider a theory of tensionless strings in three dimensional asymptotically flat spacetimes (AFS). The symmetries on worldsheet and the symmetries at the null boundary of spacetime are the same, - BMS
3
(or 2d conformal Carroll). In a manner similar to
98
;
128
, one could envision connecting worldsheet symmetries to the spacetime symmetries and thereby connect null strings with holography in 3d AFS. A different path has been proposed in
127
by connecting high string amplitudes to the 2d Celestial CFT dual for 4d AFS. See also an interesting proposal of connecting strings in AdS
3
to 2d Celestial CFTs
37
.

•

Hagedorn physics:
Another very important place where tensionless strings would become crucial is in the context of the Hagedorn transition in string theory. We have previously speculated that the appearance of the open null string in the tensionless limit is the signature of the formation of the very long string which becomes thermodynamically favourable when heating up a gas of closed strings to near the Hagedorn temperature. Although there have been tantalizing hints of this, it would be important to pin down a concrete calculation to show the natural emergence of Hagedorn physics in the tensionless string.

These are of course only the very immediate goals of the tensionless strings programme. Since there have been surprises at every turn, with each new calculation, we expect new physics to appear, especially in the magical world of the quantum null string. It seems this is an exciting journey that has only just begun.

APPENDICES

Appendix A
Review of tensile open strings

In this Appendix, we provide a concise discussion on the tensile bosonic open strings, whose worldsheet has boundaries at
\sigma=0
and
\sigma=\pi
. To study the variation of the Polyakov action (
56
) for open strings, one also need to consider the contributions arising from these boundaries. Working in the conformal gauge (
60
), variation of the action with respect to
X^{\mu}
yields

\begin{split}\delta_{X}S=T\int d\tau d\sigma\penalty\ \big(-\square X\cdot\delta X+\nabla\cdot(\nabla X\penalty\ \delta X)\big).\end{split}

(489)

The second term in the integral gives us the boundary term which takes the following form at
\sigma=0,\pi
boundary

\displaystyle T\int_{\mathcal{B}}dl_{\mathcal{B}}\penalty\ (\vec{n}\cdot\nabla X)\penalty\ \delta X=T\int d\tau\penalty\ X^{\prime}\cdot\delta X\Big|^{\sigma=\pi}_{\sigma=0}.

(490)

This term vanishes in two alternative ways,

1.

Dirichlet Condition:
In this case, we set
\delta X^{\mu}=0
at
\sigma=0,\pi
. Since
\sigma
has fixed values at both the boundaries this essentially means

\displaystyle\dot{X}^{\mu}\big|_{\sigma=0,\pi}=0.

(491)

Physically this means the string endpoints are fixed to a particular value of
X^{\mu}
.

2.

Neumann Condition:
In this case, we set

\displaystyle X^{\prime\mu}\big|_{\sigma=0,\pi}=0.

(492)

Physically this means the endpoints of the string are free to move along
X^{\mu}
directions.

One can also have mixed boundary condition where in one endpoint we have Dirichlet boundary condition and other end Neumann condition. However, in this review we only cover both side Dirichlet (DD) and both side Neumann (NN) boundary conditions which will be useful later.

Imposing Dirichlet condition (
\dot{X}^{\mu}=0
) on both boundaries
\sigma=0,\pi
on the solution of the wave equation as given in (
72
) gives the following

\displaystyle x^{\mu}=c^{\mu},\quad\alpha^{\mu}_{0}=0,\quad\alpha^{\mu}_{n}=-\tilde{\alpha}^{\mu}_{n}\qquad\forall n\neq 0.

(493)

Imposing Neumann condition
X^{\prime\mu}=0
on both boundaries
\sigma=0,\pi
on the solution of the wave equation as given in (
72
) gives the following

\displaystyle\alpha^{\mu}_{n}=\tilde{\alpha}^{\mu}_{n}\qquad\forall n.

(494)

Due to the identifications in (
493
) and (
494
) open string worldsheet theory only has one copy of Virasoro algebra given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}\alpha_{m}\cdot\alpha_{n-m}.

(495)

Appendix B
Superstring: RNS formalism

This appendix focuses on the study of RNS formalism of the tensile superstring. We begin with the action of tensile RNS superstring in superconformal gauge

\displaystyle S=\frac{T}{2}\int d^{2}\sigma\penalty\ (\eta^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\alpha}X^{\mu}\partial_{\beta}X_{\mu}+\bar{\psi}^{\mu}\rho^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\psi_{\mu}).

(496)

In the above,
\psi^{\mu}
are Majorana-Weyl spinors

\displaystyle\psi^{\mu}=\begin{bmatrix}\psi^{\mu}_{-}\\
\psi^{\mu}_{+}\end{bmatrix}.

(497)

\rho^{\alpha}
(
\alpha=0,1
) are the Dirac matrices in Majorana representation

\displaystyle\rho^{0}=\begin{bmatrix}0&&-1\\
1&&0\end{bmatrix},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \rho^{1}=\begin{bmatrix}0&&1\\
1&&0\end{bmatrix}.

(498)

These matrices follow the Clifford algebra in two dimensions

\displaystyle\{\rho^{\alpha},\rho^{\beta}\}=2\eta^{\alpha\beta}\mathbb{1}.

(499)

In the above,
\eta^{\alpha\beta}
is the gauge fixed worldsheet metric. For the sake of convenience, we rewrite the action (
496
) in terms of worldsheet lightcone coordinates
\sigma^{\pm}=\tau\pm\sigma
.

\displaystyle S=\frac{T}{2}\int d^{2}\sigma\penalty\ \left[2\partial_{+}X^{\mu}\partial_{-}X_{\mu}+i(\psi^{\mu}_{+}\partial_{-}\psi_{+\mu}+\psi^{\mu}_{-}\partial_{+}\psi_{-\mu})\right].

(500)

In the above
\partial_{\pm}=\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{\tau}\pm\partial_{\sigma})
. This action is invariant under the worldsheet superconformal transformation parametrized by
\xi
and
\epsilon
, under which the fields
X
and
\psi
transform as below

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}X

\displaystyle=\xi^{a}\partial_{a}X,\qquad\delta_{\xi}\psi_{\pm}=\xi^{a}\partial_{a}\psi_{\pm}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\pm}\xi^{\pm}\psi_{\pm},

(501a)

\displaystyle\delta_{\epsilon}X

\displaystyle=\bar{\epsilon}\psi,\qquad\delta_{\epsilon}\psi=-i\rho^{a}\partial_{a}X\epsilon.

(501b)

In the above,
\xi^{a}
and
\epsilon^{a}
satisfy the following conditions

\displaystyle\partial_{\pm}\xi^{\mp}=0,\qquad\partial_{\pm}\epsilon^{\mp}=0.

(502)

Using (
497
) and (
498
), one can rewrite the supersymmetry transformation as below

\delta_{\epsilon}X=\epsilon^{+}\,\psi_{-}+\epsilon^{-}\,\psi_{+},\quad\delta\psi_{-}=2\epsilon^{-}\partial_{-}X,\quad\delta\psi_{+}=-2\epsilon^{+}\partial_{+}X.

(503)

From (
500
) it is straightforward to derive the EOM for bosons as well as fermions

\displaystyle\partial_{-}\partial_{+}X^{\mu}=0,\qquad\partial_{+}\psi^{\mu}_{-}=0,\qquad\partial_{-}\psi^{\mu}_{+}=0.

(504)

The energy momentum tensor components and the supercurrents of the RNS action also vanish, which lead us to the following constraints

\displaystyle T_{\pm\pm}=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \partial_{\pm}X^{\mu}\partial_{\pm}X_{\mu}+\frac{i}{2}\psi^{\mu}_{\pm}\partial_{\pm}\psi_{\pm\mu}=0,

(505a)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ J_{\pm}=\psi_{\pm}\cdot\partial_{\pm}X=0.

(505b)

B.1
Closed superstrings

The EOM for bosonic field
X^{\mu}
as given in (
504
) yields the closed string mode expansion identical to (
73
). (
504
) it is apparent that
\psi^{+}
(
\psi^{-}
) are essentially functions of
\sigma^{+}
(
\sigma^{-}
). For closed string case, there can be two possible periodic boundary conditions for fermions given by

\displaystyle\psi^{\mu}_{+}(\tau,\sigma)=\pm\psi^{\mu}_{+}(\tau,\sigma+2\pi).

(506)

The boundary conditions with +ve sign lead to the Ramond sector while these with -ve sign lead to the Neveu-Schwarz (NS) sector. If both
\psi^{\mu}_{+}
and
\psi^{\mu}_{-}
belong to the NS sector then the mode expansion of them becomes

\displaystyle\psi^{\mu}_{+}(\tau,\sigma)=\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}b^{\mu}_{r}e^{-ir(\tau+\sigma)},\quad\psi^{\mu}_{-}(\tau,\sigma)=\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\tilde{b}^{\mu}_{r}e^{-ir(\tau-\sigma)}.

(507)

The oscillators
(b,\tilde{b})
satisfy the following algebra

\displaystyle\{b_{r}^{\mu},b_{s}^{\nu}\}=\{\tilde{b}^{\mu}_{r},\tilde{b}^{\nu}_{s}\}=\delta_{r+s}\eta^{\mu\nu},\qquad\{b_{r}^{\mu},\tilde{b}^{\nu}_{s}\}=0.

(508)

Using this mode expansion (
507
) along with the mode expansion of
X
given in (
73
) in the constraints in (
505
) one can find the super-Virasoro constraints in terms of the modes

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}\alpha_{-m}\cdot\alpha_{m+n}+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}(2r+n)b_{-r}\cdot b_{r+n},

(509a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{r}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}\alpha_{-m}\cdot b_{r+m}.

(509b)

The generators
\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n}
and
\bar{\mathcal{Q}}_{r}
depend on
(\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{b})
in the same way as above. Using the oscillator algebras of
(\alpha,\tilde{\alpha})
and
(b,\tilde{b})
, one obtains the (left moving) super-Virasoro algebra as,

\displaystyle[\mathcal{L}_{n},\mathcal{L}_{m}]

\displaystyle=(n-m)\mathcal{L}_{n+m}+\frac{c}{12}(n^{3}-n)\delta_{n+m,0},

(510a)

\displaystyle[\mathcal{L}_{n},\mathcal{Q}_{r}]

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{n}{2}-r\Big)\mathcal{Q}_{n+r},

(510b)

\displaystyle\{\mathcal{Q}_{r},\mathcal{Q}_{s}\}

\displaystyle=2\mathcal{L}_{r+s}+\frac{c}{3}\Big(r^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\Big)\delta_{r+s,0}.

(510c)

Similar relations are satisfied for (right moving) super-Virasoro generators
\bar{\mathcal{L}},\bar{\mathcal{Q}}
. Together the two sectors give two copies of super-Virasoro algebra.

B.2
Open superstrings

Having discussed the vanishing of the bosonic part for Dirichlet and Neumann boundary conditions earlier in Appendix
A
, here we shall see what happens in the case for fermions. We begin with the fermionic part of the action (
500
),

S_{F}\sim\int d\tau d\sigma\left(\psi_{+}\partial_{-}\psi_{+}+\psi_{-}\partial_{+}\psi_{-}\right),

(511)

where we have suppressed the Lorentz indices. The variation of this action results in

\delta S_{F}\sim\int d\tau\left(\psi_{-}\delta\psi_{-}-\psi_{+}\delta\psi_{+}\right)\Big|_{\sigma=0}^{\sigma=\pi}.

(512)

For open strings, the boundary terms in (
512
) must vanish separately at boundaries
\sigma=0,\pi
. This is achieved for

\psi_{+}(0)=\pm\psi_{-}(0),\qquad\psi_{+}(\pi)=\pm\psi_{-}(\pi).

(513)

In order to preserve spacetime Poincare invariance, we impose Neumann or Dirichlet boundary condition in analogy with the bosonic string. Without loss of generality, we choose
NN
and
DD
boundary conditions for the worldsheet fermions as

\displaystyle\text{NN:}\qquad\psi_{+}(0)=\,\psi_{-}(0)\,,\qquad\psi_{+}(\pi)=\eta\,\psi_{-}(\pi)\,,

(514a)

\displaystyle\text{DD:}\qquad\psi_{+}(0)=-\,\psi_{-}(0)\,,\qquad\psi_{+}(\pi)=-\eta\,\psi_{-}(\pi)\,,

(514b)

where
\eta=+1
labels the Ramond sector and
\eta=-1
corresponds to the NS sector of the open string. It is straightforward to show that the boundary conditions (
514a
) break half of the worldsheet supersymmetry. From (
501b
), the Dirichlet condition implies

\delta_{\epsilon}X\Big|_{\sigma=0,\pi}=0,\qquad\epsilon^{+}(0)\,=\epsilon^{-}(0)\,,\qquad\epsilon^{+}(\pi)=-\eta\epsilon^{-}(\pi)\,,

(515)

where,
\eta=\pm 1
label the two sectors as defined earlier. In this review, we focus on the Dirichlet boundary condition in the NS sector given by

\psi_{+}(0)=-\,\psi_{-}(0),\qquad\psi_{+}(\pi)=\psi_{-}(\pi).

(516)

Applying this condition to the mode expansions (
507
) yields the relation between left- and right- moving modes fermionic modes

\displaystyle\tilde{b}_{r}=-b_{r}.

(517)

Consequently, the mode expansions for the bosonic field
X^{\mu}
and fermions
\psi^{\mu}
(after imposing (
493
) and (
517
)) take the form

\displaystyle X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=x^{\mu}+\sqrt{2\alpha^{\prime}}\alpha_{0}^{\mu}\sigma+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{1}{n}\left[\alpha^{\mu}_{n}e^{-in(\tau+\sigma)}+\alpha^{\mu}_{-n}e^{in(\tau-\sigma)}\right],

(518a)

\displaystyle\psi^{\mu}_{+}(\tau,\sigma)=\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}b^{\mu}_{r}e^{-ir(\tau+\sigma)},\quad\psi^{\mu}_{-}(\tau,\sigma)=-\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}b^{\mu}_{r}e^{-ir(\tau-\sigma)}.

(518b)

Using (
518
) in the constraints (
505
), one obtains the super-Virasoro generators for open superstrings. We present these generators in a different basis (also defined for relativistic BCFT (
125
)), appropriate for separating the boundary compatible generators from the incompatible ones.

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}\alpha_{-m}\cdot\alpha_{m+n}+\frac{1}{4}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}(2r+n)b_{-r}\cdot b_{r+n},

(519a)

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{r}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}\alpha_{-m}\cdot b_{r+m}.

(519b)

These generators close to form a single copy of the super-Virasoro algebra

\displaystyle[\mathbb{L}_{n},\mathbb{L}_{m}]=(n-m)\mathbb{L}_{(m+n)}+\frac{\mathbb{c}}{12}n(n^{2}-1)\delta_{n+m,0},

(520a)

\displaystyle[\mathbb{L}_{n},\mathbb{Q}_{r}]=\Big(\frac{n}{2}-r\Big)\mathbb{Q}_{n+r},

(520b)

\displaystyle\{\mathbb{Q}_{r},\mathbb{Q}_{s}\}=2\mathbb{L}_{r+s}+\frac{\mathbb{c}}{3}\left(r^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\right)\delta_{r+s,0}.

(520c)

The generators in this new basis (
\mathbb{L},\mathbb{Q}
), are related to the standard left-right generators (
\mathcal{L},\mathcal{Q}
) as follows

\mathbb{L}_{n}=\mathcal{L}_{n}+\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n},\qquad\mathbb{Q}_{r}=\mathcal{Q}_{r}+\bar{\mathcal{Q}}_{r}.

(521)

With this we conclude our discussion of tensile superstring for both the closed and open sector.

Appendix C
Supersymmetric extension of Carroll CFT

In this appendix, we briefly recapitulate supersymmetric extensions of Carroll CFTs. We first look into the Carrollian superconformal field theories in manifolds without boundaries which would be applicable to the study of closed null superstrings and then we consider Carrollian superconformal field theories with boundaries. These would find application in the study of null open superstrings.

C.1
Carrollian superconformal field theories

In earlier studies, it was found that 2d CCA can be supersymmetrically extended in two different ways, namely to the Homogeneous and Inhomogeneous Superconformal Carroll Algebras (SCCA)
40
40

40

The homogeneous SCCA was first found by Gamboa et al in
94
. Inhomogeneous SCCA was first constructed in
8
.
. These algebras arise from two different Carrollian Inönü-Wigner contractions of the super-Virasoro algebra. Since the bosonic subalgebra is still the CCA in both cases, the difference in the contraction lies in the scaling of the supersymmetry and superconformal generators
41
41

41

An algebra isomorphic to the inhomogeneous SCCA was originally constructed in
138
as Galilean limit of super-Virasoro algebra. We have seen that in two dimensions, Galilean and Carrollian algebras are isomorphic. This isomorphism extends to the superconformal algebras as well.
. To understand this, we begin with the relativistic superconformal field theories (SCFTs) whose generators
\{\mathcal{L}_{n},\mathcal{Q}_{r},\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{n},\bar{\mathcal{Q}}_{r}\}
satisfy two copies super-Virasoro algebra as given in (
510
). For both R-sector (
r\in\mathbb{Z}
) and NS-sector (
r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
), the algebra has the form given in (
510
). We will now perform both contractions and construct the resulting superconformal algebras in the following.

Homogeneous SCCA:

Let us consider the following Inönü-Wigner contraction on the supe-Virasoro generators in (
510
)

\displaystyle L_{n}=\mathcal{L}_{n}-\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{-n},\quad M_{n}=\varepsilon(\mathcal{L}_{n}+\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{-n}),\quad Q^{+}_{r}=\sqrt{\varepsilon}\mathcal{Q}_{r},\quad Q^{-}_{r}=\sqrt{\varepsilon}\bar{\mathcal{Q}}_{r}.

(522)

This scaling is called homogeneous scaling since both
\mathcal{Q}^{+}_{r}
’s and
\mathcal{Q}^{-}_{r}
’s have been scaled in an identical manner. The resulting algebra is called the Homogeneous Superconformal Carrollian algebra (SCCA
H
). Its non-zero commutation relations are given by

\displaystyle[L_{n},L_{m}]=(n-m)L_{n+m}+\frac{c_{L}}{12}\,(n^{3}-n)\delta_{n+m,0},

\displaystyle[L_{n},M_{m}]=(n-m)M_{n+m}+\frac{c_{M}}{12}\,(n^{3}-n)\delta_{n+m,0},

(523)

\displaystyle[L_{n},Q^{\alpha}_{r}]=\Big(\frac{n}{2}-r\Big)Q^{\alpha}_{n+r},\quad\{Q^{\alpha}_{r},Q^{\beta}_{s}\}=\delta^{\alpha\beta}\left[M_{r+s}+\frac{c_{M}}{6}\Big(r^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\Big)\delta_{r+s,0}\right].

Here
\alpha
and
\beta
are used to designate
\pm
. As highlighted before, this algebra also appears as the asymptotic symmetry algebra at the null infinity of conventional
\mathcal{N}=2
supergravity in 3d asymptotically flat spacetimes
40
. In the main body of the review, we will encounter this algebra as the residual symmetry algebra of the homogeneous null superstring.

Inhomogeneous SCCA:

The super-Virasoro algebra also admits another kind of Carrollian Inönü-Wigner contraction where the supersymmetry generators are scaled in a manner similar to the bosonic Virasoro generators:

\displaystyle L_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{n}-\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{-n},\quad M_{n}=\varepsilon(\mathcal{L}_{n}+\bar{\mathcal{L}}_{-n}),

\displaystyle G_{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{r}-i\bar{\mathcal{Q}}_{-r},\quad K_{r}=\varepsilon(\mathcal{Q}_{r}+i\bar{\mathcal{Q}}_{-r}).

(524)

This scaling is called as inhomogeneous scaling since here the supersymmetry generators
G
and
K
are contracted in different manners. Notice the interesting factors of
i
in the combination of the supersymmetry generators. The resulting algebra is the inhomogeneous super Conformal Carrollian algebra (SCCA
I
). The non-zero commutation relations are given by

\displaystyle[L_{n},L_{m}]=(n-m)L_{n+m}+\frac{c_{L}}{12}(n^{3}-n)\delta_{n+m,0},

\displaystyle[L_{n},M_{m}]=(n-m)M_{n+m}+\frac{c_{M}}{12}(n^{3}-n)\delta_{n+m,0},

(525)

\displaystyle[L_{n},G_{r}]=\Big(\frac{n}{2}-r\Big)G_{n+r},\ [L_{n},K_{r}]=\Big(\frac{n}{2}-r\Big)K_{n+r},\ [M_{n},G_{r}]=\Big(\frac{n}{2}-r\Big)K_{n+r},

\displaystyle\{G_{r},G_{s}\}=2L_{r+s}+\frac{c_{L}}{3}\Big(r^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\Big)\delta_{r+s,0},\ \{G_{r},K_{s}\}=2M_{r+s}+\frac{c_{M}}{3}\Big(r^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\Big)\delta_{r+s,0}.

Comparing SCCA
I
in (
C.1
) to the SCCA
H
in (
C.1
) one sees that SCCA
I
has a richer structure (more non-zero commutations) in the SUSY sector. Like SCCA
I
, SCCA
I
too has been found as the symmetry algebra at the null infinity of an exotic theory of supergravity in 3d asymptotically flat spacetimes
136
. In the main text, this algebra arises as the residual gauge symmetries of the inhomogeneous tensionless superstring. The factors of
i
in the linear combinations mentioned above lead to some intriguing features of the parent theory, as we will mention in passing in the body of the article.

C.2
Superconformal Carroll CFT with boundaries

Following the recent work
19
, we now have a quick look into the Superconformal Carroll CFT with boundaries.

Homogeneous SCCA with boundaries:

We have seen in section
4.5
that after introducing boundaries in a system with conformal Carrollian symmetry, only a subalgebra of CCA
2
consisting of
\mathcal{O}_{n}
s and
\mathcal{P}_{n}
survive. In
19
, it has been shown that in presence of boundaries in a system with homogenous superconformal Carrollian symmetry, the following subalgebra of SCCA
H
survives

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{n}

\displaystyle=L_{n}-L_{-n},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{P}_{n}=M_{n}+M_{-n},\quad\mathcal{H}_{r}=Q^{+}_{r}+Q^{-}_{-r}.

(526)

They satisfy the following algebra

\begin{split}&[\mathcal{O}_{n},\mathcal{O}_{m}]=(n-m)\mathcal{O}_{n+m}-(n+m)\mathcal{O}_{n-m}\\
&[\mathcal{O}_{n},\mathcal{P}_{m}]=(n-m)\mathcal{P}_{n+m}+(n+m)\mathcal{P}_{n-m}+\frac{c_{M}}{12}(n^{3}-n)(\delta_{n+m}+\delta_{n-m})\\
&[\mathcal{O}_{r},\mathcal{H}_{s}]=\Big(\frac{r}{2}-s\Big)\mathcal{H}_{r+s}+\Big(\frac{r}{2}+s\Big)\mathcal{H}_{s-r}\\
&\{\mathcal{H}_{r},\mathcal{H}_{s}\}=\mathcal{P}_{r+s}+\frac{c_{M}}{3}(r^{2}-\frac{1}{4})\delta_{r+s,0},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ [\mathcal{H}_{r},\mathcal{P}_{s}]=[\mathcal{P}_{r},\mathcal{P}_{s}]=0.\\
\end{split}

(527)

This is the fully centrally extended version of the symmetry algebra we encounter while studying the homogeneous null open superstring in (
236
). This algebra can also be obtained from the the following Inönü-Wigner contraction of one copy of super-Virasoro algebra given in (
520
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{n}=\mathbb{L}_{n}-\mathbb{L}_{-n},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ P_{n}=\epsilon\penalty\ (\mathbb{L}_{n}+\mathbb{L}_{-n}),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{H}_{r}=\sqrt{\epsilon}\mathbb{Q}_{r},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \epsilon\to 0.

(528)

For realisation of this algebra in superspace the reader is referred to
19
.

Inhomogeneous SCCA with boundaries:

The work
19
also looked into the inhomogeneous SCCA in presence of boundaries, where the surviving generators are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{n}

\displaystyle=L_{n}-L_{-n},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{P}_{n}=M_{n}+M_{-n},\quad\mathcal{K}_{r}=K_{r}+K_{-r},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{Y}_{r}=K_{r}-K_{-r}.

(529)

These generators satisfy the following algebra

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{O}_{n},\mathcal{O}_{m}\right]=(n-m)\mathcal{O}_{n+m}-(n+m)\mathcal{O}_{n-m}

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{O}_{n},\mathcal{P}_{m}\right]=(n-m)\mathcal{P}_{n+m}+(n+m)\mathcal{P}_{n-m}+\frac{c_{M}}{12}(n^{3}-n)(\delta_{n+m}+\delta_{n-m})

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{O}_{n},\mathcal{K}_{r}\right]=\left(\frac{n}{2}-r\right)\mathcal{K}_{n+r}+\left(\frac{n}{2}+r\right)\mathcal{K}_{n-r}

(530)

\displaystyle\left[\mathcal{O}_{n},\mathcal{Y}_{r}\right]=\left(\frac{n}{2}-r\right)\mathcal{Y}_{n+r}-\left(\frac{n}{2}+r\right)\mathcal{Y}_{n-r}

\displaystyle\{\mathcal{K}_{r},\mathcal{K}_{s}\}=\{\mathcal{Y}_{r},\mathcal{Y}_{s}\}\;=\{\mathcal{Y}_{r},\mathcal{K}_{s}\}\;=\;[\mathcal{P}_{r},\mathcal{K}_{s}]=[\mathcal{P}_{r},\mathcal{Y}_{s}]\;=\;[\mathcal{P}_{n},\mathcal{P}_{m}]=0.

This algebra comes from the following Inönü-Wigner contraction of one copy of super-Virasoro algebra

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{n}=\mathbb{L}_{n}-\mathbb{L}_{-n},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ P_{n}=\epsilon\penalty\ (\mathbb{L}_{n}+\mathbb{L}_{-n}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{r}=\epsilon(\mathbb{Q}_{r}+\mathbb{Q}_{-r}),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{Y}_{r}=\epsilon(\mathbb{Q}_{r}-\mathbb{Q}_{-r}),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \epsilon\to 0.

(531)

Dropping
\mathcal{Y}_{r}
from the above algebra (
C.2
), the remaining version comes from the contraction of the Homogeneous algebra (
527
). This makes the Inhomogeneous BSCCA less interesting compared to the its Homogeneous version.

As of now, we do not have an open null superstring which realises this algebra, the BSCCA
I
as its worldsheet residual gauge symmetries.

Appendix D
Superspace formalism for tensile and tensionless strings

In this appendix we briefly discuss about the superspace realisation of tensile superstring followed by the same of homogeneous and inhomogeneous null superstrings. We also discuss how the superspace for homogeneous and inhomogeneous null superstrings can be obtained from appropriate limit of the superspace for tensile strings.

D.1
Tensile supertrings

In an
\mathcal{N}=(1,1)
superspace, which consists of worldsheet coordinates
\sigma^{\alpha}
(
\alpha=0,1
) and Grassmanian coordinates
\theta^{\pm}
42
42

42

In this section, for convenience, we denote
\pm
to express holomorphic and antiholomorphic coordinates (both
\sigma
and
\theta
) as well as holomorphic and antiholomorphic super-Virasoro generators.
, one can define a general superfield
Y
as

\displaystyle Y^{\mu}(\sigma^{\pm},\theta^{\pm})=X^{\mu}(\sigma^{\pm})+i\theta^{+}\psi^{\mu}_{+}(\sigma^{\pm})+i\theta^{-}\psi^{\mu}_{-}(\sigma^{\pm})+\frac{1}{2}\theta^{+}\theta^{-}B^{\mu}(\sigma^{\pm}).

(532)

In the study of on-shell supersymmetry the auxiliary field
B^{\mu}
can be set to zero. In this superspace, the RNS superstring action in (
496
) can be rewritten in terms of superspace integral of a Lagrangian constructed out of the superfield
Y

\displaystyle S=-\frac{T}{2}\int\penalty\ d^{2}\theta d^{2}\sigma\overline{D}Y^{\mu}DY_{\mu}.

(533)

In the above, the
D,\overline{D}
are supercovariant derivative.
The gauge symmetry transformations of the superstring action as described in (
501
) translate to the following transformations in terms of the superspace coordinates

\displaystyle\delta\sigma^{\pm}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\xi^{\pm}+i\epsilon^{\pm}\theta^{\pm},\ \ \ \delta\theta^{\pm}=\epsilon^{\pm}+\frac{1}{2}\theta^{\pm}\partial_{\pm}\xi^{\pm}.

(534)

Now we consider the variation of the superfield
Y
under the superconformal transformation which will help us finding its generators

\displaystyle\delta Y

\displaystyle=(\delta\sigma^{+}\partial_{+}+\delta\sigma^{-}\partial_{-}+\delta\theta^{+}\partial_{\theta^{+}}+\delta\theta^{+}\partial_{\theta^{-}})Y

\displaystyle=\Big[\Big(\xi^{+}(\sigma^{+})\partial_{+}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{+}\xi^{+}(\sigma^{+})\theta^{+}\partial_{\theta^{+}}\Big)+\epsilon^{+}(\sigma^{+})\Big(\partial_{\theta^{+}}+i\theta^{+}\partial_{+}\Big)

\displaystyle+\Big(\xi^{-}(\sigma^{-})\partial_{-}+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{-}\xi^{-}(\sigma^{-})\theta^{-}\partial_{\theta^{-}}\Big)+\epsilon^{-}(\sigma^{-})\Big(\partial_{\theta^{-}}+i\theta^{-}\partial_{-}\Big)\Big]Y

\displaystyle=[\mathcal{L}^{+}(\xi^{+})+\mathcal{Q}(\epsilon^{+})+\mathcal{L}^{-}(\xi^{-})+\bar{\mathcal{Q}}(\epsilon^{-})]Y.

(535)

Here
\mathcal{L}
’s and
\mathcal{Q}
’s respectively are bosonic and fermionic generators. From (
502
) it is manifest that
\xi^{+}
(
\xi^{-}
) and
\epsilon^{+}
(
\epsilon^{-}
) are essentially functions of
\sigma^{+}
(
\sigma^{-}
). They have the following Fourier expansion

\xi^{\pm}(\sigma^{\pm})=\sum_{n}a_{n}^{\pm}e^{in\sigma^{\pm}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \epsilon^{\pm}(\sigma^{\pm})=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}b_{r}^{\pm}e^{ir\sigma^{\pm}}.

(536)

Using (
536
), one can Fourier expand the generators
\mathcal{L}^{\pm}
, and
\mathcal{Q}^{\pm}
too

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\pm}(\xi^{\pm})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\sum_{n}a^{\pm}_{n}\mathcal{L}^{\pm}_{n},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{Q}^{\pm}(\epsilon^{\pm})=\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}b^{\pm}_{r}\mathcal{Q}^{\pm}_{r}.

(537)

Using (
537
) along with (
D.1
), one can obtain the following vector field representation of the superconformal generators in the superspace:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\pm}_{n}=ie^{in\sigma^{\pm}}\Big(\partial_{\pm}+\frac{in}{2}\theta^{\pm}\partial_{\theta^{\pm}}\Big)\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \mathcal{Q}^{\pm}_{r}=e^{ir\sigma^{\pm}}(\partial_{\theta^{\pm}}+i\theta^{\pm}\partial_{\pm}).

(538a)

These generators follow two copies of the Super-Virasoro algebra wthout central extension, non-vanishing commutators (and anti-commutators) of which are given bellow

\displaystyle[\mathcal{L}^{\pm}_{n},\mathcal{L}^{\pm}_{m}]=(n-m)\mathcal{L}^{\pm}_{n+m},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ [\mathcal{L}^{\pm}_{n},\mathcal{Q}^{\pm}_{r}]=\Big(\frac{n}{2}-r\Big)\mathcal{Q}^{\pm}_{n+r},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \{\mathcal{Q}^{\pm}_{r},\mathcal{Q}^{\pm}_{s}\}=2\mathcal{L}^{\pm}_{r+s}.

(539)

D.2
Homogeneous null superstrings

In order to understand the superspace for homogeneous null superstrings, let us first recall the residual symmetry of the gauge fixed homogenous null superstring action from (
197
)

\xi^{0}=f^{\prime}(\sigma)\tau+g(\sigma),\qquad\xi^{1}=f(\sigma),\qquad\epsilon^{\pm}=\epsilon^{\pm}(\sigma).

(540)

Just like for the case of tensile RNS superstrings, here too, we can clearly visualise the generators generating these transformation. In order to do so, here again, one has to realise the transformations (
197
) in a
\mathcal{N}=(1,1)
Carrollian superspace, consisting of two Grassmann coordinates
\theta
and
\bar{\theta}
along with the two world‑sheet coordinates
\{\sigma,\tau
}. This superspace is called homogeneous Carrollian superspace and in such superspace, the transformations (
197
) translate to the following
21
,

\displaystyle\sigma^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\sigma^{\prime a}=\sigma^{\alpha}+\xi^{\alpha}+\frac{i}{2}\epsilon^{+}V^{\alpha}\theta+\frac{i}{2}\epsilon^{-}V^{\alpha}\bar{\theta}

(541a)

\displaystyle\theta

\displaystyle\rightarrow\theta^{\prime}=\theta+\epsilon^{+}+\frac{1}{4}\theta\partial_{\alpha}\xi^{\alpha}

(541b)

\displaystyle\bar{\theta}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\bar{\theta}^{\prime}=\bar{\theta}+\epsilon^{-}+\frac{1}{4}\bar{\theta}\partial_{\alpha}\xi^{\alpha}

(541c)

Let us define a superfield
Y\left(\sigma^{\alpha},\theta,\bar{\theta}\right)
in this superspace

\displaystyle Y^{\mu}\left(\sigma^{\alpha},\theta,\bar{\theta}\right)=X^{\mu}\left(\sigma^{\alpha}\right)+i\theta\psi^{\mu}_{+}\left(\sigma^{\alpha},\theta,\bar{\theta}\right)+i\bar{\theta}\psi^{\mu}_{-}\left(\sigma^{\alpha},\theta,\bar{\theta}\right).

(542)

This superfield transforms under (
541
) as given below,

\delta Y=\left(\delta\tau\partial_{\tau}+\delta\sigma\partial_{\sigma}+\delta\theta\partial_{\theta}+\delta\bar{\theta}\partial_{\bar{\theta}}\right)Y.

(543)

Using (
197
) and (
541
) along with
V^{\alpha}=(1,0)
from (
190
) in (
543
) we end up with the following form of superfield transformation

\displaystyle\delta Y=

\displaystyle{\left[\left(f^{\prime}\tau+g+\frac{i}{2}\epsilon^{+}\theta+\frac{i}{2}\epsilon^{-}\bar{\theta}\right)\partial_{\tau}+f\partial_{\sigma}\right.}

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \left.+\left(\epsilon^{+}+\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime}\theta\right)\partial_{\theta}+\left(\epsilon^{-}+\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime}\bar{\theta}\right)\partial_{\bar{\theta}}\right]Y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\left[L(f)+M(g)+Q^{+}\left(\epsilon^{+}\right)+Q^{-}\left(\epsilon^{-}\right)\right]Y}.

(544)

Looking at this explicit form, one can easily identify superfield transformation generators,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&L(f)=f\partial_{\sigma}+f^{\prime}\left[\tau\partial_{\tau}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\theta\partial_{\theta}+\bar{\theta}\partial_{\bar{\theta}}\right)\right],\quad M(g)=g\partial_{\tau},\\
Q^{+}&\left(\epsilon^{+}\right)=\epsilon^{+}\left(\partial_{\theta}+\frac{i}{2}\theta\partial_{\tau}\right),\quad Q^{-}\left(\epsilon^{-}\right)=\epsilon^{-}\left(\partial_{\bar{\theta}}+\frac{i}{2}\bar{\theta}\partial_{\tau}\right).\end{split}

(545)

In the above,
L
and
M
are bosonic generators and
Q^{\pm}
are fermionic generators. Since the string is closed, the periodicty of the worldsheet in
\sigma
implies that the functions
f,g
and
\epsilon^{\pm}
are essentially periodic functions of
\sigma
and hence, can be Fourier expanded as

f=\sum_{n}a_{n}e^{in\sigma},\quad g=\sum_{n}b_{n}e^{in\sigma},\quad\epsilon^{\pm}=\sum_{n}\zeta_{n}^{\pm}e^{in\sigma}.

(546)

Substituting (
546
) back into (
545
), one can Fourier expand the generators
L
,
M
, and
Q^{\pm}
too

L(f)=-i\sum_{n}a_{n}L_{n},\quad M(g)=-i\sum_{n}b_{n}M_{n},\quad Q^{\pm}\left(\epsilon^{\pm}\right)=\sum_{n}\zeta_{n}^{\pm}Q_{n}^{\pm}.

(547)

One can identify the mode operator
L_{n}
,
M_{n}
and
Q^{\pm}
given as ,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&L_{n}=ie^{in\sigma}\Big[\partial_{\sigma}+in\tau\partial_{\tau}+\frac{in}{2}\big(\theta\partial_{\theta}+\bar{\theta}\partial_{\bar{\theta}}\big)\Big],\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ M_{n}=ie^{in\sigma}\partial_{\tau}\\
&Q^{+}_{r}=e^{ir\sigma}\Big(\partial_{\theta}+\frac{i}{2}\theta\partial_{\tau}\Big),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ Q^{-}_{r}=e^{ir\sigma}\Big(\partial_{\bar{\theta}}+\frac{i}{2}\bar{\theta}\partial_{\tau}\Big).\end{split}

(548)

These generators satisfy the homogeneous super‑Conformal Carrollian algebra (SCCA
H
), without the central extension.

\begin{split}&[L_{n},L_{m}]=(n-m)L_{n+m}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ [L_{n},M_{m}]=(n-m)M_{n+m}\\
&[L_{n},Q^{\alpha}_{r}]=\Big(\frac{n}{2}-r\Big)Q^{\alpha}_{n+r}\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \{Q^{\alpha}_{r},Q^{\alpha^{\prime}}_{s}\}=\delta^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}M_{r+s}\\
&[M_{m},M_{n}]=[M_{m},Q^{\alpha}_{r}]=0.\end{split}

(549)

These generators in the superspace can be derived from the limiting approaches as well. We recall from earlier discussion of homogeneous null superstrings in section
7.1
that for this theory both components of the worldsheet fermion
\psi_{\pm}
are scaled similarly i.e.
\psi_{\pm}\to\sqrt{\epsilon}\psi_{\pm}
. The same is reflected in the superspace as well; the scaling which takes us from relativistic superspace to the homogeneous Carrollian superspace involves scaling both the Grassmannian coordinates in same manner, i.e.

\displaystyle\tau\to\epsilon\tau,\qquad\sigma\to\sigma,\qquad\theta^{+}\to\sqrt{\epsilon}\theta,\qquad\theta^{-}\to\sqrt{\epsilon}\bar{\theta}.

(550)

Applying this scaling along with the Inönü-Wigner contraction (
522
) (
\mathcal{Q},\bar{\mathcal{Q}}
replaced by
\mathcal{Q}^{\pm}
) on the super-Virasoro generators given in (
538
) leads us to the generators in (
548
).

D.3
Inhomogeneous null superstrings

In order to understand the inhomogeneous tensionless superstrings from superspace perspective we first recall the residual gauge symmetry transformations of the action (
211
) from (
212
) and (
553
)

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}X=\xi^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}X,\qquad\delta_{\xi}\psi_{0}=\xi^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\psi_{0}+\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\alpha}\xi^{\alpha}\psi_{0},

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}\psi_{1}=\xi^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}\psi_{1}\penalty\ +\penalty\ \frac{1}{4}\partial_{\alpha}\xi^{\alpha}\psi_{1}\penalty\ +\penalty\ \frac{1}{2}(\partial_{1}\xi^{0})\psi_{0},

\displaystyle\delta X=i(\epsilon^{1*}\psi_{0}+\epsilon^{0*}\psi_{1}),\quad\delta\psi_{0}=-\epsilon^{1}\dot{X},\quad\delta\psi_{1}=-\epsilon^{0}\dot{X}-\epsilon^{1}X^{\prime},

\displaystyle\partial_{0}\xi^{0}=\partial_{1}\xi^{1},\qquad\partial_{0}\xi^{1}=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{0}\epsilon^{1*}=\partial_{0}\epsilon^{0}=\partial_{1}\epsilon^{1}=\partial_{1}\epsilon^{0*},

\displaystyle\partial_{0}\epsilon^{0*}=\partial_{0}\epsilon^{1}=0,\qquad\psi_{0}=\psi_{1}^{*}.

Now, since the fermions in this theory are complex, it is natural to choose complex Grassmannian coordinates in the inhomogeneous superspace.
Let us consider them to be {
\phi,\chi
}. The superfield for this case is given by

\displaystyle Y^{\mu}(\sigma^{\alpha},\phi,\chi)=X^{\mu}(\sigma^{\alpha})+i\phi\psi_{0}(\sigma^{\alpha})+i\chi\psi_{1}(\sigma^{\alpha}).

(551)

In this superspace the residual gauge symmetry transformations given in (
212
) translate to the following transformations

\displaystyle\delta\tau

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\xi^{0}+i(\epsilon^{0}\chi+\epsilon^{1}\phi),\qquad\delta\sigma=\xi^{1}+i\epsilon^{1}\chi,

(552a)

\displaystyle\delta\phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\epsilon^{0}+\frac{1}{4}(\partial_{0}\xi^{0}+\partial_{1}\xi^{1})\phi+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{1}\xi^{0}\chi,

(552b)

\displaystyle\delta\chi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\epsilon^{1}+\frac{1}{4}(\partial_{0}\xi^{0}+\partial_{1}\xi^{1})\chi.

(552c)

In the above
\xi^{\alpha}(\sigma,\tau)
and
\epsilon^{\alpha}(\sigma,\tau)
comes from the solution of the differential equations satisfied by them given in (
214
)

\displaystyle\xi^{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(\sigma)\tau+g(\sigma),\ \ \ \ \xi^{1}=f(\sigma),

(553a)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\prime}(\sigma)\tau+h(\sigma).\ \ \ \ \epsilon^{1}=e(\sigma).

(553b)

In the above,
e
and
h
are Grassmann-odd functions of
\sigma
having periodicity
2\pi
while
f
and
g
are Grassmann-even functions of
\sigma
with the same periodicity. Hence, the transformations in (
552
) ultimately become the following

\displaystyle\delta\tau

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f^{\prime}\tau+g+ie^{\prime}\tau\chi+ih\chi+ie\phi,\qquad\delta\sigma=f+ie\chi,

(554a)

\displaystyle\delta\phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\prime}\tau+h+\frac{f^{\prime}\phi}{2}+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}\tau\chi}{2}+\frac{g^{\prime}\chi}{2},\penalty\ \quad\delta\chi=e+\frac{f^{\prime}\chi}{2}.

(554b)

Variation of the superfield
Y
under this residual gauge transformation gives

\delta Y=(\delta\tau\partial_{\tau}+\delta\sigma\partial_{\sigma}+\delta\phi\partial_{\phi}+\delta\chi\partial_{\chi})Y=[L(f)+M(g)+G(e)+H(h)]Y,

(555)

where
L(f)
,
M(g)
,
G(e)
and
H(h)
take the following form

\displaystyle L(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[f\partial_{\sigma}+f^{\prime}\Big(\frac{\phi\partial_{\phi}+\chi\partial_{\chi}}{2}+\tau\partial_{\tau}\Big)+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}}{2}\tau\chi\partial_{\phi}\Big],

(556a)

\displaystyle M(g)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[g\partial_{\tau}+\frac{g^{\prime}}{2}\chi\partial_{\phi}\Big],

(556b)

\displaystyle G(e)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[e\partial_{\chi}+ie(\phi\partial_{\tau}+\chi\partial_{\sigma})+e^{\prime}\tau\partial_{\phi}+ie^{\prime}\chi\tau\partial_{\tau}\Big],

(556c)

\displaystyle H(h)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[h\partial_{\phi}+ih\chi\partial_{\tau}\Big].

(556d)

In the light of the fact that
f
,
g
,
e
and
h
are all periodic functions of
\sigma
having
2\pi
periodicity, they all can be Fourier expanded in terms of
e^{in\sigma}
. Applying their Fourier expansions in (
556
) one can find the Fourier modes of
L(f)
,
M(g)
,
G(e)
and
H(h)

\displaystyle L_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle ie^{in\sigma}\Big[\partial_{\sigma}+in\Big(\frac{\phi\partial_{\phi}+\chi\partial_{\chi}}{2}+\tau\partial_{\tau}\Big)-\frac{n^{2}}{2}\tau\chi\partial_{\phi}\Big],

(557a)

\displaystyle M_{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle ie^{in\sigma}\Big[\partial_{\tau}+\frac{in}{2}\chi\partial_{\phi}\Big],

(557b)

\displaystyle G_{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{ir\sigma}\Big[\partial_{\chi}+i(\phi\partial_{\tau}+\chi\partial_{\sigma})+ir\tau\partial_{\phi}-r\chi\tau\partial_{\tau}\Big],

(557c)

\displaystyle H_{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{ir\sigma}\Big[\partial_{\phi}+i\chi\partial_{\tau}\Big].

(557d)

These generators satisfy the inhomogeneous version of the superconformal Carrollian algebra

\displaystyle[L_{m},L_{n}]=(m-n)L_{m+n},\quad[L_{m},M_{n}]=(m-n)M_{m+n},

\displaystyle[L_{m},G_{r}]=\Big(\frac{m}{2}-r\Big)G_{m+r},\quad[M_{m},G_{r}]=\Big(\frac{m}{2}-r\Big)H_{m+r},\ \{G_{r},G_{s}\}=2L_{r+s},

\displaystyle[L_{m},H_{r}]=\Big(\frac{m}{2}-r\Big)H_{m+r},\quad\{G_{r},H_{s}\}=2M_{r+s}.

(558)

In order to understand this formulation from limiting perspective one needs to look into define complex superspace coordinates in the tensile superspace

\displaystyle\phi=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\theta^{+}-i\theta^{-}),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \chi=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\theta^{+}+i\theta^{-}).

(559)

To obtain inhomogeneous Carrollian superspace one needs to the following scaling on the coordinates

\displaystyle\tau\to\epsilon\tau,\qquad\sigma\to\sigma,\qquad\phi\to\epsilon\phi,\qquad\chi\to\chi.

(560)

This scaling along with the Inönü-Wigner contraction (
C.1
) of super-Virasoro generators (
538
) lead us to the generators (
557
).

Appendix E
Connection to Ambitwistor strings

In the previous decade, a series of seminal works by Cachazo-He-Yuan (CHY)
57
;
58
;
56
;
60
;
59
examined the tree-level scattering amplitudes of massless particles. Ambitwistor string theory proposed by Mason and Skinner
139
provided a string theoretic basis for these scattering equations. This version of string theory was originally formulated as holomorphic strings with constraint
\Pi^{2}=0
(
\Pi^{\mu}
is the momentum of the string). Target spacetime for this theory is ambitwistor space, the space of complex null geodesics in complex Minkowski space
139
;
97
. Ambitwistor string theory is often considered to be field theory limit (
\alpha^{\prime}\to 0
) of string theory
43
43

43

Ambitwistor string action has been obtained from chiral
\alpha^{\prime}\to 0
limit of tensile string action in
139
.
. Interestingly, however, the CHY formulas for Yang-Mills and gravity amplitudes bore striking resemblance to the Gross-Mende scattering amplitudes which correspond to the tensionless (
\alpha^{\prime}\to\infty
) limit of strings
105
;
106
. In
68
, Casali and Tourkine proposed an alternative formalism for ambitwistor strings as tensionless null strings which is consistent with the Gross-Mende limit. In subsequent work
67
, they extended their formalism by complexifying both worldsheet and the target spacetime, quantised their theory and obtained the CHY formula for tree level amplitudes. Here we briefly review their formulation of ambitwistor strings as null strings.

E.1
Ambitwistor string action from ILST action

Let us begin with rewriting the ILST action (
137
), using the vielbein density
V^{\alpha}
as given in (
136
)

\displaystyle S=\int d^{2}\sigma\penalty\ \frac{1}{\lambda}(\dot{X}-\rho X^{\prime})^{2}.

(561)

The conjugate momentum for this action is given by

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mu}=\frac{1}{\lambda}(\dot{X}^{\mu}-\rho X^{\prime\mu}).

(562)

Recalling from earlier analysis that
\lambda
and
\rho
act as Lagrange multipliers one can see that they impose the following constraints on the action

\displaystyle\Pi^{2}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \Pi_{\mu}X^{\prime\mu}=0.

(563)

Hence one can define the following phase space action which is equivalent to the null string action (
137
)

\displaystyle S=\int d^{2}\sigma\penalty\ (\Pi_{\mu}\dot{X}^{\mu}-\rho\Pi_{\mu}X^{\prime\mu}-\lambda\Pi^{2}).

(564)

In timelike gauge
\rho=0
and
\lambda=1
, we retrieve the gauge fixed action we have worked in so far. For
\rho=1
and
\lambda=0
gauge (called ambitwistor gauge) we arrive at the ambitwistor string action
44
44

44

For detailed discussion of the action of the form (
564
) in different gauges one can look into
12
.

\displaystyle S=\int d^{2}\sigma(\Pi_{\mu}\partial_{-}X^{\mu}).

(565)

However, the constraints accompanying this action (
563
) are different than that of ambitwistor strings, which are

\displaystyle\Pi^{2}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \Pi_{\mu}\partial_{+}X^{\mu}=0.

(566)

Fortunately, as discussed in
68
, the EOM corresponding to the action (
564
) is
\partial_{-}X=0
, using which on (
563
) one can find (
566
) in following way

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mu}\partial_{+}X^{\mu}=\Pi_{\mu}(\partial_{-}X^{\mu}+X^{\prime\mu})=0.

(567)

Hence, one can clearly see that ambitwistor string is on shell equivalent to the null strings.

E.2
ILST equivalent formalism for ambitwistor strings

Using partial gauge fixing, where
\rho=1
with unspecified
\lambda
45
45

45

It is important to note that in the phase space action fixing
\lambda=0
works perfectly, however, in the form (
561
), singularity arises.
, we can rewrite the action (
561
) as

\displaystyle\int d^{2}\sigma\frac{1}{\lambda}(\partial_{-}X)^{2}.

(568)

This action is basically ILST action (
137
) with
V^{\alpha}
given by

\displaystyle V^{\alpha}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}}(1,-1).

(569)

Comparing with the gauge fixed action of the null strings (i.e. (
137
) along with (
139
)) one can clearly see that the
\sigma^{-}
coordinate for (
568
) is equivalent to the coordinate
\tau
in (
137
), while the
\sigma^{+}
coordinate is equivalent to
\sigma
. Consequently the classical analysis for the action (
568
) is much the same as section
5
. This action (
568
) has a the following residual gauge symmetry on the worldsheet

\displaystyle\sigma^{+}\to\sigma^{+}+\sigma^{-}f^{\prime}(\sigma^{+})+g(\sigma^{+}),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \sigma^{-}\to\sigma^{-}+f(\sigma^{+}).

(570)

Consequently the gauge symmetry algebra will be

\displaystyle L_{n}=ie^{in\sigma^{+}}(\partial_{+}+in\sigma^{-}\partial_{-}),\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ M_{n}=ie^{in\sigma^{+}}\partial_{-}.

(571)

This
L_{n}
s and
M_{n}
s satisfy the classical part of the BMS
3
algebra in (
149
)

[L_{m},L_{n}]=(m-n)L_{m+n},\penalty\ \penalty\ [L_{m},M_{n}]=(m-n)M_{m+n},\penalty\ \penalty\ [M_{m},M_{n}]=0.

(572)

The EOM are given by

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{-}X^{\mu}=0.

(573)

Using the fact that
\Pi=\frac{1}{\lambda}\partial_{-}X
for (
568
) he constraints the theory is subjected to as given in (
563
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle(\partial_{-}X)^{2}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \partial_{-}X\cdot\partial_{\sigma}X=0.

(574)

Solution to the EOM (
573
) with closed string periodicity
\sigma^{\pm}\sim\sigma^{\pm}+2\pi
gives the following
46
46

46

This mode expansion has the same form as (
150
), where the summation includes the
n=0
modes and {
\sigma,\tau
} are replaces by {
\sigma^{+},\sigma^{-}
}.

X^{\mu}(\sigma^{\pm})=\sum_{n}\frac{1}{n}(A^{\mu}_{n}-in\sigma^{-}B^{\mu}_{n})e^{-in\sigma^{+}},

(575)

where we need to make the following identification for the modes in order to ensure periodicity in
\sigma^{\pm}\sim\sigma^{\pm}+2\pi

\displaystyle A_{n}\sim A_{n}+2\pi B_{n}

(576)

Classical constraints from phase space formalism

Let us consider the phase space action in (
564
). If we partially gauge fix the action with
\rho=1
without fixing
\lambda
. For such action the EOM for
X
and
\Pi
are

\displaystyle\partial_{-}X^{\mu}=2\lambda\Pi^{\mu},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \partial_{-}\Pi^{\mu}=0.

(577)

The constraints for this action are given in (
563
). The EOMs are solved by following mode expansions

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \Pi^{\mu}=\sum_{n}B^{\mu}_{n}e^{-in\sigma^{+}},

(578a)

\displaystyle X^{\mu}(\sigma^{\pm})=

\displaystyle\sum_{n}\frac{1}{n}(A^{\mu}_{n}-i2n\lambda\sigma^{-}B^{\mu}_{n})e^{-in\sigma^{+}}.

(578b)

From (
578b
) one can see that in order to confirm the periodicity of
X

\sigma^{\pm}\sim\sigma^{\pm}+2\pi
, setting
\lambda
to zero is essential. The constraints for ambitwistor strings as given in (
566
) take the following form

\displaystyle\Pi^{2}=\sum_{n}M_{n}e^{-in\sigma+},

(579a)

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mu}\partial_{+}X^{\mu}=\sum_{n}(L_{n}-in\lambda\sigma^{-}M_{n})e^{-in\sigma^{+}},

(579b)

where
L_{n}
s and
M_{n}
s are constructed on the
A,B
oscillators as usual

L_{n}=\sum_{m}A_{-m}\cdot B_{m+n},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ M_{n}=\sum_{n}B_{-m}\cdot B_{n+m}.

From (
579b
), we see that
\Pi_{\mu}\partial_{+}X^{\mu}
for
\lambda=0
acts like a holomorphic Virasoro constraint as shown below

\displaystyle\Pi_{\mu}\partial_{+}X^{\mu}|_{\lambda=0}=\sum_{n}L_{n}e^{-in\sigma^{+}}.

(580)

It is important here to recall that ambitwistor string theory was originally formulated as holomorphic CFT on the string worldsheet with an additional constraint
\Pi^{2}=0
. Here, from the classical analysis, we saw that exactly same scenario is arising from null string formulation with
\lambda=0
gauge.

Complex null strings

As highlighted before, the ambitwistor string theory is formulated on a space of complex null geodesics in complexified Minkowski spacetime, where the worldsheet too, is complexified. While the foregoing discussion (following
68
) did successfully connect the classical action of ambitwistor string to ILST action for null strings, both target spacetime and worldsheet has been considered to be real. In
67
, a complex version of ILST strings was formulated in a complexified Minkowski target spacetime which filled this gap. Here the worldsheet is 2d complex manifold with two holomorphic coordinates
z
and
\bar{z}
and vielbein density
V^{\alpha}
too is complexified. Here too, the gauge fixed action display the BMS
3
algebra with the following form of generators

\displaystyle L_{n}=-z^{n+1}\partial_{z}-(n+1)z^{n}\bar{z}\partial_{\bar{z}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ M_{n}=-z^{n+1}\partial_{\bar{z}}.

(581)

This formalism of complex null string makes the connection between null stings and ambitwistor strings much more robust.

E.3
Quantisation

It was known from earlier studies
139
that bosonic ambitwistor string has critical dimension 26, also that the spectrum of this theory is finite with only massless particles. Casali et al in
68
;
67
identified that quantum theory of null strings constructed on flipped vacuum has the identical features and hence, is the correct candidate for ambitwistor strings. Let us recall the definition of flipped vacuum

\displaystyle A_{n}\ket{0}=B_{n}\ket{0}=0,\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \forall n>0.

As discussed in section
11.3
, only level 2 states constructed on this vacuum are physical states and they give massless spin 2 particles like graviton,
B
-field and dilaton. As shown in
67
, the physical states for this theory can be identified as null states in the highest weight representation of BMS
3
algebra with
h_{L}=2
and
h_{M}=0
. The central charges for this particula quantum theory has been found to be
c_{L}=2D
(
D=26
is dimension of target spacetime) and
c_{M}=0

7
. Highest weight representation for BMS
3
algebra has been earlier studied in
25
, where it has been shown that for
c_{M}=0
, the descendant states for BMS
3
highest weight representation reduces to descendants of the Virasoro subalgebra of the BMS
3
algebra. Consequently, we are left with chiral Virasoro module. This confirms that at the quantum level ambitwistor string theory is equivalent to the null string theory constructed on flipped vacuum (since it is known that ambitwistor string theory is chiral CFT).

Appendix F
What is an inconsistent constraint?

We saw in section
9
, only four possibilities out of nine are consistent with BMS
3
algebra resulting in three distinct quantum theories. In this appendix, we demonstrate the origin of inconsistency by examining one of five non-viable constraint choices listed in (
248
). We consider the following constraint condition as an example

L_{n}|phys\rangle=0,\quad\forall\ n>0,\qquad M_{n}|phys\rangle=0,\quad\forall\ n\neq 0.

(582)

We begin by considering a physical vacuum
|0,k^{\mu}\rangle
which satisfies

M_{n}|0,k^{\mu}\rangle=\sum_{m}B_{-m}\cdot B_{m+n}|0,k^{\mu}\rangle=0\qquad\forall\ n\neq 0.

(583)

Using the conditions imposed by
M_{n}
and
L_{n}
, we obtain

\displaystyle B^{\mu}_{n}|0,k^{\mu}\rangle

\displaystyle=0\qquad(n\neq 0)

(584a)

\displaystyle L_{n}|0,k^{\mu}\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{m}A_{-m}\cdot B_{m+n}|0,k^{\mu}\rangle=0\qquad\forall\ n>0

(584b)

\displaystyle=A_{n}\cdot B_{0}|0,k^{\mu}\rangle=0\qquad\qquad\qquad\forall\ n>0.

(584c)

Since the vacuum is eigenstate of
B_{0}
with eigenvalue
k^{\mu}
, this implies

A^{\mu}_{n}|0,k^{\mu}\rangle=0\qquad\qquad\forall\penalty\ n>0.

(585)

Consequently, any excited state must be constructed by acting with negative modes of the oscillators on which in turn implies the excited states can be defined by

A^{\mu_{1}}_{-n_{1}}A^{\mu_{2}}_{-n_{2}}\ldots A^{\mu_{j}}_{-n_{i}}|0,k^{\mu}\rangle,\qquad\forall\ n_{1},n_{2},\ldots,n_{i}>0.

(586)

Hence, the vacuum must satisfy

\displaystyle A^{\mu}_{n}|0,k^{\mu}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(C^{\mu}_{n}-\tilde{C}^{\mu}_{-n})|0,k^{\mu}\rangle=0\quad\forall\ n>0,

(587a)

\displaystyle B^{\mu}_{n}|0,k^{\mu}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(C^{\mu}_{n}+\tilde{C}^{\mu}_{-n})|0,k^{\mu}\rangle=0\quad\forall\ n\neq 0,

(587b)

We can rewrite the above conditions as

\displaystyle C^{\mu}_{n}|0,k^{\mu}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{C}^{\mu}_{-n}|0,k^{\mu}\rangle=0\quad\forall\ n>0,

(588a)

\displaystyle C^{\mu}_{n}|0,k^{\mu}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\tilde{C}^{\mu}_{-n}|0,k^{\mu}\rangle\quad\forall\ n<0.

(588b)

As we shall see later the first condition is the definition of the flipped vacuum. The second relation couples the creation modes of
C
and
\tilde{C}
oscillators. This identification effectively collapses the two independent sets of oscillators into a single one. As a consequence, the realisation of the algebra is destroyed, since the decoupling of the modes is crucial for the closure of the BMS Algebra. This completes our brief examination on the inconsistent constraints.

Appendix G
Central charges of three quantum theories

The central charges arise due to quantum anomalies and normal ordering ambiguities. Their values can be computed using the Jacobi identities, which relate them to the spacetime dimension
D
. In this appendix, we determine the central charges for each of the three quantum null string theories
7
.

We recall the classical BMS
3
algebra (
149
) given by

[L_{m},L_{n}]=(m-n)L_{m+n},\qquad[L_{m},M_{n}]=(m-n)M_{m+n},\qquad[M_{m},M_{n}]=0.

(589)

We now turn to compute the quantum correction to the above algebra. At the quantum level, for
m+n=0
on the RHS, we encounter normal ordering ambiguity which involve a
c
-number given by

[L_{n},L_{-n}]=2nL_{0}+A_{L}(n),\qquad[L_{n},M_{-n}]=2nM_{0}+A_{M}(n).

(590)

This is the centrally extended BMS
3
algebra where
A_{L}(n)
and
A_{M}(n)
are called the normal ordering ambiguities. From (
590
), it can be easily seen that
A_{L}(-n)=-A_{L}(n)
and similarly for
A_{M}
. From the Jacobi identities, one can obtain a recursion relation to find all
A(n)
,

A_{L}(n)=\frac{n(n^{2}-1)}{6}A_{L}(2)-f(n)A_{L}(1),

(591)

where
A_{L}(1)
and
A_{L}(2)
are unknown constants. This leads to a general form of
A_{L}(n)
,

A_{L}(n)=\alpha n^{3}+\beta n.

(592)

Similarly for
A_{M}(n)
. In what follows, we compute the anomaly for each of the three quantum theories. For this, we need to determine
\alpha
and
\beta
which can be evaluated by computing expectation values of commutator
[L_{n},L_{-n}]
in vacuum
\ket{0,0^{\mu}}
with zero momentum.

\begin{split}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|[L_{1},L_{-1}]|0,0^{\mu}\rangle=2\langle 0,0^{\mu}|L_{0}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle+A_{L}(1)\\
\langle 0,0^{\mu}|[L_{2},L_{-2}]|0,0^{\mu}\rangle=4\langle 0,0^{\mu}|L_{0}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle+A_{L}(2)\end{split}

(593)

Similarly for
A_{M}(n)
,

\begin{split}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|[L_{1},M_{-1}]|0,0^{\mu}\rangle=2\langle 0,0^{\mu}|M_{0}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle+A_{M}(1)\\
\langle 0,0^{\mu}|[L_{2},M_{-2}]|0,0^{\mu}\rangle=4\langle 0,0^{\mu}|M_{0}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle+A_{M}(2)\end{split}

(594)

We now turn to find the central charge for Oscillator, Induced and Flipped vacuum.

Oscillator vacuum:

We begin with the oscillator vacuum
|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{c}
. From (
249c
) we have,

{}_{c}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|L_{0}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{c}={}_{c}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|M_{0}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{c}=0.

(595)

We now compute the following expectation value of the commutators

\displaystyle{}_{c}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|[L_{n},L_{-n}]|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{c}

\displaystyle={}_{c}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|L_{n}L_{-n}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{c}-{}_{c}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|L_{-n}L_{n}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{c}

(596a)

\displaystyle=A_{L}(n)=0.

(596b)

\displaystyle{}_{c}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|[L_{1},M_{-1}]|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{c}

\displaystyle={}_{c}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|L_{1}M_{-1}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{c}-{}_{c}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|M_{-1}L_{1}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{c}

(596c)

\displaystyle=A_{M}(1)=2{}_{c}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|c^{\prime}k^{2}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{c}=0.

(596d)

\displaystyle{}_{c}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|[L_{2},M_{-2}]

\displaystyle|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{c}={}_{c}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|L_{2}M_{-2}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{c}-{}_{c}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|M_{-2}L_{2}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{c}

(596e)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}{}_{c}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|C_{1}\cdot C_{1}C_{-1}\cdot C_{-1}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{c}+\frac{1}{4}{}_{c}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|\tilde{C}_{1}\cdot\tilde{C}_{1}\tilde{C}_{-1}\cdot\tilde{C}_{-1}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{c}

(596f)

\displaystyle=A_{M}(2)=D.

(596g)

Putting the values of
A_{L}(n),A_{M}(1)
and
A_{M}(2)
in (
590
), we obtain the following centrally extended algebra

\begin{split}[L_{p},L_{q}]&=(p-q)L_{p+q},\\
[L_{p},M_{q}]&=(p-q)M_{p+q}+\frac{D}{6}p(p^{2}-1)\delta_{p+q},\\
[M_{p},M_{q}]&=0.\end{split}

(597)

with central charge
c_{L}=0
and
c_{M}=2D
.

Induced vacuum:

Here we consider the Induced vacuum
|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{I}
. The action of
L_{n}
and
M_{n}
on the vacuum can be read from (
340
) and (
346
) as

{}_{I}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|L_{n}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{I}=c^{\prime}k\cdot{}_{I}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|A_{n}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{I}=0=M_{n}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{I}\qquad\forall\ n.

(598)

The expectation value of the commutator yields

\begin{split}{}_{I}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|[L_{n},L_{-n}]|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{I}&=A_{L}(n)=0,\\
{}_{I}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|[L_{n},M_{-n}]|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{I}&=A_{M}(n)=0.\end{split}

(599)

From the expressions of
A_{L}(n)
and
A_{M}(n)
derived above, we obtain
c_{L}=c_{M}=0
.

Flipped vacuum:

We recall the action of
L_{n}
and
M_{n}
on the flipped vacuum
|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{A}
from (
348
) as given below,

L_{n}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{A}=0,\qquad M_{n}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{A}=0\qquad\forall\ n\geq 0.

(600)

We now proceed to compute the expectation value of commutators to find central charge.

\displaystyle{}_{A}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|[L_{2},L_{-2}]|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{A}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}{}_{A}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|C_{1}\cdot C_{1}C_{-1}\cdot C_{-1}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{A}

(601a)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{4}{}_{A}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\cdot\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{-1}\cdot\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{-1}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{A}=A_{L}(2)=D,

(601b)

\displaystyle{}_{A}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|[L_{2},M_{-2}]|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{A}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}{}_{A}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|C_{1}\cdot C_{1}C_{-1}\cdot C_{-1}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{A}

(601c)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{4}{}_{A}\langle 0,0^{\mu}|\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\cdot\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{1}\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{-1}\cdot\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{-1}|0,0^{\mu}\rangle_{A}=A_{M}(2)=0.

(601d)

Putting the above values of
A_{L}(2)
and
A_{M}(2)
in (
590
), we obtain the following centrally extended algebra as

\begin{split}[L_{p},L_{q}]&=(p-q)L_{p+q}+\frac{D}{6}p(p^{2}-1)\delta_{p+q,0},\\
[L_{p},M_{q}]&=(p-q)M_{p+q},\\
[M_{p},M_{q}]&=0.\end{split}

(602)

with central charge
c_{L}=2A_{L}(2)=2D
and
c_{M}=2A_{M}(2)=0
. With this we conclude the discussion of central charges for all the three quantum theories.
